This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Indonesia REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016";

b) Notification letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Socio-economic impact assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

g) Gender documentation.

# Funding Proposal

## REDD-plus results based payments

### Version 1.0

Accredited entities are expected to develop a funding proposal in close consultation with the relevant national designated authority and REDD-plus entity/focal point, in response to the request for proposals for the Pilot Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (Decision B.18). The funding proposal should follow the terms of reference of that Board decision and be assessed per Stage 2 (Sections 2 – 5) of the scorecard annexed to the same Board decision.

**Results period in this proposal:** 2014 - 2016

**National Designated Authority:** Fiscal Policy Agency, Ministry of Finance

**REDD-plus entity/focal point:** Directorate General for Climate Change, Ministry of Environment and Forestry

# REDD+ RBP Funding Proposal

## B. Carbon Accounts

### B.1 Forest Reference Emission Level / Forest Reference Level (FREL/FRL)

Please provide a link to the FREL/FRL submission: https://med.unfccc.int/efr/submission-by-indonesia_final.pdf

Please provide a link to the UNFCCC Technical Assessment Report: https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/resource/technical-assessment-report-for-the-proposal-to-establish-a-reference-level-and-reference-emission-level-for-indonesia-2019-2038.pdf

Indonesia's FREL reference period exceeds the maximum of 20 years, as indicated in the GCF REDD+ RBP scorecard (24xRBP). Following the guidance contained in footnote 22 of Box 20c) of the scenario, Indonesia requested that the TA be conducted for a 15-year period. The FREL/FRL submission is based on this 15-year period of this funding proposal and does not impact the UNFCCC RBP or results having already been submitted or reported to the GCF. This submission will be considered by the GCF Board in its consideration of future submissions to the UNFCCC by Indonesia. The 20-year reference period of 1993 – 2012 was selected by Indonesia for the purposes of this funding proposal, but is not reflected in the FREL/FRL submission.

To meet the retention of the GCF RBP pilot phase, which does not allow for propositions from non-HFLD countries, an annual average was generated and applied to the shorter period for all activities and carbon pools. The 15-year average was calculated by dividing the total average emissions by the number of years in its TORs. It is Indonesia's view that this approach does well represent the very nature of Indonesian forests.

In its interest: Indonesia has included soil organic carbon emissions from post deforestation in the UNFCCC RBP, but not in the GCF RBP. This is because the inclusion of these emissions was justified by the high level of uncertainty associated with these emissions. Indonesia would like to highlight that the scorecard does not consider this very important element of its own national circumstances.

More details can be found in Section B.12 of this funding proposal.


#### B.1.2 Technical Assessment and Analysis Process

(Consistency) The TA provides information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the consistency of the FREL with the GHG inventory. It also describes how the TA has addressed any inconsistencies between the GCF RBP and the UNFCCC RBP, and describe how they will be resolved in the next reporting period.

In 2015, Indonesia, on a voluntary basis, proposed a national FREL based on average historical emissions from 1990 to 2012 and covering the activities reducing emissions from deforestation and reducing emissions from forest degradation (REDD+). The proposal was submitted in accordance with the guidance provided by the Climate Change (UNFCCC) Secretariat, and successfully went through a technical assessment by UNFCCC experts. This resulted in Indonesia's FREL being approved as 103.5 million metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2e), which is equal to 89.7 Mt CO2e per year or 113.2 million hectares in 2015, comprising up to 60 per cent of the national territory and 78 per cent of the country's forest area.

The proposed FREL was based on historical emissions from 1990-2012, which were estimated at 568,859.8 Mt CO2e for this period. This means annually increasing amounts of emissions from post deforestation and forest degradation (PDD) activities in Indonesia are included in the FREL. The TA provides a detailed explanation on how these PDD emissions have been accounted for in the FREL.

The GCF RBP scorecard has established overall consistency of data sources between the FREL and the national greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory, which is based on the same methodology as the UNFCCC RBP. The TA provides a detailed explanation of how these data sources are consistent with each other.

The emission factors are derived from the national forest inventory (1990-2013). Key activity data and emission factors were also provided by Indonesia in 2014. Both the FREL and the GHG inventory rely on the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and the Wetlands Supplement.

The AT did note that Indonesia's FREL applied a different forest definition than that used in the national GHG inventory, which is based on the UNFCCC RBP. The TA provides an explanation of how this difference contained in the FREL, the GHG inventory includes information about plantation forests. In the modified FREL submission, Indonesia has addressed these concerns and has aligned the emissions with the AT; during the TA, the definition of forest is not being changed in the FREL but rather excluding the plantations.

In the Report on the technical assessment of the proposed forest reference emission level of Indonesia submitted to the GCF Board in 2015, it was noted that the national GHG inventory and the UNFCCC RBP established overall consistency of data sources between the FREL and the national greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory. The TA provides a detailed explanation on how these data sources are consistent with each other.

The emission factors are derived from the national forest inventory (1990-2013). Key activity data and emission factors were also provided by Indonesia in 2014. Both the FREL and the GHG inventory rely on the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and the Wetlands Supplement.

Given the framework of REDD+, and the focus on natural forests, Indonesia explained that what is applied can be considered a "working definition" that also considers the cost of practicality and cost-effectiveness associated with forest monitoring. The submission notes that the data used in the calculation of the TAR for including this additional explanation of the forest deforestation in the modified FREL submission.

#### (a) Data Source of the FREL

Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the data used for the construction of the FREL. The activity data used for the construction of the FREL are land-cover data from the National Forest Inventory System (NFIS). These data can be accessed at the website: <https://nfisda.menlhk.ristekbkn.go.id/>. The official data describe land cover and forest classes, with a resolution of 10 meters. Data for the period 2003–2015 were collected by visual interpretation from Landsat imagery. Data for the period 1996 to 2002 were collected using aerial photography. Data for the period 1990s were added to the NFIS (from freely available Landsat satellite data archives). The ongoing effort of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) in developing a national forest inventory system, in a system that has become a success story that credits monitoring deforestation over years and becomes one of the most important tools for forest management. This is supported by the National Forest Management System (NFMS), which is specific for the land-based sector and is supported by the NFMS has also been developed since 2015, as part of the implementation of the REDD+ Action Plan. The data used in the construction of the FREL are based on Regulation No. 75/2017 on "REDD+" procedure and No. 72/2017 on MRV system. In addition, the Ministerial Regulation No. 73/2018 on the National Forest Inventory System (NFIS) was also used.

#### For Its FREL Submission

Indonesia submitted use data for years: 1996, 2000, 2005, 2008, 2010, 2011 and 2012 to capture historical land-cover data. The well-worn land-cover maps that serve as activity data were produced by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) using aerial photography and satellite imagery digitized manually via visual interpretation. The platted spatial data used in the construction of the FREL were provided by the Ministry of Agriculture, and were based on several years' image, field, and forest inventory checks.

#### Deforestation and Degradation Data

In each step of each time period, a set of maps was generated using permanent land cover maps. In the second step, areas of change were determined from overlaying these maps with others. During the technical assessment process, it is recommended that Indonesia should consider the use of remote sensing techniques as an alternative to visual interpretation for the production of forest inventory data and is seen as best practice in remote sensing analysis. Indonesia acknowledged that such an approach is often used by other countries but noted that it would be difficult to obtain sufficient spatial resolution information of the whole country and for the long timeframe in question, which would be challenging and would require a significant amount of time and resources. The submission also notes that this could affect forest mapping. The TAR acknowledged this explanation and commended Indonesia for these efforts, agreeing that more research is needed on how to improve the quality of forest inventory data using remote sensing techniques.

The TAR also commented Indonesia for its efforts to compile land cover databases using automated classification techniques across a long time series (at least 10 years) with the support of existing NFMS.

#### The Previous Data Source

The previous data source used to derive emission factors for deforestation and forest degradation was the national forest inventory (NFIS). This provided sufficient forest reference points to fill information gaps for forest types (e.g., mangrove forests) for which there were not a sufficient number of NFRI plots. As part of the Indonesian submission, it is noted that the data used in the calculation of the TAR for including this additional explanation of the forest deforestation in the FREL was based on the use of permanent land cover maps. These maps were utilized to generate a national database on aboveground biomass and thus emission factors, which were then applied to calculate the emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. The submission notes that cluster clustering of a permanent sample plot of 1 ha is surrounded by eight temporary sample plots. Emission factors for each land cover type are calculated based on the average biomass of all nine plots. These emission factors for the NFIS were mostly derived using data collected in the field, but some were estimated from existing assumptions and information used in the FREL. In the submission, Indonesia reports that 450 permanent sample plots have been established to date, with a further 120 planned over the next two years. Despite the large number of plots, the TAR noted that there are still gaps for individual strata.

Indonesia is constantly working to improve its data and information on forests. Indonesia's submission highlights ongoing efforts in this regard. The submission notes that the Ministry of Environment and Forestry agrees and agrees that improving the set of emission factors is an area for improvement. In addition, specific areas, namely PAA (Performance Assessment Area), or NFRI in Indonesia where REDD+ activities will be more important and verified have been identified. The TAR was generated based on the natural forest extent in the


---

end of 1972 and distribution of forested peat land in 1980. An adjusted version of the linear regression equation to estimate emissions from peat decomposition was deemed necessary. A new equation using a non-linear model was developed based on data collected by the Indonesian Forest Research Institute (LIPI) for peat decomposition, which Indonesia recognized as needing improvement and which the LULUCF experts had argued should be considered in the context of the 2nd FREL proposal. Indonesia intends to submit an national FREL to the UNFCCC in 2020 (see §9, item 13, for more details).

To maintain consistency with the RFP criteria, which do not allow for projected emissions from non-HFL countries, an annual average was generated and applied to the recalculated FREL for all activities and carbon pools. Indonesia would like to highlight that this approach does not fully reflect the reality of forest land use change in Indonesia, but it is necessary to ensure alignment with its TFRS. It is Indonesia's view that this approach does not rightly represent the very nature of forest land use change in Indonesia, which is a result of conversion from agricultural land into forests and vice versa.

Indonesia has included emissions from soil organic carbon in peatlands in the UNFCCC FREL in the spirit of the RFP criteria. However, it should be noted that this approach does not fully reflect the reality associated with these emissions. Indonesia would like to highlight that the scorecard does not consider this very important element of its own national circumstances.

(b) If a country is considered HFLD: Please provide the justification for this classification.
Indonesia is not considered an HFLD; so this criterion is not applicable.

(c) FRELFRL adjustments for a HFLD country. If adjustments are made, please provide information that the adjustment does not exceed ±1% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period in the relevant area and/or explain why it exceeds this limit. Indonesia would like to highlight that the scorecard does not consider the reference period that likely underestimates future rates of deforestation or forest degradation during the eligibility period.

The ToR for the pilot process does not allow for projections from non-HFL countries. (ii) FRELFRL: In accordance with 12CP-17. Please provide any information that demonstrates the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the quantified estimate of the FREL for all activities and carbon pools as well as the reference period, specifically on the modellles for FRELFRL and whether the raised issues were material or not mentioned in the TAR.

In the TAR, the AT noted that the data and information used by Indonesia in constructing its FREL are transparent, complete, and in overall accordance with the guidelines contained in the annex to decision 12CP-17. The AT also noted that Indonesia has applied the IPCC Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (hereinafter referred to as the "IPCC GPG") and the Revised Guidelines for National Forest Inventories (hereinafter referred to as the 2006 IPCC Guidelines), as applied to the construction of the FREL. The AT also noted that Indonesia has used the Wetlands Supplement to the Revised Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories: Wetlands (hereinafter referred to as the Wetlands Supplement) was used.

In its submission, Indonesia stated that it had applied a stepwise approach to the development of the FREL, in accordance with decision 12CP-17, paragraph 10, with the aim of improving the FREL by incorporating better data and information. The AT noted that this approach has resulted in improvements in the quality of technical information, such as refining activity data and emission factors, estimating peatland fire emissions and establishing a reference period for forest land use change. Indonesia intends to continue this process starting with the re-submission of a national FREL at the end of 2020. This new FREL is expected to include the following improvements: (i) improved activity data; (ii) improved emission factors, especially those related to forest emission factors (carbon stock) by adding data from recent research and plot measurements (especially for mangrove forests); (iii) improved peat fire emissions; (iv) inclusion of an additional REDD+ activity (enhancement of forest carbon stocks).

Quality assurance and quality control (QA/QC) procedures for the FREL and data and calculations were carried out.

In the case of quality control, this was conducted by the relevant data custodian. For land cover data, this was the responsible authority in Indonesia, the Ministry of Forestry and Environmental Planning, while for peat decomposition, this is handled by the Indonesian Center for Peatland Management (BKSDA). In addition to the QA/QC procedures carried out within the FREL, independent expert assessments were carried out for both the activity data as well as the emission factors considered in the calculation of the REDD-plus RFB. These assessments are conducted by the Forest Research and Development Agency (FORDA) as well as from academic institutions such as Bogor Agriculture University.

These calculations were carried out with the aim to align the data and information presented with Indonesia by the guidelines contained within the relevant CJP document including transparency, accuracy, completeness and consistency of data.

uncertainties associated with these emissions, Indonesia would like to highlight that the GCF pilot programmatic score does not consider the fact that the emissions from soil carbon in peatland accumulate over the years and by definition are not a one-time event, due to the inherent emissions, which is a very important element of Indonesia's national circumstances.

One area of further improvement identified by the AT was the assessment of whether post-convension removals are significant enough to be considered as an alternative to forest cover loss. In order to improve its data in a stepwise manner, Indonesia is now compiling emissions-free non-forest land cover change data for the period 2015–2016 and will continue to do so on an annual basis. (vi) FREL/FREEL consistency: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the FREEL submission. The AT noted that Indonesia has provided a detailed explanation on how it uses the time series used for the construction of the FREEL/FRL and whether significant issues were raised in the process. The AT also noted that Indonesia has provided an explanation regarding the consistency of the FREL/FREEL raised in TA Report that could not be resolved due to time and data constraints.

The activity data for deforestation and forest degradation used for the construction of Indonesia's FREEL, generated from the available national forest monitoring system, are methodologically consistent over time: the FREEL estimates are based on the same methodology as the FREL estimates. The data were also consistent since the establishment of the system in 2000, and then also applied backwards in time to the 1990s.

The AT acknowledged the immense effort that went into building the time series of land-cover maps but noted that maintaining this level of detail over a long period is challenging. The AT also highlighted that this could be a potential source of the observed discontinuity. In response, Indonesia explained that it is exploring ways to develop an integrated system for continuous monitoring and management of forest resources in order to improve the land-cover data. In line with this, the view of the AT, improving quality management is an area for improvement. The AT also noted that Indonesia has already implemented a number of automated interpretation approaches across long time series of satellite images and to improve the existing NFCS, Indonesia is currently developing a new system which will be based on Landsat imagery. Indonesia continuously improves its technical approach. Indonesia is already applying an integrated system, combining automated interpretation with field validation, and is also exploring the use of drones for the collection of detailed information for national purposes. Indonesia would not completely migrate the system from visual to automatic interpretation.

A set of satellite images was used to generate land cover maps. In a second step, changed areas were determined by overlaying these maps with others. As noted above in Section ii.a), the AT pointed out that direct comparison between two time series is challenging and may lead to false positive results. Indonesia acknowledged that such an approach is often considered to generate better results, but explained the challenges of doing so for Indonesia's case due to the large number of images involved. The AT also noted that the long timeframe in question would be highly challenging and would require extensive ground-truthing. As expected by the AT, Indonesia has not yet developed a system which can provide continuous monitoring at the level of detail required.

Activity data were developed using a combination of visual interpretation of land cover and land cover change maps for each time period, compared to earlier FREL initiative. The Ministry of Forestry data sets are based on Landsat imagery with a spatial resolution of 30 m. Indonesia has also conducted field checking and comparing the consistency with other available data, e.g., forest and non-forest data from LAPAN (2015), Indonesian Forest Change Analysis (IFCA) (2016), and the Global Forest Change Analysis (GFC) to other similar products that have been published in peer-reviewed international journals (e.g. Hansen et al., 2013).

The updated emission factors better reflect the diversity of forest types and conditions, compared to earlier FREL estimates. Furthermore, the data used in this submission have been thoroughly scrutinized in terms

### (vi) FREL/RFL accuracy: Further provides any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the accuracy of the FREL/RFL, and whether significant improvements have been made since the last assessment. The RFL is based on a combination of methodologies used rather over/under-estimate emissions and/or removals during the reference period if applicable. ### Indonesia has included in its RFL submission, the most significant activities and carbon pools, also considering the extent to which the data that was available and was considered accurate. By using current land cover data derived from Landsat imagery (2011), it is possible to assess the level of forest degradation and deforestation with a high degree of confidence. However, it is still problematic to monitor different degrees of forest degradation with the same level of confidence because of the lack of information on the extent of tree mortality and the time since the last assessment. The level of forest degradation could be better quantified.

Emissions from deforestation and forest degradation primarily originate from the aboveground biomass (AGB). At present, there is no national inventory for AGB in Indonesia, which allows more accurate calculations of carbon emissions using Tier 2 or Tier 3 AGB data are widely available in Indonesia and considered to be accurate. The level of uncertainty associated with these estimates was derived by applying atomic equations. Uncertainties from activity data of forest degradation and deforestation were derived from the overall accuracy of the land cover map (RFL) and the reference period (2011). The assessment was conducted for all 23 classes of the 2011 land cover map and the results found an overall accuracy of 95% of the AGB data, with a high level of uncertainty in forest emissions could be justified from its exclusion from the FREL. Indonesia is now working on improving the activity data by using more accurate and transparent information. The average uncertainty for emissions was 16%.

* **Activity data:** to improve accuracy and consistency in satellite image interpretation, time series satellite imagery (e.g., Landsat) can be used (rather than analyzing satellite images individually), remote sensing techniques could be improved by overlaying maps to detect changes and automated processing image techniques could be employed.
* **New system for new activity assessment of land cover mapping from remote sensing data:** a new system for accuracy assessment of land cover mapping from remote sensing data is being developed. This will allow the use of more accurate and transparent information in activities (e.g., forest inventory, land cover classification, and land cover change detection using satellite scans). Enhancing remote sensing technology to improve both scope and depth of burn estimates, as well as improving the accuracy of biomass estimation.
* **Plantation data:** to refine and to redefine the set of emission factors. Indonesia is now working on focusing data of carbon stocks and emissions factor from HFL pools specifically for mangrove forests. Meanwhile, improved methodologies are being developed to improve the quality of information in accurate and effective ways.

Improvements made by Indonesia have made progress in improving the prevalent activity data, by developing detailed, higher-resolution maps of peatland areas, which is being piloted at the district

### (iv) Sources of emissions: Emissions primarily from deforestation and forest degradation which represent the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to whether activities listed in paragraph 70 of UNFCCC Decision 1/CP.13 are significant sources of emissions. The AT acknowledged that there were no studies that justified whether activities that are significant sources of emissions were not included due to lack of data. However, it is important to note that the Technical Assessment Report does not include all REDD+ activities that are significant sources of emissions in future FREL/FIREL submissions.

The AT acknowledged that Indonesia included in its FREL emissions from both deforestation and forest degradation, which are the two most significant REDD-plus activities in Indonesia.


### (ix) Significant posts: Please refer to the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of the most significant posts. If applicable, justify why other posts were not included as significant sources of emissions or underestimate emissions. In addition, provide a plan to include all significant posts in future FREL submissions.

The carbon pools included in Indonesia's FREL were aboveground biomass for all sites and soil organic carbon emissions from peatland degradation associated with deforestation and forest degradation on peatlands. Belowground biomass was not considered as the necessary data were unavailable.


### Indonesia included in its FREL submission the most significant carbon pools, consistent to the extent which available data were considered accurate. All other important carbon pool for LULUCF emissions such as soil organic carbon are dominant pools compared to the others (i.e., belowground biomass, deadwood, litter and mineral carbon). The inclusion of these other carbon pools in Indonesia's FREL submission is limited. Review on the proportion of carbon pools showed that the biomass proportion of understorey vegetation and peatland degradation was higher than the soil organic carbon pool.


Without neglecting the importance of belowground biomass, some underlying reasons presented by Indonesia on its FREL submission to focus only on aboveground biomass carbon pools were:

1. Emissions from deforestation and forest degradation are primarily originate from AGB post. AGF accounts for 90% of total emissions in Indonesia. The AGB is the most studied carbon pool across forest ecosystem types in Indonesia, which allows more accurate calculation of carbon emissions. In addition, there have been several studies that have used this approach to estimate emissions and can be estimated from forest inventory or sample plot data using allometric equations. These methods are widely available (e.g., Trabucco et al., 1995; Kettner et al., 2001; Chave et al., 2004; Basuki et al., 2006).

2. Indonesia has a nearly complete estimation of AGB values at the national level, managed by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) through the National Forest Inventory (NFI) Data Field System that covers the entire forests across Indonesia.

3. The Forest Research and Development Agency (FORIDA) within the Ministry of Environment and Forestry in collaboration with the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) has established an on-site

### Carbon Monitoring System in 13 Provinces (http://osaka.jrc.ec.europa.eu/)

The system estimates AGB based on permanent sample plots established at various vegetation types.

Based on the preliminary data from the Indomalaya ecoregion, the AT found that deadwood and belowground biomass carbon, deadwood and soil organic carbon can be an area for technical improvement. The extraction of deadwood and soil organic carbon is currently not considered to have a significant impact or significance. Belowground biomass is being considered for inclusion in the resumption of the national FREL.

The inclusion of soil organic carbon emissions on deforested or degraded peat is a very important effort made by Indonesia, as it is a significant carbon pool. This demonstrates Indonesia's intention to make its information more comprehensive and accurate. However, this area still requires improvement. Due to the complex processes in peatland ecosystems, their mutual relationship with land cover change, and limited data availability, the FREL submission is limited to the inclusion of emissions from past decomposition.

In its submission, Indonesia applies a reverse approach to the development of the FREL in accordance with decision 13/CP.2015, paragraph 7(b), and decision 12/CMP.9, paragraph 10, on implementing a strengthened REDD-plus system. The submission lists a number of areas for technical improvement including data gaps and emission factors, estimating peatland emissions and including additional REDD-plus activities.

#### Emissions from gases

Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of all gases that are significant sources of greenhouse gas emissions. The submission should also indicate whether estimates of emissions were not included due to lack of data and/or whether the emission overstimates emissions or underestimates emissions.

Indonesia's FREL includes only CO2 emissions and does not cover emissions of other GHGs as CO2 constitutes more than 99.9 per cent of total GHG emissions from LULUCF. The AT, as opposed to its claim in the TAR that CO2 is the only significant greenhouse gas emission source for Indonesia's FREL, found that the information provided on its ongoing work in the development of the FREL and improving the accuracy and comparability of national estimates of emissions and removals of all GHGs is not sufficient. The AT noted that decision 13/CP.2015, paragraph 7(b), and decision 12/CMP.9, paragraph 10, on activities undertaken, paragraph 7(b) and decision 12/CMP.9, paragraph 10, on implementing a strengthened REDD-plus system.

The AT also noted that, according to Indonesia's First BUR as well as its second national communication, fires occur frequently in the country and these could result in large amounts of non-CO2 emissions, on peatland fires, which are not included in the FREL. The AT found that Indonesia's FREL does not include information on peatland fires. Indonesia's FREL explained that peatland fires have not been included in the FREL owing to the complexity and high uncertainty of estimating emissions from peatland fires (e.g., fire intensity, duration, area burned, hotspot detection size, type of burned area estimation, fire frequency, burned peat depth, and mass of fuel available for combustion). The AT found that Indonesia's FREL does not include information on peatland fires in the future. As shared with the LULUCF experts during the TA, Indonesia has already been making progress to improve its capacity to estimate emissions from peatland fires.

Advancing remote sensing technology to improve burned area and speed of burn estimation will increase the accuracy of estimates for non-CO2 emissions from peatland fires in the future. The AT found that improvement in a future FREL, Progress has already been made to develop a methodology applying manual analysis of MODIS data, for estimating emissions from peatland fires.

#### IPCC guidance for FRELs

Historical data for weather and the construction of the FREL (LULUCF) were guided by 2003 GPGs or 2010 GPGs.

(xii) issues related to applying IPCC guidance: Please mention any significant issues related to the application of IPCC GLA/GPAs as raised in the TA report. Include any significant issues that are material to the application of IPCC guidance and that could not be resolved due to time or data restrictions.

There was only a single, specific issue listed regarding the application of IPCC guidance; however, it is not clear what this issue was.

Indonesia's FREL includes soil organic carbon emissions from decomposition associated with deforestation and forest degradation. This is in contrast to the Indonesian national context that does not include these emissions as they are considered part of the land use sector. Therefore, therefore, considers the treatment of all secondary forests as drained forests, a justifiable approach when applying IPCC guidance. The FREL also includes the carbon emissions from peatlands, which means it is possible to trace back the negligible small areas of outlanded secondary forest.

Indonesia plans, however, as noted in its FREL submission, to improve the data on peatlands, chiefly regarding emission factors and land use change. Indonesia has already started collecting data for this purpose and agrees that collecting more detailed data on the management of peatlands is an area for improvement.


B.1.2 Additional reference periods related to FRELR

(xii) Reference period for the FREL/FRL: Please indicate the reference period (number of years) applied for the construction of the FRELR/FRL.

The reference period applied for the combination of the UNFCCC technically assessed FREL was 1980-2012. However, Indonesia has indicated that this reference period exceeds the maximum of 20 years as indicated in the GCF REDD+ RFP scenario; in 2xRCP4.5. The scenario is a required assessment as part of the terms of reference for the FREL/FRL and is not indicative of the actual project design. Indonesia has indicated that it will report on the basis of the UNFCCC technically assessed period, which is 1980-2012. This is exclusively for the purpose of the GCF REDD+ RFP scenario; in 2xRCP4.5. The reference period applied to the FREL/FRL baseline was reported in the BUR technical annex, and technically assessed or analyzed, respectively. It also does not pre-serve for the actual project design. Indonesia has indicated that it will report on this reference base in the future. Details on the reclassification of the 20-year FREL baseline are presented in Annex XVII.

Indonesia selected the 20-year FREL baseline because it is as consistent as possible with the UNFCCC period, while still meeting the requirements of the GCF REDD+ RFP scenario. The project proposal of a length that was as close as possible to the length of the UNFCCC technically assessed FREL period of 1990-2012.

The UNFCCC technically assessed FREL reference period was selected, based on a thorough analysis which considered the following criteria:

(1) availability of land-cover data that is transparent, accurate, complete and consistent;

(2) the length of time series available for analysis; and

(3) the length of time that could reflect the national circumstances, policy dynamics and impacts (biophysical and socio-economic).

As described in Indonesia's FREL submission, five different scenarios for the FREL period were considered, considering the trends, availability and reliability of historical data. The 1990-2012 period was selected as it best reflected the national circumstances of the land use sector in Indonesia, including the policy dynamics and social aspects.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 1 OF 8)

As detailed in the FREL submission, biomass establishment on intensive forest plantation development in 1950s-2000s was a major driver of deforestation, especially important during this time for the wood-pulp and paper industry; such as wood-fiber industry and forest estates (plantations). This time period also covers the economic boom years of Indonesia's timber sector. The rapid expansion of plantations coincided with illegal logging activities and forest encroachments on the ground. Additionally, there was rapid expansion of palm oil plantations in the 1980s-2000s, which led to deforestation and land degradation. In addition, several major fire disasters occurred in Indonesia due to a prolonged El Niño. Following these years, in the period 2000-2003, the deforestation rate was reduced by about half compared with the previous decade.

Forestry aimed to reduce Annual Allowable Cut for timber extraction. In addition, during that period, the government set policies to encourage efforts for forest rehabilitation.

### (xii) If previous reference level submitted: Please indicate whether a previous reference level applying to the land cover map used in this submission is different from either of the two reference levels applied to the previous one and the current one. Describe any adjustments made to the current FREL/FRL, compared to the previous reference level.

No previous REDD+ reference level had been submitted by Indonesia to the UNFCCC, as can be seen on the REDD+ Web Platform here: https://redd.unfccc.int/missions/Indonesia?countryId=4

### (xiii) Uncertainties: Please indicate whether the country has provided information on aggregated uncertainties of the emissions or removals estimate, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances, and if so, indicate how these were calculated. If not, please explain why.

Indonesia conducted a comprehensive uncertainty analysis of its FREL. Uncertainties associated with the activity data (e.g., land cover map) are considered to be the main source of error in the emissions assessment. The land cover mapping was against 181 ground truth points. The land cover assessment was conducted for all classes of the forest/non-forest MfOR, and for each class, a separate uncertainty analysis of the map was done for aggregated classes of forest and non-forest (MfOR, 2012; Margono et al., 2012).

Since the AGB emissions calculation is using Tier 2 accuracy, the uncertainty level for AGB emissions from forest degradation is ±35% (Hirashita et al., 2014). Uncertainty levels of ±60% and ±70% were used for land degradation on peat lands. For peat decomposition, uncertainty of activity data was derived from the overall accuracy of the land cover map (±80%) and the uncertainty level of ±35% for AGB emissions from forest degradation (Hirashita et al., 2014) were adopted as uncertainty values of emission factors. The average uncertainty for all emissions is ±67%.

The AT commented Indonesia for the exemplary effort made in providing the uncertainty analysis. During the TA, several aspects of the uncertainty analysis were discussed and areas for future improvement were identified. These include: (i) further work on the development of a methodology to differentiate sampling errors between the individual forest classes; Additional work to assess the accuracy of change detection using remote sensing data is also recommended. The AT suggested that Indonesia move forward with a new approach for accurate assessment of land-cover mapping from remote sensing data, in order to provide more reliable estimates of emissions.

The AT notes that, in line with the stepwise approach, the uncertainty analysis provided in the FREL submission covered only selected sources of error, and the AT said broadening the scope of the uncertainty analysis to cover forest degradation on peat lands is recommended. The AT also suggested including additional emission factors and activity data, as areas for technical improvement. In response, the uncertainty analysis for the reforestation component was expanded in the current FREL/FRL submission. However, no further improvements have already been undertaken.

A re-design of the current NFI system is being initiated to ensure the sustainability of NFI data measurement, coverage and quality, as well as reduction of uncertainties of the emission factors.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 18 OF 80)

A baseline for results-based payments (RBP) has been established as a benchmark for assessing Indonesia's performance in reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. Emission reductions achieved in emissions from performance indicators are compared to a results-based payment baseline to report results under the REDD-plus mechanism.

This RBP baseline is national in scale, covering all natural forests in Indonesia including dryland, mangrove, and swamp forests in both primary and secondary classes. The baseline is based on the annual historical average of deforestation and forest degradation from 2001-2015. It does not include emissions from land use change or degradation.

The results-based payment baseline distinguishes between the activities covered, or performance indicators. Aboveground biomass is the only pool included in the RBP baseline, and carbon dioxide is the GHG included.

The RBP baseline for the Indonesia-Norway Partnership was developed using a reference period from 2001-2015. The baseline is based on the annual historical average of deforestation and forest degradation in MioC/ha/year. The emission reduction result is the difference between the RBP baseline and the emissions in each year.

From the reported emission reduction results, set-asides will be made to determine the maximum number of emission reductions Indonesia can be rewarded for by Norway and other financiers. These deductions are intended to cover the costs associated with monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) of Indonesian emissions.

While peat decomposition and emissions from peat fires shall be measured and reported, these will not be included in the results-based payment baseline or the emission reduction result. Set-asides should be made which include peat decomposition and peat fires in the results-based payment model over time. Even though past fire events are not included in the RBP baseline, emissions from past fires shall be measured and reported on this basis.

The RBP baseline will be updated every 5 years but will continue updating if any new knowledge is gained or improvements in methodologies and data. The updated RBP baseline (for a new period) will represent an increase in ambition over time. If the update does not represent an increase in ambition, a percentage increase in ambition will be established.

The RBP baseline will be consistent with the UNFCCC reporting cycle subject to the UNFCCC's periodic agreement. Updating of the RBP baseline will be updated for the upcoming period. Updating of the RBP baseline will consider new knowledge gained and improvements in methodologies and data. The updated RBP baseline (for a new period) will represent an increase in ambition over time.

### Subsequent regrowth after forest cover loss: i.e., "grass" deforestation, the forest degradation area (defined as a change from primary forest to degraded secondary forest) and the degraded postforest area where disturbance has occurred but no regeneration of trees has taken place.

Both derived from the same forest monitoring system using the same methods, procedure and stratification. By default, the reference level is defined by the land cover type at the beginning of the period (1990–2012) at different time points, with a minimum mapping unit of 6.25 ha. Deforestation is identified by the change from forest to non-forest land use and/or land cover.

Forest degradation is identified by the change from primary natural forest land classifications to secondary natural forest land classifications or degraded/secondary forest land classifications in the reference level area.

Using consistent methodologies and data to generate emission factors (EF). The same EF associated with soil land-cover changes based on the constant land-cover stratification are used for both the FREL and RBP.

Including the same carbon pools: above-ground biomass and soil organic carbon. Including the same forest types. Covering the same area: entire national territory. Using the same reference level area where deforestation or forest degradation has occurred is considered as degraded/drainage. Using the same forest definition as that used in calculating to FREL.

The LULUCF team of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) is responsible for consistency of the data and methodologies described in the FREL, for 1990–2012 and in the technical annex with the results of the implementation of the adjusted reduction emissions from deforestation and reducing emissions from forest degradation for 2013–2017.

In the Technical Analysis Interpretation Report (TATR) the LULUCF experts state that Indonesia ensured overall consistency between the FREL, RBP, and the corresponding results. The same reference level area is used in the BUR using consistent methodologies and data to generate activity data (AD), as well as the same EFs.

In recalculating the FREL to represent 1993–2012 and presenting the corresponding results for the sake of the REDD+ RBP, the LULUCF experts have used the same reference level area in the BUR. The same assumptions, definitions or comprehensiveness for either the recalculated FREL or corresponding results. Changes resulting from the recalculation are due to differences between the original data and the revised data as well as the presentation of the average annual emissions for the reference level period, versus the historical average (1990–2012) used in the IPCC technically assessed FREL and the linear projection for soil carbon emissions from peat decomposition.

The LULUCF experts noted that the Party's estimation of the results was undertaken using a transparent and consistent approach.

According to decision 12/CMP.17, paragraph 8, the FREL shall be established taking into account decision ACIP/CP.10/2013, which provides for the establishment of the FREL based on the Parties' estimates of forest sources and removals by forests reported in the Party's GHG inventory. Indonesia clarified during the consultation process that the data used to estimate emissions from deforestation and degradation were based on post-peatland degradation-estimations in its GHG inventories between the first and second BURS. Taking into account the information provided, it is noted by the LULUCF experts that the Party's estimation of results was based on the GHG inventory in the first BUR, the team assessing Indonesia's FREL noted that the Party maintained consistency with its previous estimates.

It was noted by the LULUCF experts that Indonesia's reported emission estimates from peat decomposition in its GHG inventory contained within the second BUR are different from the emission estimates reported in the technical analysis report. The TATR explained that this difference is due to the use of a new methodology for estimating emissions from peat decomposition in the second BUR. Indonesia explained that the methodology used for the estimation was the same, but the activity areas were different and the results were based on the data provided by the Party. It should be noted that the TATR did not AD for REDD+ activities included only the areas of peat decomposition that were forested in 1990. In the TATR, there are no specific technical analysis results related to this activity; it may still be inferred that the examination was considered sufficient.

In terms of series times, the LULUCF experts concluded that the consistency and comparability of the data used by Indonesia for its REDD+ activities – by directly comparing time series of satellite images (rather than analyzing satellite images for time periods individually by overlaying maps to detect changes), as well as (2) producing a consistent time series of forest cover change, were not affected. The TATR noted that the rules in the FREL that also apply to the provision of information on the REDD+ results. Beyond the identification of discrepancies between the data used for the REDD+ activities and those used by Indonesia for its other activities, no concerns regarding consistent application of data and methodologies over the time series data.

The TATR noted that all estimates reported in the BURs are based on the following assumptions: (i) all carbon stocks in peatland and non-peatland forests are estimated as CO2, (ii) the year of land-cover change (i.e., by stopping intentional clearance), (iii) regrowth after land-cover change is not considered, and (iv) all post-deforestation areas where deforestation appears to have ceased is designated as degraded. ## B.2.2 Additional criteria related to the achieved results

### (v) Uncertainties: Explain whether the country has provided information on aggregate uncertainties of the NFI data and/or other sources used in the estimation of REDD+ results, including how it quantifies its uncertainties and provide information on assumptions and sources. If applicable, indicate the process followed for updating these.

The uncertainties of the biomass stock estimates used in the estimation of REDD+ results range from 3% to 50%, depending on the availability of NFI data of each land cover stratum in each island.

A re-design of the current NFI system is being initiated to ensure the sustainability of NFI data measurement, covering all forest lands and other land uses in Indonesia. The new NFI system will be implemented by 2016.

The most considerable uncertainty, however, comes from the estimates of the peatland emissions. Current estimates were based on the IPCC default values, which indicate 50% uncertainty. Therefore, moving to a Tier 2 emission factor for peatlands is expected to reduce this uncertainty and improve the accuracy of the results.

Other donor organisations have already initiated 66 groundwater level monitoring system (GWLS) at various depths in peatland areas in Indonesia. The GWLS data will be available from 2015 onwards, which will help to improve the estimates of emissions from peatlands under concession and non-concession areas. The GWLS data will be available in the future and it will be used for estimating the CO2 emission factor.

It is important to reiterate, as was described above in B.2.V) and was part of a response by the LULUCF experts during the TA, that the overall accuracy estimates for the results were improved to be more comprehensive than those presented in the FREL. The improvements are due to moving from a single uncertainty value in the FREL to improved uncertainty values generated to improve AD for year(s), and also to the use of alternative approaches for estimating emissions from peatland degradation; these improvements are ongoing, with the Olofsson et al (2014) methodology being tested and the results compared.

**Payments that are expected to be received**

Under the Indonesia-Norway Partnership, Indonesia has reported 7.4 MCO2eq/y as results for 2017. Third-party verification is ongoing, but assuming the verified results match the results reported in the Emission Reductions Verification Report (ERVR) for 2017, the 2017 emission reductions expected to be available for payments are 4 MCO2eq/y.

Reductions achieved between 2014 and 2016 have a combined baseline value of 278.5 MCO2eq/y, representing the total annual average emissions for deforestation and forest degradation.

The total emission reductions reported for 2017 under this Partnership was 7.4 MCO2eq/y for 2017, which was then reported to Norway as results for 2017 (in line with Indonesia’s reporting ambition), so that only 50% of the reported emission reductions can be rewarded by Norway or other financiers, which is in line with the GCF’s funding rules. The inclusion of these results in this GCF proposal does not risk double payments for this year. The exclusion of them was a decision taken exclusively for this GCF proposal.

**REDD+ results paid by the GCF**

Indonesia will retire 27MCO2eq upon receipt of payment by the GCF. Indonesia considers retirement of emissions reductions as a means of high environmental integrity. Retirement prevents any further use, for offsetting or other purposes, and therefore preempts any possibility for double counting or double claiming. The GCF has agreed to pay for 50% of the emission reductions achieved between 2014 and 2016 that were reported by GCF for by GCF for by GCF will be retired. These will be evidenced through Indonesia’s registry upon receipt of payment by the GCF.

**Regarding a transparent, accurate, consistent, comparative and comprehensive MRV implementation**, Indonesia has established modalities for National MRV system, which included:

- **National Registry System (NRS)**: https://www.menlhk.go.id/unduh/standar-prosedur.pdf
- **Guidelines for REDD+**: https://litenr-hmnhp.go.id/download/filemanager/filemanager.php?file=1202713
- **Guidelines for MRV**: https://litenr-hmnhp.go.id/download/filemanager/filemanager.php?file=1202715
- **MRV Implementation Plan**: https://www.menlhk.go.id/unduh/standar-prosedur.pdf

Indonesia's MRV scheme for REDD+ follows the five general national MRV process with appropriate adjustments to accommodate alignment with REDD+ funding schemes and its requirements. Indonesia's MRV scheme for REDD+ is based on the National Registry System (NRS) as a central database for all information related to REDD+ in Indonesia. The detail guideline for the MRV for REDD+ is provided in Guideline for MRV REDD+ activities https://litenr-hmnhp.go.id/download/filemanager/filemanager.php?file=1202715. Within the above-mentioned regulations, the role of NRS is prominent.

Indonesia has established a National Registry System (or SNR) for the implementation of REDD+ at national and subnational levels. The SNR provides a central database to manage information related to forest change monitoring, REDD+ actions and resources, and for the avoidance of double-counting. The SNR includes data and information on all aspects of REDD+ activities in Indonesia.

The National Registry System Manager is responsible for maintaining consistency between data and information on the implementation of REDD+ at the national and subnational levels and for avoidance of double counting.

The Moratorium Termination of the Granting of New Permits and Perfecting Natural Primary Forest and Peatland Management (Moratorium) and the Social Forestry programmes were two key initiatives operating during the period. The Moratorium was implemented in 2016, with the aim to provide a framework for REDD+ while striving for consistency and complementarity of REDD+ actions in Indonesia within the national forest programme. The Social Forestry programme was initiated in 2015 as an initiative to improve livelihoods by providing additional information related to these two initiatives, from the perspective of implementation and when considering the role of the safeguards.

The Moratorium Safeguards were addressed and respected during the period. The same applies for the Cancun safeguards in the implementation of the Moratorium policy and Social Forestry programmes during the period. The SNR has been developed with reference to the information provided by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry in the Summary of Information (SOI). The required Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) was conducted as part of the project design process, and the results are available on the SNR until today. The GO already increased its support for the moratorium and Social Forestry through the adoption of new policies and regulations. The GO has also been actively involved in the development of a strategy to enhance the programme's effective implementation and consistency with the Cancun Safeguards. It is understood that the GO will continue to provide support, including financial resources, as necessary, to be pursued to not only ensure avoidance and mitigation of harms, but also take advantage of opportunities to increase the benefits the program can yield for both people and the environment.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 20 OF 40)

As it stands, with the latest revision of the Indicator Map of the Termination of the Granting of Moratoriums map(PRPPI), the Government has committed to protect 68,577 hectares of natural primary forest and 139,000 hectares of secondary forest in the Moratorium area. This means no concessions or permits, so no exploitation of forest resources in the Moratorium area. This means that the Government is committed to protecting forests for all communities. Villages and Adat communities living within this Moratorium map area, and in theory, on existing land titles, have the right to pursue their self-determined priorities and pursue their distinct relationship with the forests and biodiversity within.

The Social Forestry programme allows for the same. The Government has essentially delimited an area of 12.7 million hectares as a Social Forest Area (SFA). This is in addition to the Moratorium area, which provides access rights for local communities and Villages, increased participation of these collectives, often as leads, in sustainable forest management activities, and the development of community-based enterprises where applicable. This is consistent with national forest programming and international conventions' highlights on the importance of involving indigenous peoples and local communities in forest governance, change initiatives, conservation, protection, sustainable development of forest materials and alternative timber-free products, and the recognition that their rights to use, manage, benefit from, and enjoy forests and greater tenure security for these collectives is considered essential to climate change mitigation.

Both the Moratorium and Social Forestry Initiatives were established to be implemented in a manner that contributes to Indonesia's obligations under international law. These include its obligations as a Party to the Paris Agreement, its duties and obligations under international law related to forest management, biodiversity conservation, environmental protection, non-discrimination, and human rights (particularly of indigenous peoples and minorities), among others.

The ESA performed on these initiatives analyses a number of these PiRs that were in existence at the start of the review period. These include the Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan, the 1999 Forest Law, the 2006 Presidential Decree No. 35/2006 on Community Forestry, the 2007 Presidential Decree No. 48/2007 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2008 Presidential Decree No. 19/2008 on Forest Plantations, the 2009 Presidential Decree No. 35/2009 on Community Forestry, the 2014 Presidential Decree No. 67/2014 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2015 Presidential Decree No. 88/2015 on Forest Plantations, the 2016 Presidential Decree No. 39/2016 on Community Forestry, the 2017 Presidential Decree No. 40/2017 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2018 Presidential Decree No. 50/2018 on Forest Plantations, the 2019 Presidential Decree No. 36/2019 on Community Forestry, the 2020 Presidential Decree No. 47/2020 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2021 Presidential Decree No. 58/2021 on Forest Plantations, and the 2022 Presidential Decree No. 39/2022 on Community Forestry.

The ESA recognizes that as resourced and applied, the Government has continued to increase the implementation of these PiRs in a manner consistent with its obligations under international law, particularly thereafter, especially in 2017. This started with conversion of Indonesia's forest policy, the 1999 Forest Law into the 2006 Presidential Decree No. 35/2006 on Community Forestry, which was "designed to be accountable manner" and "be capable of accommodating the dynamics of community aspirations and participation in forest governance". The Government has also continued to implement other laws and regulations that are applicable to Indonesia provide a further enabling environment such as: the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) and its National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan, the 2014 Presidential Decree No. 67/2014 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2015 Presidential Decree No. 88/2015 on Forest Plantations, the 2016 Presidential Decree No. 39/2016 on Community Forestry, the 2017 Presidential Decree No. 40/2017 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2018 Presidential Decree No. 50/2018 on Forest Plantations, the 2019 Presidential Decree No. 36/2019 on Community Forestry, the 2020 Presidential Decree No. 47/2020 on Forest Management and Utilization, the 2021 Presidential Decree No. 58/2021 on Forest Plantations, and the 2022 Presidential Decree No. 39/2022 on Community Forestry.

Further, as required by UNFCCC REDD+ decisions, a system has been developed for providing information on how Indonesia is meeting its commitments under the Paris Agreement. This system, known as the REDD+ Information System (RS-REDD+) was implemented to complement and ensure consistency with the Carbon safeguards and consequently, the national forest monitoring system (NFM). The RS-REDD+ was initially piloted in 2014 and officially launched in 2016 via a MOEF regulation. Strengthening in update and application since 2015, it has since been operationalized as a web-based platform that provides information on Indonesia's forest resources status and activities, continues to enjoy the support of the GEF. Efforts continued to ensure that the RS-REDD+ is supporting the Government's efforts to meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement, strengthening the system and increasing the public's access to information via its online platform (https://www.reddplus.go.id). The RS-REDD+ has been developed in accordance with Indonesia's obligations related to stakeholder involvement and transparency, the development of SS-REDD+, and its subsequent evolution as a key product of an extensive multi-stakeholder process (for more info., see section C.12 below).

Additional information on the consultation and coordination with PLFs can be found in the Annex to the EIA of the two initiatives found in the Annex to the PiR.

(i) Transparent and effective national forest governance structures, taking into account national legislation and sovereignty.
