The REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

The anchoring of Indonesia’s forest policy is the Forestry Law No. 41 (1999) which promotes that the forests of Indonesia are managed “for the people's maximum benefit” and that such management is to be done sustainably, in accordance with the principles of good governance as defined by the same law and the Forestery Law. The same law emphasizes the coordinated, multi-stakeholder approach to forest governance and management, which means that central and regional governments, along with Community and Individuals, shall participate in forestry supervision.

Under President Joko Widodo (2014-present), governance changes over forest and the climate change mitigation landscape have been significant. These changes are reflected in the reorganization of coordination, and most significantly, by creating two leading ministries, Ministry of Forestry and Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The new structure has also led to a more focused approach on climate change coordination in climate change by leaving the recently established MCFD to absorb the roles and responsibilities of the previous MOF and MEF. The new structure is reflected in the reorganization of the Directorate General of Climate Change to be the focal point in Indonesia and globally. The Forest Law thus allows for a more focused approach on forest governance, climate change coordination and forest coordination over a broad array of key elements to forest management, including the Directorate General of Forest Resources Management (DGFRM), the Directorate General of Forest Protection and Conservation (DGFPC) to be the focal point in Indonesia and globally, the Directorate General of Plant and Environment Development (DGPEDE), the Directorate General of Sustainable Production Forest Management (DGSPFM), the Directorate General of Forestry Research and Development (DGFRD), the Directorate General of Forest Product Industries (DGPI), the Directorate General of Forest and Environmental Partnership (DGKEP) and the Directorate General of Management of Watersheds and Protection Areas (DGKPA). The DGFRM, DGSPFM, DGPEDE, DGPI, DGKEP and DGKPA have been merged into one entity called the Ministry of Forestry and Environment. The DGFRM is now responsible for managing the SIS-REDD+ process. The DGSPFM is now responsible for managing the SIS-REDD+ process. The DGPEDE is now responsible for managing the SIS-REDD+ process.

Improved forest governance is also achieved through the design and implementation of various measures to increase transparency. One way to achieve transparency is through the centralization of critical data and the harmonization of information systems. Indonesia has been working on this since 2014, when the National Climate Change change made operational in 2016 (Ministerial Decree No. T/II/2017). It is a web-based platform intended to among other things, provide access to forest data and information for monitoring and reporting of climate change mitigation; and document activities and resources on adaptation and mitigation of climate change, including the compilation of national greenhouse gas inventories. The system also provides access to information from community and civil society organizations, all while making public data and information on documented measures available through a single portal. Indonesia has also been working with the Global Reporting System for Greenhouse Gas Emissions (System Inventarisasi Gas Rumah Kaca Nasional, Signa-SMART). The ministerial Decree No. T/II/2017 requires the publishing of an annual GHG inventory report (Presidential Regulation No. T/II/2017).

Another flagship of Indonesia’s efforts to improve transparency and the systematization and harmonization of forest data is the development of a single reference geospatial information system (GIS). The Decree No. 9 of 2016 on Accelerating One Map Policy through use of 150,000 GIS is being pursued and further strengthened by the Ministry of Forestry and Environment. The Decree No. 9/2016 was issued by the Presidential Delivery Unit for Development Monitoring and Oversight to take the lead in developing a single reference geospatial information system (GIS) as a national asset. The single reference geospatial information system (GIS) is intended to be utilized by many parties by providing easy access to the society to forest data and information, including the compilation of national greenhouse gas inventories. The use of one reference geospatial information system (GIS) will increase transparency and sharing of information across all users. Access will increase transparency, and the sharing and combination of information across multiple users.

The Go Green Indonesia initiative is a government-led program to improve forest governance through the revision of the Ministry and Social Forest Indicators map. This is particularly salient given over forests in Indonesia are managed by both the state and communities. The initiative aims at improving access and the use of materials to identify potential opportunities and conflict. In the case of the Social Forest Forestry Law No. 41 (1999) which states that the forest should be managed for the benefit of the community, handling Tenurial and Customary Forest Conflict of the Directorate General for Social Forest and Environment is a key element to forest governance in Indonesia. The initiative aims at improving access to information which can be used, at least, in parallel with the Indonesian State Forestry map, not used to modify or reform it. The Indonesian State Forestry map can also be used along with national and district spatial history consultations with local action to coordinate national, regional and local efforts to verify customary rights and


---
**Page 25**
---

land tenure upon receipt of applications under the Social Forestry framework. This map can also help with conflict resolution in the moment and avoid future grievances arising from overlapping claims. The production of this map is a joint initiative between the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MoEF) and work done by the civil society actor, AMAN, of over 93 million hectares of Adat customary forests. Finding mechanisms to ensure that these communities are able to participate in forest governance, through community participation in forest governance, the avoidance and resolution of conflicts, and most simply, a way that such actors can use the same language to work together to manage forest resources across whole landscapes.

Further, transparency is enhanced through the Sis-REDD+ and MoEF governance online platforms (discussed further below). The REDD+ Action Plan 2015-2019 has been developed in accordance with the laws governing both matters (MoEF Regulations Nos. 7/2017 and 49/2017 respectively), each platform strives to increase transparency, accountability and participation of all stakeholders involved in forest governance.

The REDD+ Action Plan 2015-2019 has been developed in accordance with the laws governing both matters (MoEF Regulations Nos. 7/2017 and 49/2017 respectively), each platform strives to increase transparency, accountability and participation of all stakeholders involved in forest governance.

The GoI has also made concrete efforts on improving forest management system per Presidential Instruction No. 11/2015 on Forests and Land Fire Management System (FOLMS) and the Forest Act No. 46/2014, which govern forest and fire management system at their jurisdictions and implement sanctions for business players who do not comply with the law. The Presidential Instruction No. 38/2015 on National Forests and Land Fire Management System (FOLMS) has created different entities from the national to community and village levels. These include the National Forest Fire Control Command Center, the National Fire Force for Handling Forest and Land Fires, the Forest Fire Control Core Team, Fire Concerned Communities, Forest Fire Watchers, and Village Fire Watchers.

While the above contains only a few highlights related to REDD+ forest governance and transparency, collectively the material described efforts to make access to information simpler and eventually facilitate the sharing of information between local communities and sub-national governments as well as the national government. The GoI has also made concrete efforts on improving forest management system per Presidential Instruction No. 11/2015 on Forests and Land Fire Management System (FOLMS) and the Forest Act No. 46/2014, which govern forest and fire management system at their jurisdictions and implement sanctions for business players who do not comply with the law. The Presidential Instruction No. 38/2015 on National Forests and Land Fire Management System (FOLMS) has created different entities from the national to community and village levels. These include the National Forest Fire Control Command Center, the National Fire Force for Handling Forest and Land Fires, the Forest Fire Control Core Team, Fire Concerned Communities, Forest Fire Watchers, and Village Fire Watchers.

The REDD+ programming also strengthened forest governance at the local level through the establishment, and now a renewed commitment to strengthen the Forest Management Units (FMUs) (see RBP Project Activity 2.1). This is done by strengthening the capacity of the Forest Management Units (FMUs/KPMS), which are responsible for developing, implementing, and/or overseeing the land forest governance at the local level. The FMU/KPMs are also responsible for managing and monitoring forest fire control and other illegal practices, supporting local communities and Villages in applying for one of the arrangements under the Forest Act No. 46/2014, and reporting to the concerned authorities. The FMUs are joint authority over state forests, with a strong role from sub-national governments (provincial and district level) as well.

FMUs are the basis for governing and managing all forest areas and functions at the local level based on forest management plans. These plans are developed by the Forest Management Units (FMU/KPMs), which report to local stakeholders. In 2007, the government passed legislation to prioritize FMUs and safeguard the public interest in forests. The FMUs were established as a result of the National Forest Policy (NFP) and the National Development Planning (NDP). The NFP requires that "no FMU budget is allocated on achieving REDD+ targets". The NDP requires that "FMUs are to be funded by the state budget". The Ministry of Environment and Forests has requested the MoEF to have a "No FMU budget" allocation in achieving Medium Term Development Plan 2015-2019, it is to have a FMDA FMU operational by 2020. FMUs play an important role in ensuring that forest governance at the local level, forest management plans that depend on multiple actors for proper implementation and monitoring.

Also contributing to forest governance within the Social Forestry Programme (MoEF Decree Nos. 83/2016), the Forest Act No. 46/2014, and the National Forest Policy (NFP) is the role of the village forest manager, the forest officer a forest utilization / forest service business permit, a state license for forest management, and the community forest management plan. These plans enable private sector individuals or groups and local community or Village people to work together to develop and implement forest management plans in managing Forest resources within district areas of State Forests. This decree did not invent social forestry, but did play its role as a single instrument, practically always


---
**Page 26**
---

In place of other policies, laws and regulations governing community and village forest management, ultimately, Social Forestry is a recognition that forest governance has been best achieved when those depending on forests have had their livelihoods work in harmony and potential conflicts between such users are resolved through agreement.

Additional information on transparency and national forest governance structures can be found in the ESA found in the Annex to this EIA.


(v) Respect for the knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples and members of local communities, by taking into account relevant international obligations, national circumstances and the provisions of the United Nations Declaration as adopted by the United Nations General Assembly has applied the UNDRIP.

Comments:

The rights of the rural local communities, Villages and Adat communities in the context of the Moratorium and the Forest programming is discussed at length in the EIA and its 6 annexes. Incorporated by reference into the response to Q (5.10) is the text at section C.5 below on stakeholder participation, (including indigenous peoples). The EIA also refers to the UNDRIP and other international instruments as a number of the challenges and critiques of the Social Forestry Programme as related to indigenous peoples in Indonesia are discussed in the EIA. These include the lack of recognition of customary forest rights by mitigation measures defined for new RSP projects (see annex ESME), many of which build upon policies established by the Government, specifically the Director General of Social Forestry and Environmental Partnership.

Considering the interest of local community, Villages and Adat community lands with forests it is estimated that 72% of these communities are not covered under any existing forest management plans. The EIA notes that Goti has taken notable measures to affirm the rights of these populations to have access, use and effective and meaningful participation in decision-making processes related to their customary forest resources.

The Wabes administration fortified the Social Forestry programme in 2014 by increasing the previously designated 0.6 million hectares of Adat Forests to 3.5 million hectares. This was done as a result of community advocacy and an expression of a political commitment to provide both protection and opportunities for Adat communities to manage their forests according to their own traditional practices, knowledge and values, and the distinct ways of life of these populations were of integral value to avoiding deforestation and achieving forest conservation.

Acknowledging that there are vast numbers of communities and Villages still awaiting recognition as an Adat (indigenous) peoples, as well as recognition of their customary Adat Forests (per trees), for many the Social Forestry programme is not a viable option. The EIA notes that this is due to lack of legal certainty.

The current PAPM sets aside 12.7 million hectares of State forest to be subject to leases, permits or forest permit agreements with communities and villages. This is in addition to the 3.5 million hectares of Adat Forests. While not done through an established and written protocol on free, prior and informed consent (FPIC), the EIA notes that there are a number of mechanisms for FPIC in place.

Indicators related to monitoring the impacts of REDD+ activities these processes are done by communities and villages themselves with support from the government. The EIA notes that this is done through their designated leaders and governing institutions. The Social Forestry Programme respects the self-determination of indigenous peoples and local communities, and does not impose the leases, permits and partnership agreements on them. What does exist however is a lack of recognition for customary forest rights in the context of the Forest programme as designed solely by the State. Although every five years when spatial planning is reviewed, there are opportunities to include these areas into the Forest Programme.

The subject of some debate, the mechanism by which a permit/lease or use/access rights will be transitioned into a full legal title within the community or Village so designee - has not been defined yet though it is arguably not prohibited by law. The EIA notes that this is because the ESFIP that accompanies the PAPM does not recognize and/or does not provide for communities to have use/access or ownership of their traditional lands, forests and territories.

Adat forests and the more ample property rights they enjoy along with such recognition, the Social Forestry permits, licenses and agreements are recognized as many as provisions of law. However, in the process of processing, granting, the permit/lease or use/access right to exclude those that would otherwise intrude into their lands and imparably exploit the very forest resources upon which their physical and cultural survival depends.


---
**Page 27**
---

Through opportunities to secure both support and resources from the State to engage in sustainable forestry (Community Forest Management), communities are able to generate forest-based enterprises and livelihoods,
opportunities to reduce poverty and improve livelihoods. Through new laws emerging after the 2013 Constitutional amendment, which repealed the Forestry Law of 1974, and through a series of amendments
to the Social Forestry Programme has become more complementary and consistent with the objectives of the
Constitution. The introduction of the Forest Management Unit (FMU) as an alternative to the customary
mechanisms has been put in place to issue FMUs for Adat forests which can prove control over their lands prior to the issuance of a title. This has made the process of issuing titles to communities under the auspices
of the Social Forestry Programme has been made more equitable and possible through new, simpler application procedures. The introduction of the FMU system has also facilitated the implementation of field
investigations and verifications of license, permits, and partnership agreements to identify potential
forestlands for community management. As a result, 102,435 hectares of forestland were identified as suitable
throughout the review period; by December of 2017, 438,690 FMUs were established, and 29 of 34 provinces in Indonesia have now issued titles to communities under the auspices of the Social Forestry Programme,
Local Champions, - all empowered to accelerate the programme, socialize with communities, and assist in
the application process.

Furthermore, as a signatory to the Convention on Biological Diversity which provides that States will "respect,
maintain and preserve knowledge, innovations and practices of indigenous and local communities embodying traditional ecological knowledge", the Government has been working towards ensuring that the Social Forestry Programme demonstrate this respect. Specifically, Article 52 of Decree No. 83 on Social Forest provides recognition to indigenous peoples and local communities as forest owners and managers. The decree further
provides that "utilization of timber from forest products and/or in non-timber forest products and/or in environmental services shall be carried out by the community or individual who has obtained a title and
approved by traditional institutions". Technical guidance on local wisdom / traditional knowledge are further regulated under Decree No. 83, which provides that "the utilization of forest resources is subject to customary
law and regulations". The decree also states that communities are entitled to manage and utilize these forests in accordance with tribal wisdom including integrated farming systems, agroforestry, and other forms of sustainable land use. Furthermore, Decree No. 83 on Social Forest Procedures for Complaints and Handling of Complaints due to Alleged Pollution and / or Environmental Damage and/or Forest Damage (2017) provides a ground for complaint if there is "utilization of forest
resources that violates the customary law".

Nevertheless, the Social Forestry Programme is not without its critics. In response, the Government has largely acknowledged where its ambitions to cut 12.7 million hectares under Social Forestry must also be met with additional support from the State and civil society organizations. The Government has been working towards
respecting the rights of Adat communities. As highlighted in section E.S-5.Early, a number of shortcomings of the programme have been identified by the DG of Forest and Environmental Partnership. These include the lack of
the DG of Social Forestry and Environmental Partnership's creation of an indicative Adat forest map, as well as the lack of recognition to indigenous peoples and local communities as forest owners and managers under
Decree No. 83. The Government has been working towards addressing these shortcomings by providing
their recognition of local communities as Adat communities (a precondition to land titling). The ESM attached to this document provides a summary of the recommendations made in the report, including the need for
mitigation measures (RPM) to improve delivery of benefits and opportunities to indigenous peoples and local communities. These include the need for the DG of Social Forestry and Environmental Partnership,
before the legislature is lands bill and bill on Adat rights which will eventually strengthen the RPG as it pertains to social forestry.

Taking a broader overview of the REDD+ programme that provides the backdrop and foundation of the Social
Forestry Programme, it is also important for representatives of indigenous communities and their supporting NGOs to understand how they can participate in the various national and international forums discussed in this section and section E below (including to developing the Sis-REDD+, PCCAs, APGs and NCDs).

In terms of Adat law and community local and Village women, while collective rights of use, access, even ownership of forest resources are recognized under Indonesian law, these rights have not been fully realized. This is
through the Social Forestry (whether through the protection and conservation of forest resources, ecosystem or sustainable management) has not been fully realized by women in Indonesia. The reasons for this include:
within the collective: women, youth, elderly. As the Social Forestry programme is strengthened through the proposed RPG Project (Activity 2.7.1), it is envisioned that all women will be included and overall, all programmatic activities will be implemented with a gender-responsive approach. This includes, for example, special measures

# REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GREEN CLIMATE FUNDS) (PAGE 25 OF 80)

To target more systematic use of gender analysis and collection of gender response and sex-disaggregated data, and increased gender equity in participatory forums: capacity sessions, multi-stakeholder bodies, parties to partnership agreements, etc. The REDD+plus RBP is consistent with the recommendations of the results of the EISA, ESFII, and Gender Assessment and Action Plan found in the Annex III (A) to this FP.

Also as a form of respecting rights that includes the right to justice and a remedy, the national grievance mechanism has been established. The REDD+plus RBP is consistent with the recommendations of the EISA, ESFII, and was and continues to be available to receive grievances from local communities, Villages and Adam Communes.

Taking into account the above provided information, it is anticipated that the strengthening of REDD+ including monitoring framework, including monitoring mechanisms, and the expansion and strengthening of the FMUs and Social Forestries will be consistent with the recommendations of the EISA, ESFII, and the other measures recommended to date and building upon the efforts the Government has already begun. There is great potential for further strengthening of the REDD+plus RBP project consistency with the Carlsson Safeguards related to local community and indigenous peoples' rights.

### (iv) The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions referred to in paragraphs 50 and 72 of UICP-18:

#### Comments:
Please refer to the relevant comments in sections C.1.2 (Stakeholder Involvement), E.3 (Consultation with Stakeholders) and E.5 (Indigenous Peoples) below for a fuller response to the program's consistency with this safeguard.

In addition to the information provided in the aforementioned section, it is be highlighted that the Moratorium and Social Forestry initiatives were both commenced with substantial support by civil society. They each responded to the need to protect forests from illegal logging, forest fires, encroachment by settlers and pastoralists, through prohibitions on licensing and concessioning, and for the rights of use, access and participation in decision-making related to forest resources. The Moratorium was designed as a measure that most depend on forest resources for their livelihoods and cultural identity. The two initial indicator maps justify the need for the Moratorium, which is currently being enforced by the police and military forces.

Moratorium was heavily dependent on stakeholder engagement, in terms of local populations, except with respect to the indigenous peoples who were not consulted or involved at all. This was due to threats and threats, including intruders. This facilitated Moratorium enforcement and forest protection.

On the other hand, the Social Forestries Programme involved regular stakeholder engagement as the participation of communities in decision-making related to forest resources is a key element of this voluntary participatory way, via applications that involved regular exchanges with local communities and the government. The programme was designed to provide opportunities for communities to participate in collective action and other private actors trying to engage in sustainable community timber plantations and/or the dedication of land for conservation purposes.

As noted below, while the voluntary application for licenses, permits and forest partnership agreements was a baseline requirement of the Social Forestry, concerns were raised as to the extent to which all grants of forest resources were subject to such applications. This is because some communities or individuals might have sustained claims on the underlying lands. Where the local communities or Villages were in fact contracted with private companies for timber production and/or other activities, it was not clear whether they were still the soliciting parties and programme beneficiaries. Consistent with the UNDP's Social and Environmental Safeguards, the REDD+plus RBP is consistent with the recommendations of the EISA, ESFII, and when and how - for future applications to activities that may affect Adam communities' rights and interests, lands, resources and livelihoods, it will be necessary to ensure that they are fully informed and consulted by all concerned.

### Actions to address the risks of reversals.

Comments:

**Concepts of Reversals/Non-reversance of Carbon:**

Reversal is a major technical issue that was widely discussed as part of the REDD+ regulations under the UNFCCC, and in the draft Global Standard of Reference (TorR) of the GCF-REDD+ RBP pilot program. Under both the UNFCCC and the GCF-REDD+ RBP pilot program, it was decided to include reversals under uncertainty.

The risk of "reversals" describes the possibility of reversing climate benefits through the loss of forest carbon biomass, for example through fires or pest outbreaks. There are two types of reversals (i) intentional such as harvesting timber and (ii) unintentional such as natural disturbances.

Recognizing the challenges in attributing and separating natural from anthropogenic effects on emissions, i.e., factoring out "natural emissions and sinks", countries report environmental reversals only for those lands that are considered to be under their jurisdiction. This is because it would be difficult to attribute any reduction or lack of option of excluding emissions from natural disturbances.

There is no such flexibility under REDD+ even though there is a lot of agreed definition for reversals, neither under the UNFCCC nor the GCF-REDD+ RBP pilot program. Due to unintentional circumstances, emissions may increase and this is considered as a reversal. However, if emissions are decreasing below the FREL again, not be considered a reversal trend. It is unclear if a single occurrence of reversals can be considered as a trend or part of a trend.

### Indonesia's actions to address the risk reversals

In this proposal, Indonesia is seeking results-based payments for the emission reductions of 9 Mio/tons annually from 2014-2030. The baseline period will be 2010-2013 and the reference period (FREL) eq. The GCF will be paying for about 19% of the total volume of carbon reductions achieved reducing deforestation in Indonesia in years 2014 to 2016. This means that the risk of reversals of emissions paid for by


---
**Page 30**
---

**REDDO-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL**

**(GREEN CLIMATE FUND)** (Page 27 of 80)

The GCF is relatively low and could be compensated with surplus emissions that are not being paid for by the GCF.

It is important to note, nevertheless, the important policies and measures that the Government of Indonesia has put in place to reduce the risk of reversals both at the national and subnational level. The following are examples of such initiatives:

1. **Policies, laws and regulations have been introduced to prevent and manage forest fires:**
   a. National Forest Fire Management System (NFFMS) which is implemented at all levels of government to develop land and forest fire management system at their jurisdictions.
   b. RBP has established the National Control of Forest and Land Fires that empowered different entities from national to community and county level such as National Task Force for Handling Forest and Land Fires, Provincial Fire Management Team, District Fire Management Team, and Village Fire Fines.
   c. Ministry of Agriculture Regulation No. 5/2018 on Land Clearance and Management for Plantation Without Burning that mandates all estate crop concession holders to maintain environmental sustainability in their land management practices.
   d. Other practices to avoid fires are described in the ESEA Annexes (Annex A, indicator “PLRs Maintain and enhance biodiversity of ecosystems” and Annex B “Conserve biodiversity and maintain and enhance benefits of ecosystem services”).

2. **Community engagement in forest and land fire prevention:**
   Community engagement is vital to protect the environment and reduce the risk of forest fires. For example, by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, one is seen in a Daops Dumai in Riau province, where the following actions have been taken:

   - **SOCIALIZATION:** Awareness efforts through socialization and other efforts to increase community awareness.
   - **SIGNAGE:** Creation of Warning Boards and fire hazard rating information.
   - **RIVER BANKS:** Construction of river banks and embankments for flood protection through river normalization, canal block construction, reservoirs, and bore wells.
   - **COMMUNICATION:** Establishment of communication systems such as the provision of information, warning and prevention campaigns.
   - **EARLY WARNING SYSTEM (EWS):** Use of EWS to detect early through patrol and environmental safety system (Sistem Keamanan Lingkungan or skamling in Indonesian language) by doing regular patrols.

3. Centralized forest monitoring and information systems to track mitigation activities. Ministry Regulation No. 18/2015 provides for a Directorate of Forest Resources Inventory and Monitoring (PSHAKL) under the Forestry Agency (BKSDA). The PSHAKL is responsible for establishing units to perform distinct tasks and responsibilities, i.e., (a) forest inventory, (b) forest monitoring, (c) forest management, (d) forest protection, (e) forest restoration, (f) forest land use planning, (g) forest reversions. Indonesia's NAMA and corresponding regulations related to evaluation, reporting, and verification of GHG emissions are also implemented by the PSHAKL. The PSHAKL is responsible for timely knowledge of forest changes. Equally important is the National Greenhouse Gas Inventory System (Sistem Inventarisasi Gas Rumah Kaca Nasional - SINGRAK) under the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MENLHK). Regulation No. 71/2015 which mandates the development of annual GHG inventory report to be published periodically in accordance with the provisions of the Kyoto Protocol. The GHG inventory at sub-national level can be delivered and centralized through MDEP, improving the analysis and documentation of forest carbon stocks and flows. The National Greenhouse Gas Inventory System Decree 71/2015 was operationalized since 2016 and collects all on activities undertaken

## 5. Peatland Restoration Information & Monitoring System (PRISM) web-platform

Online Web GIS platform for monitoring peatland restoration activities, including the restoration of degraded peatlands. PRISM enables users to monitor restoration activities, peak degradation indicators and restoration impact.

### Includes:

- **Action Plan**: Multi-agency coordination and multi-stakeholders collaboration.
- **Trends & Alerts**: GLAD tree cover loss alerts with 5-month trends, and threats for tree cover loss in the next five months.
- **Restoration Implementation Spatial Distribution**: Allows users to observe the spatial distribution of restoration activities across peatland areas. This feature was introduced as a priority in restoration priority provinces since 2016. Types of restoration implemented by Peatland Restoration Unit (PRU) and Forest Resource Gambar of Borneo in Indonesian language consists of:
    - Deep well for past meeting
    - Firebreaks
    - Revegetation through active intervention such as establishing nurseries, planting trees, and seedling dispersal
- **Monitoring**: Peatland Health Index (PHI): HIPPO is one of several indicators of forestland fires, whereby the higher the number and concentration of fire points, the more severe the damage. These pre-set displays highlight distribution in the past 24 hours with a confidence level of 95% (based on data from the Indonesian Forest Resource Management System). Tanakau's Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectrometer (MODIS) is used to monitor forest fires using the Terra/Aqua MODIS Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite (AIRS) with a spatial resolution of 375 meters. This data is updated daily.
- **Global Land Analysis and Discovery (GLAD)** alerts for tree cover loss
- **Database**: Data on peatland areas, restoration activities, and the location and distribution pattern of Peat Care Villages (PCV) within peatland areas in 7 BRR

#### Green Climate Fund (Page 8 of 10)

**restoration priority provinces: Phat Care Village (Desa Pedalul Gunung), DPG**, is a village development program in BRG priority restoration area. Among other things, the project aims to improve water quality and quantity through soil moisture monitoring, resolution, recognition and registration of rights and access, institution building for watershed management, strengthening of local knowledge, and community fire prevention in rural areas and villages. The project also seeks to build capacity among communities in fire prevention, detection, remediation, and ties with the project (DPP).

**6. Peatland Water Monitoring System (SPALAGA):** The BRG, through the DFG for Research and Development, developed the latest information technology innovation in the form of a Peatland Water Monitoring System (SPALAGA) to monitor peatlands using a time-lapse camera and soil moisture monitoring tool (TMA) that can measure peat soil moisture, radial levels, air temperature and humidity as well as rainfall. The system is installed at 7 Restoration Priority Provinces. Monitoring TMA on peatlands is important for preventing the occurrence of land fires (karhutla). Continuous monitoring prevents peatlands from losing water content irreversibly. The reduced water on peatlands is a major cause of fire and subsequent loss of biodiversity, as well as the release of greenhouse gases into the atmosphere due to decomposition and total dryness (irreversible damage). The government has tried to prevent damage to the peatland ecosystem by implementing various policies such as the moratorium on new forest concessions. However, the utilization of peat ecosystems must be done by maintaining the hydrological function of peat. The SPALAGA system is a tool that can help monitor and maintain the water content in peatlands so that forest and land fires (karhutla) due to drought. The system was introduced by the BRG as a prevention measure against fire and loss of biodiversity caused by the drying up of peatland ecosystems.

If something unexpected happens, the BRG Head immediately informs the Task Force to increase the number of monitoring points in the area where the damage occurs so that they can come together with the Village Head and the community to help.

**7. Results-based payments as key financial incentives for the permanence of REDD+ results:** The implementation of the REDD+ program is expected to be accompanied by continuous, adequate and predictable results-based payments in accordance with the relevant international standards. In addition, the government has also made efforts to support decentralized sustainable forest governance will be a central strategy to address the risk of reversals. Agreements with donors have been signed but no resources have been received so far. In this process, expectations are that the implementation of results-based payments will be able to provide certainty on the ground have been waiting to receive resources from results-based payments that have yet to materialize. The government has also made efforts to ensure the permanence of the results already achieved in Indonesia.

**Conclusion**

The actions described above provide a general summary of the initiatives that the Government of Indonesia has put in place to address the risk of reversals and ensure the permanence of its REDD+ results. These actions are in accordance with international best practices and with the GCF current rules. Actions to address the risk of reversals and ensure the permanence of REDD+ results will be able to reduce forest degradation and increase in deforestation. It is nevertheless clear that the Government of Indonesia is committed to anticipate this challenge by taking action to prevent it. This RBP proposal comes in the right direction to assist the Government of Indonesia in these efforts.

Please describe and provide evidence that the Canopy safeguard information was made transparently available to stakeholders.

Stakeholder engagement in REDD+ began early in the preparation phase including with the development of the National REDD Strategy (STRANAS). Building upon that, stakeholders were then involved in key stages of the REDD-plus RBP proposal. The following are some examples around how to monitor and demonstrate project respect and compliance with the Canopy safeguards.

National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS)

The STRANAS was developed between 2012 December under Decision No. 3/Permenhut/Kep/09/2012. It was coordinated by the State Ministry of National Development Planning (BAPPENAS) based on its mandate from the Council of Ministers. The STRANAS is a strategic document that provides a clear, transparent and accountable and reliable roadmap for REDD+ in Indonesia.

BAPPENAS followed the four key principles:

1. Inclusiveness: BAPPENAS ensured that all stakeholders were involved directly or indirectly.
2. Transparency: BAPPENAS ensured that the process was open to the public, with the values of openness, honesty, and clarity, and with the public having access to information concerning the stages of implementation as well as the results.
3. Credibility: With the formulation process managed by reputable institutions or individuals, and conducted based on an inclusive, transparent, and reliable approach.
4. Institutionalization: BAPPENAS ensured that the STRANAS was institutionalized in organizations, resources, structure and organizational mechanism which describe the six key aspects: order, autonomy, adaptability, connectivity, resilience, and legitimacy.

The STRANAS development process was guided by (i) a team of seven writers with REDD+ expertise; (ii) a working group comprising civil society including among others, the Aliansi Masyarakat Adat Nusantara (AMAN); (iii) a national expert meeting which reviewed the draft document and provided feedback to BAPPENAS; and (iv) a steering committee consisting of high government officials from BAPPENAS, the then-ministry of Forestry, Ministry of Environment and Forestry, and the Indonesian Red Cross.

Multi-stakeholder consultations were held in seven regions that covered 33 provinces in Indonesia, at the national as well as international levels. This was followed by a national expert meeting which further revised the draft document. The final version of STRANAS was then submitted to the Council of Ministers for presentation to the multi-stakeholder conforming REDD+ Task Force which consists of ten working groups which are each responsible for developing detailed action plans and strategies in their respective areas.

The STRANAS integrates gender considerations and attention to equality in rules, needs, and responsibilities of men and women reflecting the increasing awareness and commitment to integrated gender equality into REDD+ policies.

### Safeguards Information System

In early 2011, the previous Ministry of Forestry commenced a multi-stakeholder process to develop a Safeguards Information System (SIS) that would:

- Assess the relevance of national-level instruments to evaluate their relevance to the Cancun safeguards.
- Translate the Cancun safeguards into the national context with appropriate principles, criteria and indicators (PCIs) for each of the Cancun Safeguards were established (and made public in 2013), along with various other tools and guidance documents.
- Identified the most suitable structure and mechanism for SIS-REDD+ and drafted its institutional design.
- Determined and developed assessment tools for safeguards implementation.

The main outputs from this process included:

- The national-level, named Sin-SEREDD+, that compiled information based on 7 principles, 17 criteria and 32 indicators on safeguard implementation in Indonesia. These were then translated into PCIs at the provincial level.
- Various other tools and guidance documents developed to support SIS-REDD+ implementation.

During 2011 to 2013 the development of the PCIs for Sin-SEREDD+ was an inclusive process, facilitated through multi-stakeholder participation through three national workshops and 7 Focus Group Discussions. Nine public consultation events were conducted, including regional workshops and focus group discussions, and a number

## C.2 Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

### C.2.1 General

Provide a description on how the process will be reinforced in activities consistent with the country's NDC, including the use of proceeds from REDD+ and other carbon markets. The description should include how the process will be used in a manner that contributes to the long-term sustainability of REDD+ plus projects.

### Indonesia submitted its first Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the UNFCCC on November 2016. In this NDC, Indonesia voluntarily commits to reduce its greenhouse gases emissions against the business as usual scenario by:

- By 29% in 2030, unconditionally;
- By an additional 45% with a level of ambition of 41% by 2030, subject to availability of international support for finance, technology transfer and development and capacity building.

The importance of REDD+ is clearly reflected in the first Indonesian NDC, which mentions explicitly that "REDD+ will be an important component of Indonesia's efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions". The importance of REDD+ is also highlighted by the fact that Indonesia has consistently that REDD+ must be mainstreamed into national policies and strategies at sub-national level as well as in its overall emission reduction target as well as access positive incentives through REDD+ results-based payments.

The NDC highlights the significant steps that Indonesia has already taken to reduce emissions in the land use and forestry sector, including: reducing deforestation and forest degradation of remaining forests, restoring ecosystem functions, as well as sustainable management of natural resources. The NDC also points out the importance attributed to landscape-scale and ecosystem management approach, emphasizing the critical role of sub-national jurisdictions to ensure greater and more enduring benefits from REDD+.

The active participation of civil society organizations, local communities and the most vulnerable groups, especially women and indigenous peoples, as well as the private sector, including small and medium enterprises. The National Action Plan for Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions 2010-2020 (Rank-GIK) in 2011, aimed at reducing Indonesia's greenhouse gas emissions by 35% below business-as-usual levels by 2020 with BAU and adequate international support. The Rank-GIK provides the basis for the implementation of various initiatives to reduce emissions from land use change and forest sector, as well as other sectors. Indonesia's commitments in its NDC and NDC submission, Rank-GIK sets two targets for the forestry sector: land and forest restoration targets and social forestry targets (more details are on this below).

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 18 OF 20)

The National Medium-Term Development Plan 2015-2019 (RPJMN 2015-2019) established GHG emissions reduction targets for various sectors (forestry and post-lands, agriculture, energy and transportation, industry, and waste) to achieve the 2020 target in line with the RAN-WRI.

The National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS), which was released in 2012 and which substantive content remains unchanged until now, is divided into two main pillars: 1. Institutions and processes; 2. Laws, Programs, Actions and Implementation. The STRANAS presents the many Policies and Measures (P&Ms) that Indonesia has implemented to collectively achieve its REDD+ targets.

A. Addressing institutional gaps: This pillar aims at strengthening institutions in order to tackle the barriers to sustainable management of forests, conservation and enhancement of carbon stocks. It contributes to a more sustainable and equitable development.

B. Contributing to a more sustainable and equitable development: This pillar aims at contributing to a more sustainable and equitable development through various actions and plans while mainstreaming REDD+ into national policies.

The STRANAS is complemented by REDD+ Provincial Strategy and Action Plans (Strategi dan Rencana Aksi Provinsi - SRAPIs), the eleven REDD+ pilot provinces. SRAPI documents describe elements of deforestation and forest degradation, as well as the institutional framework for addressing these issues in each province, taking into account the specific circumstances of each province. SRAPIs are aimed to be revised periodically to ensure alignment with policy changes at national level.

The STRANAS and SRAPI documents serve as reference documents supporting the mainstreaming of climate change and REDD+ into sub-national development planning processes and systems.

## Pillar of the National REDD+/Strategy

### (i) Institutions and Process

#### REDD+ Institutions:
- A. The national level: A REDD+ National Preparation Task Force was established in place.
- B. Since 2015 based on Presidential Regulation No. 16/2015, the tasks of the Agency for the Conservation of Natural Resources and Environment (BKSDA) under the Directorate General of Climate Change (DGCC) are to: i) Develop a National REDD+ Strategy; ii) Implement the provisions in Article 119 of the Constitution in the form of Working Group, Commission, or Task Force.
- C. At the provincial level: The REDD+ Provincial Strategy and Action Plan (SRAPI) at Sub-national level are encouraged to participate in NDC institutions, which are led by DGCC in MOEP in coordination with BKSDA.

#### Funding Institution:
- Establishment of a separate fund for financing REDD+ activities, including climate change funds.
- Regulation 462/OTR/2017 on the Economic Instrument for Environment, giving mandate that REDD+ funding should be managed by the Public Service Agency.
- Regulation 589/OTR/2017 establishing the Environmental Fund and providing guidelines for its management. The Public Service Agency is to manage environmental funds, including REDD+ financing.
- Regulation 634/OTR/2017 on the Procedures of the Environmental Fund.
- Regulation 635/OTR/2017 on the Procedure of the SLU model for the Environmental Fund.

#### MRV Institution:
- In place under Ministerial Decree TI/2017 in National Registry. The SRIN includes the following: i) Monitoring and reporting; ii) Verification and validation; iii) Reporting double counting of REDD+ actions and resources according to the principle of integrity, transparency, and consistency.
- Guidelines for MRV of climate change actions and support in place.

#### Land rents:
- Land rent forest governance reform is established.
- The land rent system for forests is given by the national government.
- Migration and Social Forestry Reform is ongoing.

## Table 4. Implementation status of the National REDD+ strategy as of January 2018

As highlighted in the 2nd Biennial Update Report (BR), the five priority policies issued and implemented by Indonesia to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, including logging in primary forests, fire, restructuring the forestry sector industries including enhancing plantation development, reforestation and conserving forests, promoting sustainable land use, and strengthening local economies.

Some key policies are further presented hereafter:

### Moratorium

Among many programmes and organizations to achieve these 5 priority policies, Indonesia imposed in 2011 a moratorium on the issuance of new concessions in primary forests and peatlands, renewed by Presidential Instruction No. 34/2016 dated 8 December 2016.

Through this moratorium, 6.7 million natural forests and peatlands are legally protected from planned deforestation activities for economic purposes or other uses such as agriculture in the concession areas, such as legally reducing the function of conservation forests to production forests in order to grant use permits.

This moratorium sends a clear and strong message about the importance of protecting peatlands and resulted in substantial emission reductions. According to Wijaya et al (2017), the moratorium has actually the greatest potential for reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, with 35% of the total case having been made permanent in 2019) it could reduce emissions by nearly 200 million tonnes of CO₂.

To facilitate governance around the Moratorium, an "Indicator Map of the Termination of the Granting of New Permits" (i.e. "Moratorium map" or PIPBIB) was established. As mentioned in the ESA (cf. Annex XIII (i), the


---

REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

The government has also helped to promote increased conservation among minorities, information sharing from the central to local levels (as elements of monitoring, forest management, and permit issuance needed improvements). The August 2019 Presidential Instruction provided even greater details around the responsibilities of the various governmental institutions. The REDD-plus RBP Fund is expected to be operational in mid-2020, about six months after coordination with other ministerial and non-ministerial government institutions.

The moratorium is an opportunity for Indonesia to improve its forest governance. It is further supported through other commitments such as the Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan, forest management and law enforcement, such as Forest Management Units and the Social Forestry Programme.


| Pest Restoration Agency (BRG) |
| --- |

The government has established the Pest Restoration Agency (BRG). BRG aims to restore 2.4 million hectares of peatland by 2030 in seven priority provinces (Riau, Jambi, South Sumatra [Sumatera], West Kalimantan, Central Kalimantan, East Kalimantan and North Kalimantan). The BRG is a government agency that reports to the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The BRG estimates that more than 6.7 million hectares of peatlands are degraded and have the potential for restoration.

The BRG's priority is to restore peatlands burned in 2015, the high-carbon stock peat dome areas, and peatland areas with a high risk of fire. The BRG will work closely with local communities to develop community economy, plan for reforestation and mapping of the Peat Hydrological Unit, build demonstrations for integrated land use management, and conduct awareness campaigns on forest conservation.

The BRG has already restored 105 hectares in 2019. The government plans to restore another 4 million hectares by 2030.


| Land and forest restoration |
| --- |

In the National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN) 2015-2019

The RPJMN 2015-2019 targets in terms of land and forest restoration aimed to reach 5.5 million hectares by 2018, and 7.3 million hectares by 2024.

In the National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN) 2020-2024

The RPJMN 2020-2024 is still under formulation, but the latest draft targets to restore another 15.3 million hectares by 2024.


| Land reform and Social Forestry Programme |
| --- |

One of the main components of President Joko Widodo “Just Ecology” policy is an ambitious land reform programme that aims to transfer over 27.9 million hectares of land to communities (about 12% of the nation’s land area), of which 16.8 million hectares are on forest land. The land reform programme will be implemented in three phases, with the first phase targeting 300,000 hectares and the second phase targeting another 5 million hectares of land; and the Social Forestry programme, targeting 12.7 million hectares of forests, for establishment of community-based forest management.

The Agrarian reform programme involves the distribution of land and formalization of land ownership, benefiting landless farmers or farmers with small landholdings, while the Social Forestry programme grants local communities access to forest resources.


| The social forestry (perhutani sosial) programme |
| --- |

This programme targets the adjudication, demarcation and transfer of 12.7 million hectares of degraded forest land (i.e., 10% of the total State forest land*) to communities for sustainable forest management by 2019. It will also develop a legal framework that supports community-based forest management, including private and community ownership in managing lands and forests, and to reduce the pressure to convert old-growth forests.

The programme is expected to benefit over 37 million people (representing about 8% of Indonesia’s population) and 9.2 million households, of which approximately 1.7 million are classified as poor.


| Forest Estate in Indonesia |
| --- |

The Forest Estate in Indonesia has three sub-classifications: production forest, conservation forest, and protected forest.

# REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 18 OF 40)

Social forestry in Indonesia represents a significant shift in the role given to communities in forest management, from none officially prior to 1996, to benefitting from a range of regulations that support the role of communities in forest management. This has been achieved through the development and implementation of policies on community forest resources and forest land. In addition to the central government, the civil society is also playing a significant role in supporting this process by providing technical assistance and advocacy for the policy environment and the actual implementation of this programme. Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) are complemented by local universities and research institutions, local governments as well as private sector.

The proposed REDD+plus RBP aims to support the development of community forest management systems in Indonesia supporting the development of economic activities compatible with the existing forest cover and land status. As such, it is important that communities have access to their forest resources and are able to manage them according to policies and regulations that the government has issued to improve community access rights to the forest land and forest resources.

The REDD+plus RBP aims to support the development of community forest management systems in Indonesia supporting the development of economic activities compatible with the existing forest cover and land status. As such, it is important that communities have access to their forest resources and are able to manage them according to policies and regulations that the government has issued to improve community access rights to the forest land and forest resources.

### 1. Community forestry (Hutan Konservasi Masyarakat - HKM) provides community groups with access to production forests on private land (i.e., non-custodial forest areas) for timber and non-timber forest products, environmental services, medicinal plants, and other uses.

### 2. Village forestry (Hutan Desa - HD) provides villages with access to production and protection forests on public land (i.e., state forest areas).

### 3. Community forest plantations (Hutan Tanaman Rakyat - HTR) provides individuals or cooperatives with access to production forests on private land (i.e., non-custodial forest areas) for timber and NTFPs under the scheme of individual tenure rights (imber and NTFPs). Under this scheme HRIs may request up to 15 hectares and cooperate in managing the forest.

### 4. Customary forests (Hutan Adat - HA) are managed by customary law communities (masarakat hukum adat) under customary land tenure systems. These forests provide access for production of timber and NTFPs, environmental services, medicinal plants, and other uses on private land (i.e., non-custodial forest areas).

### 5. Forest management units (Hutan Manajemen - HM) are community-managed forests, organized in community groups or cooperatives, to access production forest under concession and in specific area between the state and communities.

Capacity strengthening is provided to produce timber and NTFPs, environmental services, medicinal plants, and other uses on private land (i.e., non-custodial forest areas).

### 6. Utilization Permit for Social Forestry (Izin Penanaman Hutan Perubahan Sosial - IPHPS) provides communities with access to production forests under concession for timber and NTFPs production and protection forests for timber and NTFPs production, and environmental services.

In the case of HM, HD, and HTR, social forestry is formalized through permits providing usufruct and management rights over forest resources. These permits are issued by the government (i.e., provincial and public forest entities) and communities in the case of kamitan. For customary forests, lands are ceded to their custodians under a land concession agreement.

### Forest Management Units

In the objective to improve the status and sustainable management of open access forest areas, and curb illegal logging, Indonesia has also established Forest Management Units (Konsesi Pengelolaan Hutan - KPH). KPH is used in English in all provinces of the country. The KPHs are the basic unit for government administration of forests and their management. They are designed to be a partnership between the government and communities. These units are managed by local governments, with close coordination and involvement from communities. KPHs are designed to be part of provincial government and to manage forests for their functional purposes (e.g., timber production, environmental services) in accordance with the principles of sustainable forest management. The KPH is a partnership between the government and communities. It aims to: (i) introduce locally based, professional and publicly accountable forest management units; (ii) provide access to forest resources for local communities through the establishment of permanent forest areas while managing potential conflicts and (iii) define territorial units appropriate for efficient management of forests by providing access to forest resources for local communities and use by adopting participatory forest land zoning with planning on the subdistrict spatial plans, providing on-site management of forests, and being responsive to local needs, interests and claims.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 1 OF 80)

The FMMs have clear economic, social and ecological management objectives. Unless management has been handed over to private concession holders or local communities, FMMs are responsible for (i) to create inventories of forest resources (e.g., species composition, age structure, volume, etc.), (ii) to implement fire prevention in accordance with forest land status (i.e., conservation, production/forestry production), and (iv) ensure the sustainable management of forests. FMMs are responsible for the following functions: (a) monitor and evaluate them; and provide technical assistance and capacity building. FMMs can mobilize resources to finance their activities through a combination of public and private funding sources. They will be responsible for sustainable forest management and establish their own income by selling forest products or environmental services, including carbon credits. The FMMs are also responsible for the implementation of fire prevention and the sustainable management of the forests they oversee. These annual long-and-term management plans and related activities will be carried out in close cooperation with local communities and private sector actors and the private sector. The range of activities will depend on the type of FMM and the various forest functions (e.g., conservation, production/forestry production). The roles and responsibilities of the different stakeholders and their respective roles and responsibilities at district, regional and central government are defined as follows:

* Central Government: Design and propose the establishment and zoning of Protection and Production Forest Management Units including the technical and institutional set up if one FMM is located in one district.
* District Government: Design and propose the establishment and zoning of Protection and Production FMUs including the technical and institutional set up if one FMM is located in one district.

The Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJM) 2015-2019 aimed to complete 100% of forest declaration, establish and zone 300,000 hectares of Production Forest Management Units (FMMs), and 400,000 hectares of Conservation Forest Management Units (FMMs) in the country. The FMMs are established by local communities (including customary holdings). Though the implementation of social forestry has been strongly accelerated, only a few FMUs have been officially established nationwide as of August 2018.

* Provincial government: Design and propose the establishment and zoning of Protection and Production Forest Management Units including the technical and institutional set up if one FMM is located in one district.
* District Government: Design and propose the establishment and zoning of Protection and Production FMUs including the technical and institutional set up if one FMM is located in one district.

Institutional models such as FMMs enable the development of effective collaborative partnerships with local stakeholders, including Adat (customary) communities, to sustainably manage the forests and address issues of access to forest resources. These programmes are also part of the national strategy for biodiversity. There are therefore powerful synergies to be made between the social forestry and FMU programmes.

## Use of Proceeds

Based on elements provided above, the Government of Indonesia wishes to use the REDD+ results-based approach (RBA) funding provided by the GCF to:

1. Continue updating, building and strengthening its REDD+ architecture, as well as further operationalizing it.
2. Further extend and enhance Social Forestry and Forest Management Units (FMU) operations in order to achieve the dual objective of sustainable forest management and rehabilitation, as well as community empowerment.

Consequently, both social forestry and FMU are also part of the focus areas through which the monstrosity seeks to be addressed by REDD+, contributing to addressing the rate of deforestation and forest degradation and meeting REDD+ objectives.

## Project Management

### 3.1: Project Management

#### Output 1: Strengthening REDD+ coordination and implementation and overall REDD+ architecture

This output aims to:
- Support the continuous updating and further development of the overall REDD+ architecture (Forest Reference Level, National Forest Monitoring System and MRV capacity, Safeguards Information System).
- Strengthen the government capacity for REDD+ coordination and implementation at national and subnational level.
This output will use a conventional up-front financing modality.

**Activity 1.1: Update and further develop the REDD+ architecture**

As continuous updating of the national REDD+ architecture in Indonesia is committed to continuously update and improve its REDD+ architecture and related reporting to the UNFCCC, this activity will be implemented in parallel with existing or future projects at national and subnational level.

This activity is foreseen to include:
- Update and improve the national FRL and allocate it to Provinces, and develop a web-based application for data entry, MRV development and REDD+ Performance calculation at national and subnational level.
- Further develop NFRM.
- Develop and operationalize a spatial monitoring system for emissions from land (SIMONELA).
- Improve the existing provincial reporting system linked to the FRL and develop a subnational REDD+ reporting system (local to Province).
- Update and operationalize REDD+ Implementation Strategy and contribute to updating or developing them at subnational level.
- Improve and operationalize the SIS at national and subnational level.
- Review and update the NFRM.
- Develop Benefit sharing plans.
- Contribute to meeting requirements under the UNFCCC (i.e., BUR, SOL etc).

**Activity 1.2: Strengthen capacity for REDD+ implementation**

In parallel to the update and further development of the national architecture for REDD+, this project will support capacity building in order to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge to effectively contribute to REDD+ at national and subnational levels (in particular those related to the above activities).

This activity is foreseen to include actions such as:
- Build government capacity on REDD+ at the provincial level.
- Enhance coordination among existing networks and associated guidelines for key policies and programs contributing to REDD+ at national and subnational levels (in particular those related to the above activities).
- Contribute to capacity BPDH capacity strengthening in relation to REDD+ (including GCF accessions).
- Strengthen the capacity to monitor and coordinate REDD+ implementation at the national level.
- Support acceleration measures for the Social Forestry programme and TMUs and their link with REDD+.
- Implementation of the Stakeholder Consultation Plan, the Gender Action Plan and the Environmental and Social Management Plan for the sale of proceeds.

### Output 2: Support to decentralized sustainable forest governance

This output aims to support the government of Indonesia in advancing its objectives of decentralized sustainable forest governance, as well as its complementary objective of developing access to and management of forest land and resources to communities. This will be achieved through the establishment and operationalization of Forest Management Units (FMUs), typically as small “clusters” in much wider FMU areas (as these cover the overall forest estate), where communities are granted formal rights over forest land and resources, including timber harvesting rights.

This project will support:
(i) activities related to operationalization of FMUs and licensing of social forestry respectively; and
(ii) actual investments supporting sustainable forest management and sustainable livelihoods for communities in FMU areas. These investments will be made possible through Activity 1.2, including the guidance developed and enhanced regulatory frameworks at national and subnational levels, as well as financial mechanisms that are deployed to support FMUs. This could use a performance-based payments modality.

#### Activity 2.1: Support the establishment and operationalization of Forest Management Units (FMUs) in areas covered by the Forestry Law No.4/1999

While FMUs have been established under the Forestry Law No.4/1999 in all provinces of the country, covering nearly the entire forest estate, much remains to be done in terms of operationalizing them. While different approaches are being used across Indonesia for the establishment and operationalization of FMUs, operationalization requires a set of complementary interventions, likely to include supporting:
- Awareness raising, capacity building and technical assistance to the FMU staff, provincial and district authorities as well as local communities;
- The development of information systems, management and monitoring systems; 
- The creation and strengthening (as relevant) of multi-stakeholder platforms to ensure participation in decision-making processes and to support the implementation of sustainable forest management processes. This will build as much as possible on existing structures to avoid the fragmentation of capacity.
- The participatory development of long-term and annual sustainable forest management plans;
- The establishment of a monitoring system for the performance of FMUs, including their impact over the long-term; 
- The preparation of site design document and Water Utilization Area Maps, for FMU conservation areas.  
- The participatory development of community sustainable development plans, supporting sustainable forest management.

### Activity 2.2 Expand and enhance implementation of the Social Forestry Programme

In moving forward with the social forestry programme, the Government of Indonesia’s first priority is to continue to assist Adat and Non-Adat communities, villages, individuals to obtain licenses available through the six different mechanisms (see Table 1). The second priority is to ensure that permit or right holders are able to improve their livelihoods through the implementation of social forestry activities. This includes planting trees and other plants and related activities to addressing threats of deforestation and barriers to carbon enhancement.

The Ministry of Environment and Forestry established Social Foresty Acceleration Working Group, comprising Ministry officials, representatives from relevant government agencies, and civil society organizations further support these working groups; multi-stakeholder platforms with distinct gender and social group representation will be established or strengthened. These platforms will include representatives from various other sectors, socio-cultural, biological, economic contexts, as well as support socialization for the programme.

Support to local communities is provided through the development of Social Forestry Action Plans (SFAPs). These plans are key for the central government to allocate and distribute funds for development. In the case of the social forestry programme, SFAPs will be developed by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry for conduct verifications, prepare and implement work plans as well as monitor implementation. Therefore, additional support is required in terms of capacity building and technical assistance for the preparation of various social forestry schemes. These plans will be further supported by developing and implementing comprehensive action plans for each SFAP.

As stated above, customary tenure or land Adat, as a means to recognize customary territory and second land and forest resource rights to Adat communities. BPDUN is under the Ministry of Finance, will be responsible and account for the day-to-day management of BPDUN.

BPDUN is an official entity, fully recognized by the Ministry of Finance. Its establishment is enacted through Decree No. 10/2019 dated January 31, 2019, which is in accordance with Article 45 and Article 68 of Regulation of Minister of Finance No. 137/PMK/01/2019. Capacity assessment of BPDUN is completed with full capacity rating.

The HACT framework is part of UNDP's normal risk-based management approach as per internal policies and procedures. The common objective of the HACT framework is to support a clear alignment of development projects with UNDP’s strategic objectives, including the achievement of results in line with the UNDP Strategic Plan 2018-2021.

The HACT framework consists of a set of procedures on requesting, disbursing, providing assurance and reporting on funds to effectively manage risks. The HACT framework is based on the principle that all projects are subject to risk management. In such, UNDP will apply an integrated suite of assurance measures associated with the “moderate” risk level assigned to BPDUN.

### Spot checks
- These will be conducted at least twice per year instead of at least once for a “low” risk level.
- These spot checks are also an opportunity to review the risk level (upward or downward) depending on the results obtained from these checks.

### Internal control audits
- These will be conducted in the second and fourth years of the programme cycle, respectively.

In addition to the above, UNDP intends to provide certain execution support services to BPDUN which will be set out in a country office support arrangement and for which specific provision has been made in the project budget. The arrangements will include capacity building on risk management and internal audit capacity of Accredited Entity under the terms of the AMA/FAA), will be coordinated and agreed with Ministry of Finance, and will be implemented by UNDP’s Country Office.

UNDP is also working with BPDUN in synergy with other partners to address the recommendations in the HACT assessment. It is expected that as a result of these efforts, it will be possible for UNDP to conclude that the risk-based HACT assessment will be implemented, in accordance with UNDP requirements, prior to the start of the Project Activities.

## Co-Executing Entity

**MOEF is not a Co-Executing Entity. No other Co-Executing Entity is envisaged at this stage.**

The implementing partner may enter into agreements with other organizations or entities, known as Responsible Parties, on behalf of UNDP and/or on its own behalf to carry out the Project on behalf of the Implementing Partner. Responsible Parties are accountable directly to the Implementing Partner.

### Responsible Party Selection
- Any Responsible party may be selected as per UNDP rules and regulations, which include performing a risk assessment in accordance with the HACT framework.
- UNDP HACT policies, to determine the level or risk and capacities to manage the funds of the Project. Parties considered for selection must have the capacity to perform their tasks under the terms of the agreement.

### Additional Requirements
- For each applicable task, the parties define any additional measures to ensure that tasks can be carried out in a manner consistent with UNDP policies.
- The parties may also agree on the use of an external consultant or service provider. This will be through an action plan, as annexed to the project document or through minutes of a design meeting or workshop.

### Risk Categorization
- UNDP HACT, in accordance with the HACT macro-level, still valid, resulting in a “low” risk categorization with no gap identified.

Additionally, UNDP assures that its partners are screened against UN Sanctions and Eligibility through a UN Security Council Resolution 1373 (2001) Screening Tool. This tool is used to determine whether the partner has access to the United Nations Global Marketplace in order to verify if any supplier has been involved in terrorism and sanctions violations. The screening process also includes a review of the partner’s compliance with the Risk Assessment Tool is applied before any agreement is made. This tool includes the following exclusionary criteria:
- Controversial weapons or their components;
- Weapons of mass destruction or their components, including military supplies;
- Replica weapons;
- Tobacco products;
- Violations of UN sanctions, UN illegal listings or UN vendor sanctions list;
- Pornographic materials;
- Substances subject to international bans or phase-outs, and wildlife or products regulated under the CITES;
- Gambling (excluding lotteries with charitable objectives).
