The Project Board (PB) will be composed of GRUNDP, the Minister of Finance, GCF as well as a civil society representative. As Executive Director, the Minister of Finance will chair the PB and organize its meetings at least twice a year or upon request of either of the Parties. The Ministry of Finance will also provide the secretariat for the PB. The Project Board will be responsible at the highest level for providing guidance on the management and technical feasibility of the project. The Project Board will meet at least once per quarter, but may convene more frequently as required.

The responsibilities in project management will be complemented by inputs and recommendations from the Technical Committee (see below). The PB will appoint an Executive Director and appoint and responsibilities of a Project Manager who will be responsible for the daily project execution.


The composition of the Project Board must include the following roles:

1) Executive: The individual who represents ownership of the project and who will chair the Project Board. This role is typically filled by the Minister of Finance, but may also be held by the NDO or some senior staff member of the Ministry of Finance. The Executive is ultimately responsible for the project, including its overall direction, strategy, and success. The Executive's responsibilities include ensuring that the project is focused throughout its life cycle on achieving its objectives and delivering outputs that will contribute to the achievement of the project's goals. This includes managing resources such as time, money, ensuring cost-conscious approaches to the project, balancing the demands of beneficiary and supplier interests, and ensuring that the project is completed within budget.

2) Beneficiary Representative: The Beneficiary Representative’s primary function within the Board is to ensure the realization of project results from the perspective of project beneficiaries. The Beneficiary Representative will be responsible for providing input on the project's direction and strategy, as well as being responsible for validating the needs and for monitoring that the solution will meet those needs within the constraints set by the PB. The Beneficiary representative must represent non-project targets against their quality Charter.

Specific responsibilities of the Beneficiary Representative as part of the above responsibilities for the Project Board include:

- Prioritize and contribute beneficial opinions on Project Board decisions on whether to implement recommendations on proposed changes.

### Project Assurance
UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance regime involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional level, as well as independent external auditors who map into the Project Board by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management and technical implementation of the project. The Project Board is responsible for Project Management function; the Project Board cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to the Project Manager or other staff. UNDP will provide a Project Assurance Officer (PAO) who reports directly to an Accredited Entity to the GCF. UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance services in three areas:
- (1) Day-to-day oversight supervision;
- (2) Oversight of project completion; 
- (3) Oversight of Project reporting.

#### National Project Director (NPD)
The Project Director will be a senior staff member of the Ministry of Finance and will be responsible at the highest level for providing guidance on the management and technical feasibility of the project. The NPD is responsible to the Project Board for ensuring that the Project Manager has the authority, responsibility for advising and advising the Project Manager on Government policy and norms. The NPD will be responsible for approving all major decisions in relation to project design, implementation, risk plans, pricing and procurement processes. The NPD will also approve procurement of services and goods contracts with a value exceeding $100,000. The NPD will also be responsible for approving all major procurement and hiring requests and payments. The Combined Delivery Report (CDR) will be approved on a quarterly basis and signed by the NPD.

#### Technical Committee
A Technical Committee already exists under UNDP. Meetings will be arranged when there is need of technical inputs from outside UNDP to provide advice or guidance in areas related to this project. The aim is to provide technical support to the Project Board, Project National Director, Project Technical Experts and Project Manager for decision making.

National leadership
The Ministry of Environment will provide the space and facilities for the Project staff, with the PMI located within the newly established Environmental Fund Management Agency (BPDHL), under DG Treasury.

This is indeed particularly strategic for coordination and integration as they are foreseeing to play a key role in supporting REDD+ implementation, and in further mainstreaming and strengthening REDD+ within Indonesian public institutions and processes.

BPDHL has been recently established formally following a very comprehensive preparatory process over several years. The establishment of BPDHL as an independent agency with its own legal status enables government public service units to provide their product and/or for services for non-profit purposes with greater autonomy, while at the same time ensuring that the quality of these products and services is maintained. BPDHL includes several funding windows (and sub-windows), one of which for climate change under which REDD+ funds will be provided. The establishment of BPDHL as an independent agency has been a major milestone in Indonesia for both international and domestic finance. This will enable a much more coordinated and synergistic approach to the implementation of REDD+ projects, while also providing greater transparency and accountability to its constituencies as well as towards contributing donors.

As such, BPDHL is a key instrument to ensure national leadership in the coordination of climate finance and climate change policy, and to provide a strong institutional framework for the effective and efficient capacity building of the Fund on REDD+ and to contribute to the process of its accreditation to the GCF for

## Key Information
- **Document**: REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal (Page 13 of 40)

### Direct Beneficiaries
Key institutional bodies of BPDHL include:
- A Steering Committee
- A Custodian Bank
- The BPDHL Secretariat (Fund Manager), along with the various (v) Line Ministries responsible for executing the project activities and providing inputs to the project through the BPDHL. These various bodies and BPDHL Team will closely follow and contribute to the progress of the project.

### Project Implementation
The REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal is designed to support the implementation of a REDD-plus project in Indonesia, with specific focus on the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF). The project will be implemented by the UNDP Country Office. During the implementation phase, all procurement activities will be conducted in accordance with UNDP procedures and policies.

### Property of Equipment and Goods
Goods and equipment purchased as part of this project will initially belong to the UNDP Country Office. During the implementation phase, these assets will be maintained in accordance with UNDP procedures and policies, subject to prior agreement with the Ministry of Finance. The goods and equipment will be transferred with a delivery document.

### Audit
Financial reporting and auditing standards for the programme will follow international financial reporting and auditing standards. UNDP will provide technical assistance to UNDP's general corporate audit regulations, internal and external audits that can be carried out and these costs will be covered by the project. The audit will be performed in accordance to UNDP's General Corporate Audit Regulations (GCAR) and the National Implementation Monitoring (NIM) implemented project on UNDP and GCF projects. UNDP will be responsible for making audit arrangements with an independent auditor, who will provide audit management responses and the Project Manager and project support team will address audit recommendations.

### Learning and Knowledge-sharing
Results from the project will be disseminated within and beyond the project intervention zone through existing information dissemination channels. The project will identify and participate as, relevant and appropriate in scientific, policy-based or other any networks, which may be of benefit to project implementation though lessons learned and knowledge gained during the course of this project can be used for future design and implementation of similar future projects. There will also be a two-way flow of information between this project and other UNDP and GCF funded projects.

### Communications and Visibility Requirements
The project will comply with UNDP's, the Ministry of Finance and GCF Biodiversity Guidelines. Amongst other requirements, the project will ensure that how the UNDP and the logos of donors to UNDP projects are used. In order to accord proper acknowledgment to the GCF for providing funding, a GCF logo will appear on all communications materials and publications emanating from this project. Any citation on publications stemming from the project will also accord proper acknowledgment to the GCF.

### Financing Modalities
Outputs 1 (creating environment) and 3 (project management) will use a conventional National Implementation Modality (NIM) for timely implementation of the activities required to enhance its overall architecture and capacity for overall REDD+ implementation, as well as to ensure high quality project management and implementation in line with UNDP's Performance-Based Payments (PBP) financing modality.

For Output 2 (FMU and Social Foresty programmes), the Government of Indonesia and UNDP opted for the use of UNDP's "Performance-Based Payments" (PBP) financing modality.
- Ensure country leadership by providing more flexibility to the Government of Indonesia in the way it presents the desired results
- Enhance project management by making optimal use of existing government structures, avoiding or keeping the duplications of structures and functions to the minimum, while ensuring that UNDP can fulfill its role as a facilitator and partner for the Government of Indonesia (e.g. gender)
- Enable faster disbursements from UNDP in Indonesia than a conventional upfront payment modality would allow, depending on the government's capacity to provide the agreed results, verified

# REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) (PAGE 54 OF 80)

## Through an Independent Assessor, without compromising the quality of implementation (incl. safeguards) and the intended use of proceeds.

### Performance-based payments (PBPs) are a type of agreement between UNDP and a development partner to provide financial incentives for the achievement of agreed development results.
No advances are provided; rather payments are made only upon the verified achievement of agreed results. This approach is described in the UNDP Policies and Procedures - P&PQ.

### The modality supports service delivery by the NMR (Nationally designated entity responsible for the achievement of the results), and to use its approaches, methods, capacities and resources to achieve the agreed development results. It also provides a framework for the implementation agreement.
Upon achievement of the results, the development partner submits substantive and other reporting requirements to UNDP (UNDP P&PQ).

### This modality requires support services from the NMR. Usually, UNDP has to sign and manage the performance-based payments agreement and the various tasks attached to it. The UNDP Policy on the PBP modality and the template for the PBP Agreement are publicly available in full at UNDP P&PQ through the webinars provided in this footnote.

#### Figure 7: Financing modalities used in the project

| Traditional incentive-based modality (Volumes I & II) | Performance-based payments modality (Stage 2) |
|---|---|
| **A. Government and UNDP agree on the performance criteria and indicators, targets and prices per unit of result:**<br>Government & UNDP select an independent assessor; source elements from step 1 and define a validation methodology.<br>A preliminary agreement is signed by both parties as described in step I & 2.<br>A Project document is signed, as well as the Performance-based payment Agreement;<br>Data are collected from UNDP to be – in this case – the Funding Entity (i.e., implementing partner), based on the achievement of one or more outcomes verified by the independent assessor (including safeguards). | **B. Independent Assessor:**<br>Conducts a validation of the project performance against agreed criteria and indicators, targets and prices per unit of result.<br>Submits a report to UNDP with recommendations for payment.<br><br>**C. Government and UNDP agree on the payment amount:**<br>Government & UNDP review the independent assessor’s report and decide whether or not to make a payment.<br>If the assessment is positive, the government makes a payment to the implementing partner (e.g., a project entity). |

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 58 OF 60)

Livelihood and mitigate natural disasters like floods and landslides while the Social Forestry programmes has important implications for community engagement and involvement in protecting the REDD+ investments.

With the project investing in social forestry and establishing and renewing commitment to Forest Management Units (FMUs), it will be possible to achieve a number of co-benefits. These include: poverty alleviation, conservation, rehabilitation and economic and socio-cultural activities that can be complementarily implemented in addition to the REDD+ investments. The project will also contribute towards sustainable forest management.

With social forestry, access to land leads to increase in income of households and contribute to poverty alleviation.

Studies have suggested that achieving low-carbon economic development in Indonesia may have important co-benefits. For example, Social Forestry Programmes can provide livelihood to millions of community members living on forests (Nel 2014; Economic Synergy 2014).

The non-carbon benefits associated with implementation of REDD+ activities can be grouped into environmental benefits: hydro-regulation and biodiversity; social benefits: poverty alleviation, gender empowerment and education; and economic benefits. The main non-carbon benefits are presented below, and more details can be found in the Annex XIII (H).

### Hydro-regulation

The nature and value of regulating services provided by forests are dependent on the characteristics of the forest ecosystem and its surrounding environment. These include: water regulation, soil conservation, flood control, erosion control, sedimentation reduction, climate regulation, carbon sequestration and storage, and biodiversity conservation.

UNCCD (United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification) and UNCED (United Nations Conference on Environment and Development) have identified the following five forest ecosystem services as vital for human well-being: water regulation, soil conservation, flood control, erosion control, sedimentation reduction. The 2014 World Forest Ecosystem valuation study shows that one hectare of forest provides soil erosion equivalent to 6358 kilograms per year and sedimentation equivalent to 795 kilograms per year.

The project will avoid approximately USD 30 per hectare of forest in a year. This avoided cost provides a significant argument in favor of the importance of forests in forest protection, as failing to do so will result soil quality and contribute reduce agricultural yields.

### Biodiversity

Indonesia's forests index provide economic, social, cultural, and environmental benefits for the country (Forest Economics Fund 2014). These include: timber production; food security; health and livelihoods; providing clean water; timber; medicinal plants and other important services. Biodiversity also enhances ecosystem resilience to climate change and provides opportunities for climate change mitigation. The project activities will directly support biodiversity conservation through improved sustainable forest management practices, including the establishment of FMUs.

Species diversity has been reported in the 4th National Biodiversity Report, especially the number of plant species that positions Indonesia in the world big five. Of this species, 50% are endemic plants. In the diversity of fungi, Indonesia is also ranked as one of the top ten countries with approximately 12,783 species (Biodiversity LIP, 2014) showed that the number of documented species diversity consisted of 1,500 species of algae, 690 species of bryophytes, 1,000 species of ferns and pteridophytes, 380 species of spermatophytes plant (20,000 – 40,000 species). These numbers contribute to 15.5% of total world flora, whereas the number of documented species of insects is approximately 67,000 species of amphibian and fish, invertebrates; 190 species of butterflies that contributes to 10 % of the world species.

### Social benefits - Integrating Sustainable Development (Co-benefits)

To effectively implement the REDD+ project, this project will contribute to a broader sustainable development agenda. Output 2 of the project with the implementation of decentralized sustainable forest management and community-based social forestry programmes will have benefits beyond reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in Indonesia.

The project activities will also provide co-benefits that will come from the project's support to the decentralization of forest management, including:

- Across Indonesia, more than 74 percent of the poor depend on ecosystem services for their basic livelihoods. Decentralizing forest management has thus, have dramatic effects on the lives and livelihoods of these people.
- The national inequality gap: For instance, in East Nusa Tenggara, bearing in mind that 80 percent of the population live below poverty line, the project will provide ecosystem services for agriculture; which could particularly affect the rural poor within this province and reduce their resilience to any unexpected climate change impacts.


---
**Page 60**
---

REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

According to the World Bank, out of a population of around 364 million, about 25.9 million Indonesians still live below the poverty line. Based on March 2018 data, approximately 20.1% of the entire population remains vulnerable to food insecurity. The REDD-plus RBP funding proposal aims to support sustainable forest management of forest relies on communities and households to use the forest efficiently given the reliance on forests for livelihoods in Indonesia. This will help increase the capacity of local communities to manage the forests better. Against this setting, social forestry or community-based forestry has become an important feature of forest management in the region.

To address these issues, Indonesia is committed to increasing areas managed by communities from only 1% of the total forest area in Indonesia as at 2016 to 12.7 million ha (30%) between 2016 and 2025. This will increase the capacity of local communities to manage forests better, improve livelihoods, and recognized in economic cycle of Indonesia. The income increase expected from this project will be about $1.8 billion.

Gender empowerment: Due to insecure rights to land, forest and tree resources, discrimination has a bias towards providing services to males rather than females (Gurung and Gustawik 2009; Bandyopadhyay and Tuan 2013). This project will address this issue by increasing the capacity of women to adapt to climate change (The World Bank et al. 2009; Demetriades and Espolin 2010).

Indonesia has adopted a variety of policies to mainstream gender issues into broader policy considerations. For example, the National Action Plan on Gender Equality (PAGN) was launched in 2007. Other legislation and policy measures include the National Machinery for the Advancement of Women (Majelis Nasional Perempuan) Act No.13/2005, the Law No.4/2008 on the Elimination of Violence Against Women, and Law No 12/2003 under which each political party should consider appointing 30% of female candidates in general elections. The National Action Plan (PAGN) also includes a gender action plan for the implementation of loans. The National Development Plan (2005-2025), furthermore, identifies gender mainstreaming as a target. However, there are still many challenges to be addressed such as lack of awareness and understanding about sources of law in Indonesia, customary law and religious law which may sometimes detrimentally affect women.

The project includes a gender action plan that seeks to ensure activities implemented will use a gender-responsive approach. The project will also work with the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection and the Environmental and Social Assessment Unit in Aries III (b).

### D.1 Impact potential

Climate change is one of the main barriers to achieving the objectives of the Fund’s objectives and results.

With 93.5 million ha of forests in 2016, Indonesia is the country with the third largest tropical forest cover in the world, after Brazil and DR Congo. The UNFCCC technically assessed FREL ranges from 7.28 to 9.42 MtCO2eq for 2014 to 58.2 to 58.6MtCO2eq for 2030 (UNFCCC 2013). As mentioned in section II, it has already led to emission reductions of 7.28 MtCO2eq as a baseline and 9.42 MtCO2eq as the goal for 2030. The emission reductions from the implementation of the STRAHANs have been and will continue to be very significant.

It is important to note that the REDD-plus RBP funding proposal is not without its flaws for multiple reasons. From a conceptual standpoint, there is not always a direct and linear relationship between forest conservation and poverty reduction. This is because poverty alleviation can be achieved through series of interlinkages of different enabling policies (e.g., moratorium) and direct investment made in the field as well as through other policy measures that are not directly related to REDD-plus RBP funding proposal.

For example, implementation of the moratorium without accompanying measures could simply displace emissions outside of the areas it covers while canceling out the mitigation effects.

# STRANAS: A Multifaceted Initiative to Achieve Results at the National Scale

Indonesia uses many sources of financing, including its own budget, to implement it. With multiple partners supporting multiple activities and due to the fact that RED+ is a flexible mechanism, there are many different ways in which funding can be invested or to a specific actions/components. Reforest, each funding source have made a contribution attribution following UNFCCC guidance on RED+. The current GCF PMF which notes that indicator “4.1 Tones of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) reduced by 2030” will be reported at the national scale in line with UNFCCC requirements outlined in the Warsaw Framework.

As per the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework for RED+, the amount of emission reduction that Indonesia will achieve through RED+ activities is not required to be reported during the implementation period. During these processes (2020-2023), will be known once either the BUR with the RED+ technical annex or the Biennial Report on the performance framework for RED+ is published in 2024.

These results will be compared with FREE-L2. This information will be published on the Lima RED+ Information Hub at https://www.lima-redplus.org/red-plus-information-hub/

The UNFCCC Warsaw framework for RED+ does not require, nor provide a methodology for, attribution of emission reductions to a specific measure or action or donor. Furthermore, attribution of national emission reductions to RED+ activities is not required under the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework. The risk of displacement associated with any sub-national implementation of RED+ in Indonesia avoids this risk by implementing RED+ at the national scale, instead of through isolated sub-national projects.

## D.2: Paradigm shift potentials

### Describe the design into which the RED+ plus activity category impact beyond a one-program perspective

The successful implementation of policies and measures for RED+ identified in STRANAS or stemming from it must be accompanied by a paradigm shift at all levels, including national, sub-national, and local. This will be achieved by building confidence in UNFCCC processes through the demonstration that Indonesia’s completion of the requirements under the RED+ framework is not only possible but also feasible. The implementation of RED+ results-based payments. This is an essential signal to policy makers and their constituencies that pursuing RED+ is a win-win proposition for all parties involved.

In addition, the successful implementation of RED+ in Indonesia will provide a strong demonstration effect, enfraining financial inflows that incentives and further support that shift towards the emissions-development nexus. In addition, it will also demonstrate to other countries in the region and beyond that Indonesia can meet UNFCCC, which would represent a strong signal that this is feasible also for countries from other regions, and in particular those with similar socio-economic conditions.

The implementation of RED+ activities in Indonesia contributed to developing an approach that could be replicated in many other countries currently engaged in RED+ around the world. Indeed, to build confidence that UNFCCC processes are not only possible but also feasible, it is essential for (1) developing countries to gain confidence that they can meet the requirements of the UNFCC process in order to rapidly obtain and use RED+, and (2) for the international community to gain confidence in


---
**Page 62**
---

quality of funds coming through the UNFCCC process through REDD+ implementation (including the Warsaw Framework for REDD+).

At a country and territorial level, Indonesia's implementation of STRANAS has already directly contributed to a positive impact on forest governance and sustainable land use planning. The STRANAS approach is designed to settle an ambitious forest-sector target in the NDC. Further implementation of STRANAS, including through the REDD+ mechanism, will help Indonesia on its pathway towards a decarbonized economy, while enhancing community and biodiversity co-benefits.

### D. Sustainable development potential

#### (a) Improved land use planning and management
The implementation of STRANAS contributed and will continue to contribute to create an enabling environment for sustainable land use planning and management at the national level. This includes the identification of forest lands, and by including social-economic and environmental co-benefits into the proposed paradigm shift, such as promoting agroforestry systems, improving livelihoods, and enhancing biodiversity conservation on their ancestral and cultural heritage lands, of watersheds, etc. All the policies and measures prioritized in STRANAS are aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and other international commitments in the context of promoting sustainable development. As such, Indonesia will contribute to meeting the Sustainable Development Goals, particularly SDG 1 on poverty, SDG 5 on gender equality, SDG 13 on climate action and SDG 15 on life on land.

STRANAS does highlight the important attribute to "the creation of additional benefits along with reduced emissions from deforestation and forest degradation" (improved livelihoods), (b) improved preservation of biodiversity and (c) improved protection for other ecosystem services".

As mentioned in section C.2.1, The Social Forestry programme and Forest Management Units, two flagship government programmes under STRANAS, have been implemented to improve the management of land and natural resources has been managed in Indonesia, and particularly in the role given to communities in managing these resources. These programmes aim at improving the sustainable use of forest and natural resources to better take local circumstances into account, (i) the importance of multi-stakeholder participation in the management of forests and other natural resources, (ii) the equitable access to forest and natural resources, to prevent or avoid conflicts across sectors and users, (iii) the importance of supporting viable livelihoods for communities living around forests and other natural resources, and (iv) the sustainable use of these resources. The social forestry programme, very complementary to the FMMI programme, goes a step further by focusing on the role of women in managing forest and other natural resources, including access to land, providing predictability and security over these resources and the terms of their use.

Activities implemented in these programmes, as more widely at policies and measures from STRANAS, have strong co-benefits for biodiversity conservation. These include improved soil fertility through carbon storage, but also soil preservation, water regulation, among others. In turn, these ecosystem services are key to sustainable livelihoods and food security. Forests provide timber and non-timber forest products in agriculture, which represents the main livelihood for a large part of the population and the second biggest source of income.

See also section C.2 on non-carbon benefits for a description of the nature, scale and importance of the project's wider benefits.

Thanks to a steady economic expansion and federal government policies, poverty rates and inequality are falling in Indonesia, and access to public services is broadening. The deficit risk is containing the growth of debt, but additional spending on infrastructure, health and social services is needed to meet Indonesia's development goals. As mentioned in section C.2.1, the Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJM) for 2015-2019 aimed to complete the process of establishing forest management units (FMUs) in all forests under state ownership and partnerships across 12.7 million hectares (including customary holdings). Though the implementation of social forestry has been slow, there are now 34 FMUs covering 8.6 million hectares (50% of the total area of social forestry by October 2019 or 9.3 million hectares remaining). D.E. Country ownership

Describes the beneficiary country ownership of, and capacity to implement a funded project or programme (goals, context, strategies etc).


The Ministry of Environment and Forestry is the national environment authority in charge of designing environmental policies and coordinating strategies, projects and projects for the conservation of ecosystems and their biodiversity, as well as for the sustainable use of natural resources. The ministry also ensures environmental quality, with development based on conservation and an appropriate use of biodiversity and resources. The Ministry is led by a Minister and supported by several Directorates. Climate Change is the UNFCCC Focal Point, as well as the authority on REDD+ in Indonesia.

In 2016 MOEF was managing a budget 5.95 Trillion Rp (around USD 450 million) with an execution rate of 82.1%.


E.D. Efficiency and effectiveness

Describes how efficient and effective the programme is.


REDD+ is implemented by developing countries, through policies and projects, not projects.

Assurance that mitigation results have indeed been achieved is a fundamental element for a results-based payment system. The REDD+ Technical Annex includes information on the national forest monitoring system, institutional roles, among other information.

Result-based payments are a new modality of climate finance through which resources are received ex-ante for mitigation results achieved in the past, like payments for environmental services. Developing countries within the UNFCCC have been working with the UNFCCC to develop methodologies and tools such as LULUCF experts exported by the UNFCCC. This evaluation process is conducted annually, following the procedures established by the UNFCCC. The results of this evaluation are then compiled in a report and reported on a Technical Annex to the BR. This Technical Annex also includes the REDD+ activities selected for payment, as well as information about the results, information on the national forest monitoring system, institutional roles, among other information.

The processes for assessing the FRLREs (decision 13C/CP.9) and for Measuring, Reporting and Verifying (MRV) of REDD+ activities (decision 20C/CP.10) are complex and require a high level of commitment at all levels of government. The processes also require a high level of confidence in the system, with a view to attract scaled-up investments. This approach assures the highest possible level of results, so that they can receive more payments/ financial resources, therefore efficient.

E. Compliance with GCF policies

E.1. For the period of the achieved results

E.1.1. The Environmental and social safeguards

The Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) report describing the details to which the measures undertaken to identify, assess, and manage environmental and social risks and impacts, in the context of the REDD-plus proposal, will be supported by the proceeds earned by them aligns with GCF policies for the activities that led to the results.

The ESA report annexed to the FBP describes the extent to which the measures undertaken to identify, assess, and manage environmental and social risks and impacts, in the context of the REDD-plus proposal, were consistent with the GCF Environmental and Social Standards (ES&S). The ESA found full consistency with the GCF ES&S standards (see Annex XIII for the full report).

The ESA concluded that the Social Forestry and Moratorium initiatives which contributed to the emission reduction achieved by the project were in line with the GCF ES&S. These initiatives included the setting of underlying RLRS and project measures and activities that were aligned with the principles and standards of the GCF ES&S.

Collectively, the two initiatives sought to protect forests and biodiversity from deforestation and degradation, respect local communities' rights over their land and resources, provide for their livelihoods, and empower their traditional institutions. This was done by directly prohibiting forest conversion in areas where communities had already established customary tenure systems or were seeking to do so; by systematically monitoring and enforcing the moratorium on forest conversion; by providing alternative livelihood opportunities from these and other REDD+ activities; as well as by resolving land tenure conflicts and other social issues that could have led to deforestation. As a result of these initiatives, greater areas of forests were protected and an enabling environment was fostered for forest communities to be included, rather than removed from the management of their resources.

It is further noted that in order to achieve this, the project introduced pure protection of forests and peatlands and recognizing local community rights to equitably benefit from and participate (or lead) in the conservation and sustainable use of these areas. The project also aimed at achieving the objectives and targets of these initiatives; particularly Social Forestry, were and are ambitious, more needs to be done for them to achieve their full potential. It is further noted that the project has made progress (including during the ESA review period) and observing that the continued multi-stakeholder support for both of these initiatives will be critical in order to ensure they continue to make significant contributions towards achieving the objectives set out by the GCF ES&S.

The RBP's components and activities were the subject of an ESMP providing an assessment of the social and environmental impacts. The ESMP was developed in accordance with the Green Climate Fund’s Social and Environmental Screening Process (SESP): (i) a consultative process realized to date with government parties, stakeholders, and analysis of the core REDD+ activities undertaken thus far; (ii) as well as recent interviews with key stakeholders.

As recommended by the ESMP, further impact assessment and management measures will be needed in order to manage risks. The ESMP was developed based on a preliminary environmental and social screening that conducted a participatory and comprehensive environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA). This will be carried out through an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) which will provide the basis for project management planning. In the way the findings and recommendations of the ESMP will be further clarified and focused on, in order to develop a more detailed ESMP. The ESMP will also serve as a reference document by the project and provide a way forward for future assessments if and as new activities are developed concerning REDD+ (such as those related to Social Forestry and forest management planning at the local level), overall governance, stakeholder engagement, and monitoring and reporting.

The content of the ESIA report can be found in the Annexure 2 to the ESMP (Annex VI b). It will be modified according to the findings of the ESMP.

The preliminary findings and conclusions of the annexed ESMP will be reviewed again based on the findings of the ESIA and as appropriate, modifications will be made to the final ESMP and its associated management plan. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan, a Gender Action Plan, a Cultural Heritage Management Plan, and an Action Plan is already included in the annex to this FIP. Indicative outlines for other plans are attached in the annexes to this FIP.

The ESIA consultants will be expected to continue their work on the ESMP and will also present management plans that practically assist in operationalizing and implementing the avoidance and mitigation measures (e.g., stakeholder engagement, land use planning, community participation, local participation, Adult community rights, grievance mechanisms, monitoring and reporting, etc.).

The ESMP also reviewed the existing national grievance redress mechanism (GRM) or GFRM hosted by the MOEF. The GRM is a formal process for addressing grievances of project-affected communities and other stakeholders. Based on these consultations, UNDP guidance on GRM, and the widely accepted eight “effective principles” for grievance mechanisms, the ESMP recommended that the existing GRM be reviewed and will complement and be linked to the national GRM, including its tracking system, its capacity to feed into the ESMP, and its ability to provide feedback to project stakeholders. The ESMP also recommended lessons learned.

The execution of the ESIA and the ESMP implementation will be led by the Ministry of France with the technical support of MOEF and overseen by UNDP. Compliance with UNDP’s requirements for activities that may cause adverse social and environmental impacts will proceed until the ESIA has been completed, and the ESMP is finalized and approved.

#### Continuity of stakeholder engagement throughout the life cycle of the RBP project is of utmost importance. This is mandated by law, consistent with Indonesia's STRANAS*, made possible based on:

- (i) the number of multi-stakeholder forums already in place to facilitate such engagements;
- (ii) the presence of a REDD+ Unit* which has been established at the national level and includes activities to local communities, Villages and Adat communities, as well as local governments (see Activity 2.1*) and for providing feedback on the project's progress; and
- (iii) the existence of an SIS-REDD system* that will provide a platform for stakeholder engagement and reporting mechanisms that will depend on stakeholder cooperation and participation (particularly the SIS-REDD+ system).

#### The STRANAS is a multi-stakeholder forum at the national level which has the express function of bringing stakeholders within forest management (specifically local communities, Villages and Adat communities) together.

In terms of participation, the Forestry Law provides that "(1) Community shall take part in the forestry development. (2) Government shall be obliged to encourage community participation through various effective means." The STRANAS is a multi-stakeholder forum at the national level which has been established under Indonesia's STRANAS*. It contains a section 2.2.5.1 entitled "Interaction and Strategies for Stakeholder Participation" and it provides for the establishment of an ad-hoc committee to coordinate stakeholder engagement activities, which will be most affected either positively or negatively.* In 2013, through a very participatory process (as described above), the STRANAS established the REDD+ Unit* as a multi-stakeholder forum at the national level for groups of persons or entity that is or is likely to be subject to the effects of the activities of the REDD+ Unit. Examples include local communities, villages and adat communities, forest users (including landowners), groups of persons or entities located in the neighborhood of the REDD+ Unit.

Consistent with the above, and as reported in section C 1.2., the STRANAS itself was developed in the context of a participatory process involving all stakeholders. The STRANAS is intended to be an effective forum that will be used to actively monitor social and environmental impacts so as to influence decision-makers about project activities and their effects on communities.

To further increase the information available to stakeholders, the SIS has been developed, apart from the SIS-REDD system*, no less than 10 portals/information systems that facilitate the sharing of information between the national government, provincial governments, district governments, and local communities. These are each updated in Indonesia's most recent Summary of Information (SOI).

At sub-national level, each of the eleven REDD+ pilot provinces have set up an Ad-Hoc REEDD Institution to coordinate stakeholder engagement activities. The Forest Observers Council is a part of the Task Force on the Commission. These ad-hoc institutions are intended to coexist with multi-stakeholder membership considering representation from local communities, villages and adat communities, including local NGOs, academic/university representatives.

Indonesia also has developed and promoted three types of spaces for dialogue, such as these: The Forest Observers Council, the National Forestry Council, and the Social Forestry Forum. These are all established under Article 75 of the Forestry Law. The National Forestry Council accommodates the existence of this institution.

## E2 Risk assessment

### E2.1 For the period of the achieved results

Please note that this document provides a framework that allows for an assessment of the historical performance of the activities undertaken and their track record against the risk tolerance levels specified in the Risk Appetite Statement (RAS) and/or the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

Please note that you should consider only the applicable and relevant parts of the two above documents to the framework.

Indonesia is presenting emission reductions resulting from a combination of policy and programmatic interventions set out in its REDD+ implementation (see Table 4 in Section C.2.1). While it is not possible to directly attribute these emissions reductions to the RSBP Project, they are consistent with Indonesia’s national policy, social, economic and environmental factors. Indonesia has referred to the Forest Moratorium policy as a key driver of emission reductions from deforestation and forest degradation. The moratorium was introduced in 2016 and is expected to reduce emissions by up to 35% (or approximately 478 million tonnes of CO2eq) over the period 2016-2020, compared with a business-as-usual scenario.

In the same token, the President has prioritized the Social Forest Programme as evidenced through a declaration in his State of the Nation Address on 5 September 2017. The President also established the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. It has, among others, introduced Social Forest Acceleration Working Groups at both national and provincial levels as well as facilitators to support activities on-the-ground.

To ensure that the social forest programme is in line with Indonesia’s National REDD+ Strategy and Social Forest indicative maps to ensure the protection of community and Adat rights in the planning of forest resources. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry has also established a map of customary land tenure and a map which took into account customary rights of Adat community. These maps will be used to address overlapping claims on land.

Government institutions have also been strengthened, for instance the Directorate General on Social Foresty and Environmental Partnership in 2015 and 2016 alone, resolved conflicts covering 2,775,010 ha of forest. Since 2014, the Ministry has established a system called SMART (System Monitoring and Assessment of Rights). This allows stakeholders to the complaints online for no cost, track the same, and view information about complaints.

Furthermore, Indonesia has established various instruments to ensure the performance of the activities undertaken under the RSBP Project. These include: (i) the establishment of a monitoring system called SMART System; SMART are some of the systems established to track performance of emission reduction initiatives. Indonesia also has established a national MRV system called One Map Policy. This initiative is also a priority under National Movement on Natural Resources Security (Gerakan Nasional Keamanan Sumber Daya Alam – GNPSA) administered by Anti-Compromise Commission (Komisi Pemberantasan Korupsi - KPK).

Moreover, transparency and performance assessment can also be done through SI-REDD and MOEF governance. These systems are expected to be operational in 2018 and 2019 respectively.

### Action points and next steps.
- As an action point, this project will contribute to key existing national programmes which enjoy high political support. These include the National Medium-Term Development Plans for 2015-2019 and 2020 - 2024, the NDCs up to its October 2019 revision, the STRANDS, the RAN-GIKI, and others.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 8 OF 10)

Indonesia has made significant strides in integrating gender equality and the protection of women's rights in national institutions, laws, and policies. The Government established the State Ministry for Women's Empowerment in 2017, which is responsible for coordinating and implementing gender mainstreaming at all levels within government, across sectors and at multiple levels. Indonesia also ratified CEDAW in 1984, establishing a requirement to integrate gender equality into national development plans.

Presidential Instruction No 9/2000 on Gender Mainstreaming in the National Development, The former minister of women's empowerment established the Women's Empowerment Agency (BPKP) as an independent institution responsible for implementing the gender equality and women's empowerment movement. BPKP is mandated to coordinate with all government agencies at national level and at sub-national level.

The Government also issued Presidential Instruction No 9/2000 on Gender Mainstreaming in the National Development, including the development policy on Improvement of Women's Lives 2010-2014 aimed at enhancing women's empowerment. The Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection, Ministry of Culture and the Ministry of Home Affairs Regulation No 15/2008 on Guidelines for gender mainstreaming in the region, which includes a requirement to integrate gender equality into all stages of planning and budgeting.

For a full treatment of Indonesian Legal and administrative frameworks in regard to gender, refer to the Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment section of this document.

In regards to the forestry sector in particular, MOEF has made efforts to incorporate gender into its activities including 1) the re-establishment of its Gender Working Group in 2012 and 2) the initiation of gender responsive budgeting. The Ministry also established a Gender Focal Point in 2013.

In addition, the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MOEF) has been working with the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection (MOWEP) to develop a joint action plan on gender mainstreaming. A MOU signed in 2016, the MOWEP also supports MOEF with its gender mainstreaming efforts, which include training for staff on gender equality, information dissemination, and gender responsive budgeting.

The Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MOEF) has established a Gender Working Group to support gender mainstreaming. The Group has limited authority to make any significant contribution to gender and organizational change, requiring improvements in the capacity of the group to effectively contribute to gender mainstreaming. The MOEF has developed Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming (d/31MenkaSet.Gen.Set/150207) which aims to help address gender gaps within the Ministry by providing a framework for identifying resources, funding and adequate infrastructure to support gender mainstreaming within its scope of work.

In addition, the MOF has a gender working group to support gender mainstreaming within its activities and projects. The MOEF also received an award in 2016 for its efforts on gender equality and women's empowerment (awarded by Anugrah Parikrama Ekayana) in 2013, 2015 and 2016.

The public consultation process for the National REDD+ Strategy included a series of meetings in seven regions across Indonesia. The meeting was attended by representatives from government, civil society, academia, and private sector, as well as postion on a website for public comment. The public consultation process received over 350 comments (about 12 percent), also signaling the need for more comprehensive gender justice focus in national forest management policies and programs.

The Gender Action Plan for REDD+ activities and the use of proceeds nationally provides a significant opportunity.

It should also be noted that from 2010 to 2013, the Government of Indonesia received support from the UN-REDD Programme to develop a gender action plan. The programme provided training on how to integrate gender into its efforts, including a training workshop delivered over two days by the Project Management Team (PMT) in Jakarta and Yogyakarta. The PMT also developed a set of indicators and determine relevant activities to be integrated in the Programme's work plan.

A workshop on Gender Mainstreaming was held at the 2013 UN-REDD Annual Meeting in Bali, Indonesia. The workshop provided guidance on how to integrate gender into the safeguard's principles, criteria and indicators. Similarly, a workshop on gender mainstreaming was also held as part of the 2014 UN-REDD Annual Meeting.

The Programme also implemented Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) activities in several pilot sites in Central Sulawesi which considered gender aspects and a 'Guidance Note to Integrate Gender in implementing


---
**Page 72**

In regard to the National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS), gender considerations are incorporated into various aspects, including its principles, as noted below:22

- The principle on fairness of REDD+ implementation notes that "REDD+ is implemented on the basis of the principle of equity and non-discrimination in forest management, including to women and communities vulnerable to socio-economic and environmental change" (p. 5).
- One of the gender-sensitive indicators for REDD+ projects is the inclusion of a "gender sensitivity" which calls for the attention to equality in roles, needs, responsibilities of men and women (p.23).
- Effectively addressing gender issues requires that REDD+ projects should include a gender component in public participation through capacity-building for community members, especially women and other vulnerable groups, as well as ensuring their equal representation in project planning and decision-making processes (p.25).
- The principle on equity is to be complied with by REDD+ implementing agencies including assurance of gender equality and the right of vulnerable groups to participate equally in REDD+ implementation activities.
- Requirement that the social safeguards framework is designed in such a manner to protect and benefit vulnerable groups, especially women and other vulnerable groups. These include: 1) women whose livelihoods depend on forest resources; 2) women who face the full brunt of changes in family income due to REDD+ projects; and 3) women who are most likely to be excluded from project benefits in terms of fulfillment of their human rights (p.29).
- The gender component should also be included in the social safeguards framework for the inclusion of indicators that evaluate the "accountability of verification results relating to the distribution of benefits from the implementation of REDD+ projects" (p.30).

While the SIS-REDD+ is a result of broad multi-stakeholder consultation, it currently does not contain any reference to gender, either in its principles, criteria or indicators, or its APPR tool. As such, future application of the SIS-REDD+ will need to consider gender elements and ensure that women are included in future social impact monitoring should consider gender elements, recalling that women often use natural resources differently than men (p.31). The Gender Action Plan annexed to the National REDD+ Strategy for the implementation of the FPA's Gender Assessment and Action Plan found in Annex XII c) to this FP provides a significant guidance on this regard, and is described further in Section 6.2.3 below.


E.5.2 The process of preparing the AW

Provide adequate and sufficient information from how the AE will undertake activity-level gender assessments and actions plans (GAPs).


UNDP is committed to reducing gender inequalities in access to and control over resources and within the benefits of development, including in the context of forest governance and REDD+. The project activities have been designed with a focus on ensuring that women are not only beneficiaries but also active participants in forest-based decision-making and/or initiatives, but also to ensure that project activities, and evolving REDD+ activities, are gender-sensitive. This will be achieved by ensuring that the project is implemented in such a way that men and are able to participate meaningfully and equitably, have equal access to resources, and receive compensation for their labor.

To help facilitate these outcomes and ensure the integration of a gender-responsive approach within the project components, UNDP will ensure inclusion and participatory activity-level gender assessments and action plans are undertaken at all levels. The AW will include an overview of how the project will undertake this process. It outlines gender-responsive actions for each of the activities of the project, and includes indicators, targets and measures to monitor progress towards achieving these goals.

The AW will also outline a gender-sensitive approach that is intended to be reinforced and contextualized through sub-national (provincial and district level) action plans, which will include specific gender-responsive actions. The AW will also provide guidance on how the project at national level, given the immense diversity of Indonesia's population. These assessments and action plans will comply with UNDP’s Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP), as well as the United Nations Development Framework for Empowerment and will be developed and validated in consultation with affected stakeholders, including

### Equalizing and Meaningfully Involving Women and Men (and Youth, When Applicable)

In this phase of the UNDP will identify and assess the different needs, constraints, capabilities, and priorities of women, men, girls and boys within the activity sites. It will then incorporate corresponding measures within the action plans to ensure that women, men, girls and boys are equally involved in all aspects of the project activities. Women and men will be able to equitably participate in, inform and benefit from the activities being proposed (3) that women’s decision-making capacity is enhanced through the involvement of women in the planning and implementation processes. Women will also be assigned within the action plans to ensure the gender activities identified have accountability mechanisms for their implementation and monitoring. The project will ensure that all activities are implemented equitably with affected women, men and youth stakeholders before implementation of the activities commence.

The FFP’s GAAP, found in Annex Xiii (c) to this FP, takes into account recent changes in national legislation and operationalization of Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forestry sector, as outlined in the “Tripartite Memorandum of Understanding on Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forestry Sector” issued by the Ministry of Environment and Forest Management (MoEF), the Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MWA) and the Guidelines on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forestry Sector issued by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA). The GAAP will ensure that all activities are implemented effectively and optimizes gender mainstreaming in M&E, policies, programs, and activities in the environment and forestry sector. The GAAP is based on the 2015 Guidelines on Gender Mainstreaming in Environment and Forestry issued by the Ministry of Environment and Forest Management (MoEF). The guidelines themselves, issued as a Presidential Instruction in 2005, was a strategic aim aimed at ensuring that all activities are implemented effectively and optimizes gender mainstreaming in M&E, policies, programs, and activities in the environment and forestry sector. The aim to the regulation recognizes that the Guidelines should not only be limited to the gender gap between men and women but also address the disparity of access, participation, control and benefits of development of children, the elderly, persons with disabilities, indigenous peoples, and other vulnerable groups.

In 2016, accordingly, the GAAP emphasizes capacity building within the MoEF and Forest Management Units (FMUs) to ensure that all activities are implemented effectively and optimizes gender mainstreaming in M&E, policies, programs, and activities in the environment and forestry sector. The GAAP also includes platforms, and gender-responsive reforms in the Social Forestry Program. Actions which support sensitization on gender equality and women’s empowerment will be taken through the establishment of a Gender Working Group within the MoEF, and through the creation of a Gender Unit within the FMUs. These actions are to support women-led community organizations. These actions in the GAAP will help to accelerate action towards poverty alleviation and sustainable development.

Additionally, while there is internal support with gender within the MoEF and MoW, the GAAP concludes that additional external support on gender will be necessary. As such, a gender consultant/NGO will be needed and at least one of the Safeguards Officer, hired to support the project, will be required to have gender expertise. The GAAP also includes capacity building for the Gender Working Group within the MoEF with knowledge on REDD+ and how to integrate gender into it. Capacity building of these teams will take place to ensure that all activities are implemented effectively and optimizes gender mainstreaming in M&E, policies, programs, and activities in the environment and forestry sector.

### Indonesia has established a national financial intelligence unit. Financial Transaction Reports and Analysis Center (PTAP) is an independent agency under the Ministry of Finance. PTAP was established by Law No. 15 of 2002, and the Financial Services Authority (Otoritas Jasa Keuangan or OJK in Indonesian language) the National Police, and the Attorney General’s Office are responsible for its implementation. PTAP is also responsible to monitor money flows and detect illicit financial flows that come from national budget or private budget.

PPATK is an independent agency which directly reports to the President. It uses the follow-the-money approach in preventing and combating crime. PPATK has several focuses, including natural resources related crimes, corruption, money laundering, and tax evasion. PPATK is a member of the Coordinating Ministry for Economic Affairs’ Coordinating Committee to Prevent and Combat Money Laundering Crimes that is chaired by the Coordinating Minister for Economic Affairs. PPATK also has a role in monitoring and reporting on the financial functions of the government. PPATK is also frequently reported by the President and the House of

**Page 74**

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) [Page 17 of 80]

Representatives track the financial history of candidates for high-level posts to ensure they are free from suspicious financial transactions.

OJK, a newer institution, is an independent regulator of the financial services industry, including capital market banks, and non-bank financial institutions. Its objectives include ensuring compliance with laws and regulations, providing technical assistance, monitoring and evaluation as well as investigation to ensure good financial practices by financial service providers. OJK also has a role in promoting the development of the capital markets. Examples are Integrated Licensing Information System for multi-sectoral licenses that shortened the licensing process from 20 days to less than 5 days. In addition, a digital-based RBP system provides greater transparency and the opportunity for corruption.

Moreover, Indonesia also has Anti-Corruption Commission (KPK) that was established through Law No. 30 of 2002 and amended by Law No. 19 of 2014. The KPK is an independent institution with a mandate to enforce Article 5 of Law No. 30 of 2002 provides for the court, which is the only court with the authority in Indonesia to decide on cases involving corruption. The KPK has been Indonesia's leading institution in targeting and preventing corruption in the use of national budget.

KPK’s Law was recently amended in October 2019 for the purpose of strengthening its governance but these recent amendments have not yet been implemented. The KPK is currently focusing on investigating and apprehending many high-level officials at national and local levels. The success rate of KPK prosecution is now 85%.

In addition to its strong legal enforcement presence, KPK is also leading corruption prevention. One of its main focus is on natural resources. On 19 March 2015, KPK, together with 27 relevant ministries and governmental institutions, launched the National Action Plan (NAP) for the Prevention of Corruption in the forestry, mining, and fishing sectors. This movement commenced in 2010-2011 when KPK conducted a diagnostic assessment on corruption risks in these sectors. The NAP was developed as part of the anti-corruption movement in 2011, from 11 March 2013 to 11 March 2016. KPK worked with the President’s Delivery Unit (UPRD) and other relevant institutions to develop a series of initiatives aimed at improving natural resource governance. This series of initiatives have helped improve many governance aspects of forests, including strengthening forest law enforcement capacity, improving land tenure security in protected forest gazettement and the policy to improve coordination between land agency and forestry ministry to resolve conflicts.

Another important policy is the multi-door approach initiated by the President’s Delivery Unit and REDD+ Agency, and the MoJ, signed 20 December 2012, between UPRRED+ agency with law enforcement agencies. This approach aims at improving forest law enforcement capacity through a multi-sectoral approach involving investigators: Ministry of Forestry, Ministry of Environment. This MoU allows close cooperation to use various local laws and regulations in the field of forestry and environment. The MoU also aims at improving the capacity of various agencies to use relevant rules, focusing on corporate crime. Law enforcers often mention that they are not familiar with these rules or do not have sufficient knowledge about them as well as the sectoral approaches to law enforcement. This approach has successfully prevented illegal logging and forest fires in several areas where this approach was implemented.

The private company given a license to develop palm oil plantation in a protected peat swamp forest, which might not be allowed under existing law enforcement approach was used.

The application of UNDP’s Social and Environmental Standards ensures that the project will protect and foster full respect for human rights of Indigenous Peoples (Indonesia refers instead of “Adat communities”) under international conventions, national laws, and regulations, as well as in accordance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards standards and principles relevant to indigenous peoples.

Furthermore, as designed, the RBP outputs and activities are intended to be carried out in a manner that complies with relevant international conventions, national laws, and regulations. The project’s safeguards measures are designed to carry out national forestry objectives of increasing the inclusion of Adat communities in forest management plans to improve forest integrity, sustainable forest management, the livelihoods of communities.

The project activities also will be implemented in a manner that is consistent with relevant international conventions, national laws, and regulations, as well as in accordance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards standards and principles relevant to indigenous peoples. The project will not discriminate against any person or group on the basis of race, color, sex, age, disability, language, religion, political beliefs, sexual orientation, gender identity or expression, marital status, national origin, veteran status, genetic information, or any other characteristic protected by law.

The lion share of the REDD-plus funds will be dedicated to two activities that can benefit Indonesia’s Adat communities: supporting their operations in forest management planning processes (Activity 2.1) and expanding and enhancing implementation of the Social Forestry programme (Activity 2.2).

FHMVs have a number of tasks. These include working with Adat communities as well as local communities and Village leaders to ensure that they are aware of, and can benefit from, the Social Forestry Programme. To help the benefits of the Social Forestry Programme, these help collectives apply for the programme (i.e. formulate proposals), prepare them for submission to the relevant government authorities, and monitor their responsibilities of such collectives should their applications be approved, and the FHMV to help monitor social and environmental safeguards in the implementation of the programme.

The FHMVs will also assist communities with forest management planning processes and other relevant subnational land-use planning processes. They will provide technical assistance for community-based forest management plans, including human resource capacities, as well as funding, its capacity to ensure that indigenous peoples have equitable access to their forests and other natural resources on which they depend.

Also, as summarized above, the Social Forestry Programme has as its main objective to grant forest resource rights to Village and local communities (many of which are Adat communities) over forests. These include rights of management, use, conservation, and protection of forests. The programme is designed for the benefit of Adat (customary) forest users (i.e. property titles). Villages, local communities and Adat communities are equally eligible to apply for forest resource rights under this programme, which will be granted by provincial governments.

The programmes is voluntary, and not imposed on these populations – respecting their rights to choose how they manage the forests in their areas. The terms of reference for the implementation of such agreements largely depend on applications filed by local community and Village leaders and institutions (representing communities) with provincial governments. These agreements are generally non-exclusive that exclude local communities and otherwise marginalized populations in national forest management.

The goal is to ensure that these communities have access to their forests, which are critical sources of resources upon which they depend for their physical and often cultural survival, and so to reduce deforestation and degradation of the environment.

The programme takes its lead from an indicative map of State Forest Areas. It is well-recognized that this map does not include all areas where communities have customary forest rights. The Government of Indonesia and Environmental Partnership has now begun an Adat Forest Indication map and this has been provided for by an MoU between the two parties. This map will be used to identify areas where communities such as the Masyarakat Adat Nusantara (AMAN) are recorded and is informed by the extensive mapping of Adat forests already undertaken in some parts of Indonesia.

The programme will ensure that local communities and Villages holding out access rights, will not be prejudiced in any future planning or management activities. The Government of Indonesia has also agreed to provide a letter stating no procedure defined yet to transition from a lease or permit to a titled Adat forest. The same is now exploring the possibility of including such a commitment as part of the ESRP for the RBP Project requiring that Social Forestry arrangements reduced to writing (leases, partnering agreements etc) may make clear that they in no way waive or otherwise prejudice such rights.

As evidenced by the substantial use of proceeds dedicated to this programme evidencing Indonesia's commitment to the Social Forestry Programme. Key targets to expand Adat titles to forest resources and provide for their sustainable management were identified as a priority in the National Action Plan for Indonesia as necessary for climate change mitigation success (planning to allocate 12.7 million ha under the programmatic REDD+ project). The Government of Indonesia has been working since 2014/2015 and implemented a number of key pieces of legislation to facilitate implementation in a rights-based manner, including the Forest Act, the Forestry Regulation No. 38/2016 on Community Forests, and the Dispute Resolution Act. Together with a number of the DG-activities described previously – these measures evidence the State's responsibility for ensuring that communities have access to their forest resources in a rights-based manner.

Together with the mitigation measures required by the ESF, Indonesia intends to deliver on its Social Forestry target through a community-driven, rights-based manner that properly improves both forests and the lives of communities indigenous to them.

Furthermore, the ESM will particularly guide the above efforts through the appointment of an Adat Communities Pilot. As part of this pilot, the ESM will provide technical assistance in the process of land displacements (occurs through access and use limitations), a Livelihood Action Plan). The ESM also requires that there be no encroachment on customary forest areas by other parties. This is to ensure that the rights of Adat communities are included in the PMU, that capacity building related to indigenous peoples' rights is provided, and that the process of land displacement is transparent.

The ESM will provide technical assistance for the identification of Adat communities (a precondition to filing) as examined and as necessary, referred to expedite and not delay Adat forest title applications. The ESM will also provide technical assistance in the process of land displacements by other parties. This includes the identification of Adat customary forests (also a process begun by the DG), that FPC and compensation procedures be well-defined, and that there is no encroachment on Adat communities' rights.

The ESM will also provide technical assistance to ensure that the PMU does not prejudice any potential law that accelerates the recognition of communities as Adat communities (precondition to filing) and ensures that the process of land displacements by other parties for Adat communities and Villages does not prejudice future claim for titles (also being considered by the DG).

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal (Green Climate Fund)

## F. Legal arrangements

### E.6.1. Legal title to REDD-plus results

The Government of Indonesia has granted legal title to REDD-plus results in the country. This should include an analysis of entitlements to claim for the results to be paid by the GCF.

- **Covenant:** No other party has a compelling claim to the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with the terms and conditions set out above.
- **Transfer of Emission Reductions:** There is no transfer of ownership of the emission reductions paid for by the GCF. Payments will be recorded in the UNFCCC web portal and Indonesia’s website, and corresponding results will not be transferred or used for any other purposes (e.g., offsetting).

It is important to note that there is no transfer of ownership of the emission reductions paid for by the GCF.

The REDD+ results offered by the Government of Indonesia to the GCF for payments amount to about 19% of the total volume of results in the period 2015–2016, as calculated against the reference level. In a case with multiple parties, it is not possible to determine which emission reductions (ERs) calculated using a national RSL to the GCF. It is not possible to determine which emission reductions are covered by the unit that has been established and is part of the Ministry of Finance (Article 12 of Presidential Regulation No. 77/2018 on Management of Environmental Funds).

The land area where these results were offered covers back the 27 MREs that Indonesia offered under the PAWPKH, which includes additional areas beyond those covered by the PAWPKH. Most of this land is located in areas and group islands (Jawa, Bali and Nusa Tenggara) around the PAWPKH, which involves the following provinces: West Java, East Java, Central Java, Yogyakarta, North Sumatra, South Sumatra, Riau Islands, Bangka Belitung Islands, West Kalimantan, East Kalimantan, Central Kalimantan, and South Kalimantan. There is no breakdown on whether these results are offered for private or public use.

In national programs such as Indonesia’s REDD+ program, the title to emission reductions may be shown in different ways depending on the legislation of each country. It should be noted that Indonesia's title to ER at this point in time is a general nature, deriving from laws and regulations.

Ministry of Finance is the entity with the authority to receive, distribute and manage REDD+. The authority comes from Article 12 of Presidential Regulation No. 77/2018 on Management of Environmental Funds (REP 2017). The management of REDD+ fund (including RBP) is conducted by the BPUPK at the unit that has been established and is part of the Ministry of Finance.

The main regulation in Indonesia for results-based payments for emission reductions from REDD is the Regulation of the Minister of Environment No. 20/2013 on Guidelines for Implementation of National Action Plan on Implementing Procedures for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation, Rate of Conservation of Forests, and Sustainable Management of Forests (Article 58 of REP 2017). It sets out the rules related to the implementation of REDD+ including Results-Based Payment (RBP) and other benefits. The results-based payments obtained from the verified emission reductions and other benefits carbon*, of definition para 26.

On a general level, Regulation 2017 states that implementation of REDD+ by RBP can be achieved at national or sub-national levels through the establishment of a management unit for forest conservation and management of forest management units and groups society, of article 62). In addition, executors with legitimate rights to manage forests in REDD locations can propose REDD+, of article. 63) No such proposals have been made.

Significant regulatory measures have been taken and progress has been made in clarifying legal status of forest areas and the role of indigenous communities in many countries, is still evolving. While Ministry of Forestry Regulation P.20/Menhut/12 about Management of Forest Carbon (Regulation No 26 2012) as well as Regulations No P.20/Menhut/
