| ILO/2009/03 and 36/2009 do not expressly create a right to carbon, forest carbon managers and license holders under these regulations are confirmed rights to trade carbon. |
| --- |

The right of the Government of Indonesia to offer emission reductions for payments to the GCF can be said to be derived from Article 1(4) and 3(2) of Presidential Decree No. 87/2015 (Decree on Forest Carbon Management), related to 'forest carbon management', hereinafter for activities in the form of storage and / or carbon sequestration, which are carried out by forest carbon managers who own or manage forests that are owned forest that is either production forest or protected forest. According to this regulation, forest carbon management is defined as: (a) the use of forest resources for purposes other than timber production; and/or (b) to implicitly confer the right to forest carbon to those organizations that carry out forest carbon management, if: (i) such organization has been granted a license or permit by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry related to forest carbon management; (ii) permit from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry to manage forest carbon (ii) area under Article 1(4), Article 3(2) and Article 5(6); (iii) area under Article 7(8) and (9) and (b) and (c) and (d) and (e) and (f).

The results offered to the GCF are achieved through legislative and regulatory initiatives undertaken by the central government, including the issuance of Presidential Decree No. 20/2012 on Forest Carbon Management Regulation No 20 2012 and as REDD+ implementing agency at the national level (article 6 (2) RERP 2017).

The anchor of Indonesia's forest policy is the Law of the Republic of Indonesia number 41 of 1999 regarding forestry, which states that the purpose of forestry activities are to: (a) protect and manage forests for the maximum prosperity of the people', based on justice and sustainability, of article 4(1) and Article 3. The Government of Indonesia has issued several decrees related to forest management, including: (i) Forest area and forest producers; of article 2 (2). (ii) Forest area planning includes 'forest area use'; at article 7(8), (9) and (b) and (c) and (d) and (e) and (f).

If not contradictory to the Law, of article 82.

The two key initiatives by the Government of Indonesia and operated during the period for which results-based payments are made under REDD+ are: (i) The Proclamation of the President of the Republic of Indonesia (NPRES) No. 5/2019 (collectively 'the Moratorium') and the Social Forestry SF (Programme based on Decree No. 33/Menristek/Dikti/PM.17/2016 on Social Forestry 2018) ('Social Forestry').

The Moratorium terminated the granting of new permits for natural primary forests and provided with commercial production forest areas, as well as the issuance of new permits for the release area to meet no new concessions and permits for the exploitation of forest resources in the Moratorium area. This measure is intended to reduce deforestation and degradation of forests and associated greenhouse gas emissions. Substantial natural forest areas under the Moratorium protection are also under the protection of other national laws, including those related to conservation and sustainable use of forests and forest conservation efforts.

The Social Forestry programmes had a similar effect. The Decree has defined an area of 12.7 million hectares to open for social forestry activities in accordance with Article 4 of Ministerial Decree Number F P.83/2016 on Social Forestry (Decree on Social Forestry) provides the rules and procedures for the establishment of village forest, community forests, and other types of forest management units. The Decree also provides for the establishment of social forestry areas in accordance with Article 5(4). The Decree provides facilities for local or indigenous communities to receive forest management rights, apply for SF permits, and manage SF areas under Article 10 (2) and (3), Article 16 (2) and (3), Article 17 (1), article 21(1) and article 40. It grants management and access rights, but not ownership rights.

Indonesia's forest policy further clarifies this by the issuance of Decree No. 8/2015 on Forest Carbon Management, related to 'forest carbon management', hereinafter for activities in the form of storage and / or carbon sequestration, which are carried out by forest carbon managers who own or manage forests that are owned forest that is either production forest or protected forest. According to this regulation, forest carbon management is defined as: (a) the use of forest resources for purposes other than timber production; and/or (b) to implicitly confer the right to forest carbon to those organizations that carry out forest carbon management, if: (i) such organization has been granted a license or permit by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry related to forest carbon management; (ii) permit from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry to manage forest carbon (ii) area under Article 1(4), Article 3(2) and Article 5(6); (iii) area under Article 7(8) and (9) and (b) and (c) and (d) and (e) and (f).

The Decree AFTF No. 2015 is meant to give a degree of legal certainty to holders of Adat forest (the forest that is owned by the indigenous community) and Titled Forest (the forest that is owned by private forests) in terms of the procedures to establish, verify and recognize the rights and obligations allocated to them under Article 3(1), Article 5(2) and Article 7(8). The Decree also provides for the use to be used further certainty as to the title of the forest areas, of articles 5 and 17. Furthermore, it sets out the rights of the Adat and titled forest holders such as the right to: utilise timber forest commodities, non-timber

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) (PAGE 15 OF 80)

##森林碳汇和环境服务，根据第16条的森林功能和法律及法规规定

Decree CCF 2017设立了在森林砍伐阶段、林地边界变更和土地权利证书颁发等事项中解决冲突的程序。第20条决定改变森林面积边界，通过结构化土地合并（第281/2节）或通过将土地纳入国家森林管理局管辖范围（第2节），可以进行此类更改。环境与自然资源部根据第2012年法规No 20和RBP-2017注册其活动。

印度尼西亚共和国法律第41号，即1999年林业法（Forest Law No. 41 of 1999）建立了印度尼西亚政府在森林砍伐控制中的权威。根据第4条，它规定了森林管理原则和规划，包括土地利用计划、森林资源调查、森林国家基金潜力评估以及与森林资源有关的其他事项。第14条规定，应进行“识别并完成所有数据和信息以确定森林资源、森林国家基金潜在价值和其他相关事项”的工作，并由政府在第15条中批准。森林面积确认旨在提供一个框架来定义森林边界、状态、功能以及作为森林管理一部分的活动。

利用碳储存或通过合同安排，例如通过林业合作伙伴或其他实体进行森林砍伐，以实现减缓排放的权利也可以通过许可证建立。几个安全措施已被引入，以防止主张抵消减排量：

1. 国家注册簿（NBR）(http://nbn.menlh.go.id)已设计为提供有关所有碳交易的信息的单一来源。任何新的或变更的实体应立即列出并公布。
2. 任何私人或非官方实体的排放减少，除非在计算RBP结果时使用了特定方法，否则不应被纳入减排量中。
3. 政府于2017年7月1日发布了一个声明（SE-3M/LHK-PHP/IssSet/1/2017/1A），通知称根据碳信用和向印尼国家森林管理局注册的许可持有人交易碳信用的规定，将对许可证持有人进行监管。此外，所有许可持有人还应报告其在任何情况下与业务相关的碳信用交易计划或任何现有合同。

最后，如果现有的实施规程和额外的安全措施不足以防止此类主张，则仅由政府采取必要法律措施，并承担全部责任。

Secretariat’s assessment of FP130

Proposal name: Indonesia REDD-plus RFP for results period 2014-2016

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/ies): Indonesia

Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Project background

The project presents Indonesia's REDD-plus results for the period 2014–2016, calculating a volume of around 27 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CtCO₂eq) in emissions reductions. The results have been submitted to the GCF and are subject to technical assessment.

These results are presented to GCF for a results-based payment (RBP) under the REDD-plus RFP. The project is assessed against the terms of reference (TOR) for the pilot programme (decision B.18/07). The presented results, which comply with UNFCCC and GCF TOR, have been analyzed based on the criteria set out in the annex to this assessment.

Indonesia will use the proceeds from the RBP to invest in the implementation of its National REDD-plus Action Plan (NAP) and National Strategy for Climate Change Mitigation Contribution (NSC). STRAKNAS is complemented by the REDD+ Provincial Strategy and Action Plans in the chosen pilot provinces. The proposed RFP project will focus on the following segments:

- Output 1: Strengthening REDD-plus coordination and implementation and overall REDD-plus architecture
- Output 2: Support to decentralized sustainable forest governance

1.3 Strengths and points of caution

6. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

**Strengths**

- Indonesia’s initiatives operating during the results-based payment period are consistent with the best practices of forest programmes in other countries, including carbon benefits.
- As well as the National REDD+ Strategy (STREK), the proposal highlights increased transparency via the One Map Policy and local forestry management units.

**Points of clarification**

- Strategic use of funds for Indonesia’s forestry programmes is important to help give competing land uses a more transformative course for the forestry sector in Indonesia.
- The proposed use of proceeds aims to augment existing efforts on forest governance, working with key agencies at the national and sub-national levels. It builds on previous efforts on REDD-plus

**Page 94**
---

9\. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective item sheet and addendum XVIII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

### II\. Assessment performance against the investment criteria

#### 2\.1 Impact potential

**Scale: High**

* Indonesia has developed several policies, programs and projects under the policy umbrella of REDD+ (Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation) to reduce emissions. The total volume of emission reductions achieved by these efforts and investments in the forest sector have been 24,892,137 tCO₂e between 2014 and 2016. Indonesia has not received payments for REDD+ results for the period 2014–2016 from any other source. The total volume of emission reductions achieved by these efforts and investments in the forest sector have been included in the technical annex to the biennial update report submitted under the UNFCCC (United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change) to the COP for REDD+.

* These results were scientifically assessed in accordance with the Conference of the Parties decisions on REDD+. The achievement of these past results contributed directly to GCF objectives and targets in the forest and land-use result area, and through the use of the REDD+ proceeds, Indonesia has been able to finance its own efforts. This is a strong indication that it can leverage finance to continue the Government's efforts to reduce deforestation.

* The proposed use of proceeds intends to further support the main interventions applied during the period 2014–2016 and to provide additional financial resources for the design, implementation of REDD+ related efforts and decentralizing forest governance through the operationalization of the forest management units area.

#### 2\.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: High**

* Indonesia's transition strategy document, STEKAN, aims to help the country manage its forests in a way that is best and most sustainable. There are two major initiatives that have helped Indonesia cope with deforestation, namely the "Moratorium termination of granting new forest concessions" (hereinafter referred to as the "moratorium") and the social forestry programme.

* The use of proceeds from REDD+ BPs is innovative and strategic because it aims to support the implementation of the moratorium at a national level. In addition, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), which is the accredited entity (AE) for Indonesia under the GCF, has been engaged in recently as an incentive to provide the BPs to the Government of Indonesia.

* Further, the project strengthens the already important work carried out regarding decentralization and forest governance. The project also supports other initiatives, acknowledging their important role. Other innovative components include further support to social forestry groups, a safeguarding information system and the improvement of forest monitoring.

#### 2\.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

* REDD+ investments and initiatives in Indonesia have contributed towards sustainable development by promoting non-carbon benefits relevant to Sustainable Development Goals 1, 5, 13 and 15. In particular, the various ecosystem services provided by


---
**Page 95**
---

# Forests are essential for providing the key regulating services needed for basic livelihoods in Indonesia, contributing to poverty reduction.

The conservation of Indonesia's forests directly supports biodiversity preservation and enhanced soil and water management through improved ecosystem services.

## STRANAS especially highlights the involvement of local communities, especially Adat (indigenous) peoples, in the protection of the country's forests. This has co-benefits relating to cultural heritage and social cohesion, as well as providing a more sustainable livelihood environment by contributing to the formal recognition of local communities' access rights to forest resources.

### 2.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

10. Local communities, especially indigenous peoples, are important stakeholders in the REDD+ implementation proposed for the province of Aceh. The protection and conservation of Indonesia's forests provide economic and social returns locally through ecosystem services targeted at the ecosystem level (e.g., via the regulation of soil erosion, carbon sequestration and water retention). In addition to these direct benefits, 80% of the population in Aceh and 75% of Indonesia's impoverished populations depend on these ecosystem services for their basic livelihoods. The proposed project will provide a number of co-benefits such as improvements in the forest fire management system and peatland management.

16. The proposed use of proceeds aims to enhance the implementation of REDD-plus in Indonesia, which is currently being implemented by the Indonesian government. However, domestic funding is limited, given the scale of ambition required to protect Indonesia's forests. Consequently, international financial support will be needed for this project. Additional investments will require the creation or development of institutions to support more sustainable forest management and conservation activities. These include improved REDD-plus architecture and decentralized forest governance, especially increasing the role of communities in forest management which is grounded on a human rights approach, especially on the right of participation, land ownership, self-selection, and consultation.

### 2.5 Country ownership Scale: High

18. The proposed project use of funds are fully aligned with national policies and strategies for REDD-plus implementation. Indonesia's NDC highlights the importance of REDD-plus and the need to maintain REDD-plus in national and local policies and institutions. This is reflected in key development plans, such as the National Development Plan (RPJMN) 2015-2019.

UNDP has a strong track record in REDD-plus activities. It has considerable experience of working in forestry, climate change and biodiversity issues in Indonesia.

The project team at UNDP Indonesia has very solid knowledge and experience in implementing projects of a similar nature.

23. Indonesia has continuously invested in REDD-plus effects through the years since the introduction of the National Action Plan (NAP) for Climate Change in 1998. New policies, new resources and renewed focal agencies have been created and supported by Indonesia, underpinning its commitment to reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

As evidenced by the project board and technical committee, the proposal intends to use the process of RFP to work with relevant stakeholders at the national, subnational and local levels. This is in line with the law on public procurement and participatory consultation, as mandated by law. Although there have been concerns regarding the extent of


---
**Page 96**

2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

The REDD+ plans for 2014–2016 presented to GCF and the suggested budget clearly show that the baseline of $3 per tonne per GtCO2eq will be applied to the projects, while cost-effective and in accordance with the TOR of the RBP pilot programme. The proposed use of proceeds, as outlined in the previous sections, is an innovative and transformative intervention into Indonesia’s forest sector.

The preservation and conservation of forests, resulting in improved ecosystem services, is critical for Indonesia’s economic development and social welfare. Efforts are needed to sustain Indonesia's economy and local communities' resilience and livelihoods.

3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

24 The Government of Indonesia and UNDP, which is the AE, have submitted a proposal for payment of REDD+ results on the basis of actions undertaken from 2014 to 2016 as part of Indonesia’s National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) and its implementation through the Special Forestry programme and (2) the moratorium.

3.1.1 Consistency of the design and implementation of REDD+ activities with the Cancun Safeguards, the AE SPS and GCF ESS

The UNDP social and environmental screening procedure (SESF) was used to identify potential adverse impacts on human rights and biodiversity in relation to this project. The SESF is a UNDP-required screening tool that covers the AE thematic safeguards issues and the UNDP SPS criteria of (1) human rights (encompassing risks and benefits related


---
**Page 97**
---

28 On the basis of the SA, the AE determined that the REED project’s overall risk category is moderate. The main risks are described and analysed throughout the ESA report and the ESME, and are captured in a comprehensive manner in the ESME the recommendations and mitigation measures.

29 The social forestry programme and the moratorium were two key initiatives operating in Indonesia during the 2014-2016 period. According to the AE, each contributed to emission reductions and/or avoided deforestation. However, both have been subject to criticism from Indonesia with existing national forest programmes and applicable international conventions and agreements. The ESA notes that the Government of Indonesia has not yet fully aligned these initiatives focused on implementation and what happened in practice to demonstrate how the Government of Indonesia will meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement and other programmes. The ESA concludes that the initiative to achieve the REED plus outcomes for 2014-2016 aligned with most of the principles and standards of the UNFCCC (and consequently the Paris Agreement) and the REDD+ safeguards. However, according to the Government of Indonesia, have highlighted that these two initiatives have not met their stated goals and targets and have not been fully aligned with international conventions and agreements in implementation that have occurred, and observing that the continued multi-stakeholder approach is necessary for the success of both initiatives. The ESA notes that lessons learned from these initiatives warrant the latitude needed for all parties to keep improving the project’s delivery and alignment with international standards and conventions. In response to the recommendations during the 2014-2016 review period, the Government of Indonesia further increased its support for both initiatives by adopting new PLRs, commitments of resources and outreach to stakeholders.

The AE considers that the RBP project is an opportunity for a paradigm shift that will advance the sustainable development and carbon-reduction path set by the Government of Indonesia. The RBP project has the potential to provide the full enabling environment required to meet the desired targets on the social forestry programme, the moratorium and the REDD+ plus safeguards. It also provides the full enabling environment for continued progress and consistency with applicable safeguard policies. Through the RBP project, it is possible that the Government of Indonesia will be able to achieve additional social, economic and environmental co-benefits of the project. Such benefits would include poverty reduction, improved access to education and health services, food security, and protection of the rights of indigenous peoples and other collectives (including their rights to their ancestral and cultural heritage sites). The project seeks to limit and reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting sustainable land use management in a manner that is beneficial to the development village, local community and Adat community stakeholders.

The AE conducted an alignment analysis in terms of both the alignment between the country’s PLRs and the REDD plus safeguards, and the alignment of the AE SES and the two reviewed initiatives (i.e. the social forestry programme and moratorium). The AE notes that there is a need for Indonesia to achieve full alignment between the PLRs, the SES and the REDD plus safeguards. Indeed, it describes “substantial” alignment as being achieved when all three elements are aligned with each other in terms of goals and targets. In the ESA the AE takes a view that, in the future, the ESME will make it possible for the country to achieve full alignment between these three elements. The AE notes that Indonesia All considers this to be a progressive realization and stepwise approach to full compliance with the requirements of the various safeguards. The AE sees the “transparent and honest assessment

The ESMP lays out the project's monitoring and evaluation approach, which will be based on a set of performance principles, criteria and indicators (PCIs) that are aligned with the GCF’s Performance Framework. The AE notes that these PCIs are a starting point to be used in conjunction with the Government’s own performance framework. The ESMP also provides examples of documents and proof of implementation for each PCI. Monitoring and evaluation will be informed by the project’s risk management plan, which includes a process for validation of information from the subnational to the national level on how safeguards are managed and addressed during the implementation of REDD+ activities. As well as informing the monitoring and evaluation approach, this will also provide input to the Ministry, the project board and the project management unit so that decisions can be taken in real time regarding any potential risks or issues.

The AE notes that, due to the iterative and ongoing nature of screening, assessments and risk management planning, it is expected that the project will be required to regularly review management plans and develop amendments where necessary.

The AE provided a comprehensive review of stakeholder engagement activities in the draft community plan as well as an overview of the draft communication plan including assurance and protocols for good faith consultation and free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) processes. The AE notes that while the draft mitigation measures in the ESMP include strategies and approaches for carrying out meaningful engagement, consultation, consent and information disclosure processes, the engagement plan consists of a series of activities to be carried out by the project management unit on a comprehensive, dependent upon a range of project-specific factors. All such plans, whether simple or complex, should be informed by the ESMP and the draft community plan. The AE has included in a checklist of key issues and questions pertinent to any engagement activity.

The ESMP contains detailed information on the development of a grievance redress mechanism (GRM) that will allow project people to register, elevate and resolve grievances, and for disclosure, feedback and closure of complaints. A sample GRM is provided in the ESMP. The AE notes that the ESMP provides an overall management of a responsive and transparent mechanism to be developed upon project approval. The AE also notes that the GCF Secretariat has established a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) which has an existing national GRM to which the project-level GRM will be directly linked – including its reach, scope and processes. The AE notes that the ESMP provides for real-time modifications to project activities in response to the lessons learned. The GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy also states that the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism and the Secretariat’s indigenous peoples’ contact point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.


### 3.2 Gender policy

#### The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

The AE, through the gender assessment, provides the context and indicates the country’s commitment to addressing gender equality in REDD+ activities. The assessment identified a broad range of national-level gender inequalities, and challenges and risks faced by women and other marginalized groups. The challenges in Indonesia in relation to REDD+ action plans arise in


---
**Page 101**
---

### Indonesia has ratified and/or signed a number of key international conventions, treaties and plans of action on gender equality, women’s empowerment and human rights, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Optional Protocol for CEDAW. However, gender-related gaps in national policies and laws are still prevalent at all levels of government, particularly at local levels, particularly in terms of implementing policies and programmes that focus on the promotion of women’s empowerment and the protection of women’s rights, including older people. In Indonesia, political parties are required to have a minimum of 30 per cent women candidates in parliamentary elections, although in practice representation falls significantly below this target. This is also reflected in the low levels of women’s participation and women’s empowerment as part of its human society and development targets.

### Despite the challenges, women engage in many activities contributing to their communities and households. In rural communities, women generally engage in numerous activities related to forest management and use, including collecting firewood, non-timber forest products (e.g., honey, medicinal plants, food animal fodder) and forest protection. The impacts of forest degradation and deforestation on the livelihoods of these women are often significant as they have a higher degree of dependence on the environment. Further, due to increasing development pressures in rural areas, women’s access to land is becoming more limited for activities such as water, food and fuelwood from the forest. Almost the entire forest land is owned by private individuals or companies, including large-scale logging companies, small communities and indigenous peoples or owned by firms or individuals. Local communities can engage in forest management through mechanisms that include community forests, village forests, protected areas and conservation areas. These types of tenure arrangements are usually available to community groups, cooperatives and farmer forest groups that are predominantly male-dominated. Women’s participation is often limited due to the decision-making processes related to forests and REDD-plus projects, leading to limited access to the benefits from initiatives such as carbon credits and other payments for ecosystem services (PES) and payments. The reasons for women’s limited involvement in forest-related decision-making include lack of education and training opportunities, lack of financial resources, and an assumption that forest-related activities are “men’s work.” Similarly, women also have less access to credit and support from government agencies or non-governmental organizations due to the fact that participation in these activities is usually limited to households or community leaders, who are mostly men. It should be noted that female-headed households make up almost 15 per cent of households in rural and urban areas. Women are rarely invited to such meetings


---
**Page 102**
---

and hence women do not have sufficient access to information about relevant activities, projects, etc., as well as concerning their well-being and the forests they depend on.

### As required by the Gender Policy of the GF, the AE has provided a gender action plan.
The gender action plan outlines activities and provides indicators and targets that will address challenges related to gender equality in the implementation of REDD+ plans/programmes. The proposed project/programme. The AE will engage a gender expert (or team of experts) to support this supplementary document, including the development of the gender action plan and timelines.

#### The gender action plan contains several activities that are linked to the findings of the gender equality analysis, including activities to further establish baseline and tailor gender-responsive environmental and social impact assessment which will provide additional gender-sensitive information on the project/programme. Activities at different levels and implementation levels. Activities included in the plan aim to integrate gender into the REDD+ pilot activity, including strengthening the capacity of women and men to participate in decision-making through awareness and capacity-building, by further exploring the potentials for gender-based violence occurrence in relation to project activities, and through activities geared towards the increase of women’s participation in forest management.

#### The gender action plan aims to strengthen the capacity of REDD+ pilot implementation by mainstreaming gender into all aspects of the project/programme. This includes strengthening the capacity of ministries, and strengthening local forest management units. It also includes activities that mainstream gender in community investments, increasing female staffing in extension work, and building women’s capacity for decision-making at all levels. The plan also supports the implementation of the Social Forestry Programme by integrating gender into village and provincial level planning processes. Finally, it aims to strengthen the capacity of women and men in decision-making through awareness and capacity-building. Finally, activities in the gender action plan will be monitored and evaluated on a regular basis by the AE and the UN-REDD plus working group and building their capacity to effectively be involved and participate in decisions related to integrating gender into the environmental and social impact assessment and integrating gender fully into mid-term and final evaluations.

### 3.3 Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)
Under the pilot project for REDD+ plans, GF is requested to provide a payment of USD 10 million over five years from 2014-2019. The total cost of the pilot project for the period 2014–2016. The volume of Ets for which GCF funding is sought within 30 per cent of the total volume of Ets as required under the TOE of the pilot programme.

#### Accredited entity/exceeding entity capable to execute the current programme (medium risk)
UNDP is the AE and has an extensive track record of implementing projects in developing countries. The AE is considered a reliable partner to support the REDD+ plus project in Indonesia.
The Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) and MoF will be the co-executing entities for the pilot project. The MoEF will be responsible for the implementation of the project as it will be mainly involved in Component 1 of the project. The track record of MoEF includes developing STRANAS and supporting various projects funded by different donors with a budget range of USD 50,000 to USD 2 million. The AE has reviewed the track record of MoEF that been carried out and its result is satisfactory according to the AE. As for the MoF, the capacity

### Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Use of proceeds

The proceeds will be used to continue updating and strengthening REDD+ co-funding and implementation. Proceeds will also support decentralization of forest governance and management, including through capacity building for local and sustainable forestry management investments.

The funding proposal states that Output 2, which addresses the need for a performance-based payment mechanism, will be agreed with the Government of Indonesia during the implementation phase.

#### Competing for the carbon ownership

The funding proposal states that the title to emission reductions is of a general nature, deriving from laws and regulations in Indonesia. The legal framework for REDD+ projects includes provisions on the rights of indigenous communities over their forests and the process of clarifying title and rights to its forest. The funding proposal mentions that the right to emissions reductions will be held by the Government of Indonesia until it is sold or used for carbon sequestration.

It also suggests three safeguard measures: namely, use of the national registry; government letters to suspend new contracts or business transactions of carbon credits with third parties; and a commitment from the Government of Indonesia to submit its REDD+ results plus. The AE needs to obtain a cover letter (as advised in the REDD+ plus TOE) from the Government of Indonesia, which will be provided by the GCF.

The funding proposal also states that the AE will need to make an assessment and claim to the results proposed to the GCF for payment, in accordance with national policy and legal or regulatory requirements.

#### Risk of double payments

The funding proposal states that Indonesia has established a national registry system to avoid double counting by providing information and transparency on all forest carbon projects. The AE will need to ensure that all REDD+ projects are registered internationally since 2016. The EBS submitted to GCF will be retired and registered in the Linea REDD+ Information System (LIS) as per the requirements of the Indonesia-Norway Partnership. The report resulted for the partnership is for 2017, therefore the AE will need to ensure that all EBSs are submitted to GCF with those to be considered under Indonesia Norway Partnership.

#### Disbursement plan

The implementation of the end-use activities will be over four years. The AE will need to monitor and report on the progress made in achieving the outcomes. Accordingly, it is recommended that the disbursement of GCF financing should also be carried out over the four-year period corresponding to the implementation of the activity.

### Compliance risks (medium risk)

The AE has provided a detailed description of its programme to address, monitor and mitigate the risks of money laundering, financing of terrorism and prohibited practices.

---

##### Rationale

The proposed payment is for the emission reductions already achieved by Indonesia. The executing entity (Ministry of Environment and Forestry) has a track record of proceeds. Close monitoring and a detailed implementation plan are in place. The government of Indonesia is required to mitigate the risk associated with setting ownership of emission reductions.

66. Monitoring of the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level according to the description provided in this financial proposal.

### 3.5 Legal assessment

#### 3.5.1 The AMA was signed with the AE on 5 August 2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016.

67. The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

68. The proposed project will be implemented in Indonesia, a country which is not provided with any legal protection under international law for GCFs; GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed by the Secretariat. The Government of Indonesia has signed an agreement with the Foreign Affairs of the Republic of Indonesia in 2015 and has been discussing the document since then, but it has not yet been ratified.

69. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or


---
**Page 105**
---

investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### IV. List of proposed conditions

71. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission of an activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
(b) Completion of the relevant due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

### (iii) Is the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in decision 12/CP.17?

The TAR did not flag any issues based on the modalities in decision 12/CP.17

### (iv) Are the data and information provided for the FREL/FRL transparent? (Has information been provided to allow an understanding of how UNFCCC guidance on submission of information on reference levels has been applied?)

The TAR concludes that the information used by Indonesia in constructing its FREL for deforestation and forest degradation is transparent.

### (v) Is the FREL/FRL complete? (Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?)

The TAR concludes that the information used by Indonesia in constructing its FREL for deforestation and forest degradation is complete.

### (vi) Is the FREL/FRL consistent? (Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the time series used for the construction of the FREL/FRL?)

The TAR highlighted some issues as areas for improvement in relation to consistency.
- It was noted that the FREL uses different approaches to calculating means for the biomass pools over the reference period. The AT notes explained the reasons for such methodological differences.
- To identify activity data for soil organic carbon emissions, which are relevant only when it comes to deforestation or forest degradation, the FREL submitted a map of land cover and a corresponding peatland map. For deforestation and forest degradation occurring on peatlands, additional emissions from the


---
**Page 109**
---

## soil organic carbon pool are then calculated. These two data sets are not perfectly harmonized.

* During the AT, Indonesia provided land cover matrices for peatlands. The AT observed some discontinuity in these data and noted that this was due to a lack of consistency. The AT noted that maintaining a consistent interpretation approach across different interpreters is very difficult but that the differences between the two datasets were not observed discretely.

### (vii) Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The data and methodologies used neither over- nor under-estimate emissions or/and removals during the reference period, so far as can be judged.)

* The FRL/FREL addresses issues as areas for improvement in relation to potential over-under estimation of emissions and/or removals.
    * In its submission, Indonesia includes only the gross emissions from deforestation that are associated with clearing peatlands. This excludes biomass regrowth and associated removals after the deforestation event. This is considered a reasonable approach when forests are converted to cropland with annual crops, but not necessarily when the crop is replaced by permanent crops or other high-biomass stocks. In response, Indonesia clarified that when natural vegetation is cleared on drained peatland lands, they rarely grow back into natural forest.
    * Therefore, Indonesia excludes emissions from logged forests and non-forest classes such as croplands, agricultural fallow, pasturelands, urban areas, muddy fields, transmigration area, settlement areas, ports and harbors. This exclusion of emissions is consistent with the exclusion of removals in post-conversion carbon stocks likely leading to an overestimation of emissions from deforestation.
    * Indonesia's FREL includes soil organic carbon emissions from peatlands that are associated with land use change, deforestation and forest degradation on peatlands. Indonesia excludes emissions from the effects of deforestation or forest degradation that occur on peatlands using detailed information on the post-conversion land cover, which determines decomposition.


---
**Page 110**
---

rates. The decomposition rate is variable; for example,
the rate in peatlands with annual crops may differ from
that in peatlands without annual crops. While the emission factors from the Wetlands
Biodiversity Ecosystems Research (WBER) project are based on "peatland drained organic soils", the FREL does not distinguish areas with and without drainage. Indonesia explained that deforestation and forest degradation are accompanied by drainage. Indonesia also explained that it is important to consider all of the secondary forests, including those in small areas, as well as
negligibly small areas of undrained secondary peat forest and drained peatland. In its submission, Indonesia considers all of the secondary forests as drained forests.
The AT notes that the FREL submission does not currently include information on the carbon stocks. The submission does, however, highlight Indonesia's plans to improve data collection in this area. Table 2 shows the emission factors and their dependency on the water table. The AT also notes that Indonesia is planning
collecting more detailed data on the management of peatland drained secondary forests.

(eiii) Have all REED-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions been included?

Indonesia's proposed FREL covers emissions from gross deforestation (defined as a change in land use to a certain threshold) and forest degradation (defined as a change from primary forest to disturbed secondary forest) associated with deforestation and forest degradation. Indonesia explains this exclusion of the other three REED-plus activities (conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks and sustainable management of forests).

The TAR notes that the activities included in the FREL are likely the REED-plus activities with the most significant emissions in Indonesia.

(ii) Have all of the most significant pools been included?

The carbon pools included in the FREL are aboveground biomass and soil organic carbon. Aboveground biomass is included for all strata, while soil organic carbon is included only for deforestation or forest


---
**Page 111**
---

degradation occurring on peatlands. Belowground biomass, litter and deadwood are not included. Soil organic carbon on any soils except peatlands is also not included.

According to the TAR, based on preliminary data from the Indonesian economy, Indonesia estimates that belowground biomass (BGB) in forest lands accounts for 0.5 per cent and close to 2 per cent, respectively, of forest biomass (excluding soil organic carbon). This estimate reflects the underestimation of its low significance. In the modified FREL submission Indonesia explains that, at the national level, the information about other carbon pools is not available. The information on peatlands presented in the submission is confirmed to be similar in other economies with a high proportion of forested peatlands and would likely be significant sources of emissions. The AF considers the treatment of belowground biomass (BGB) as deadwood and soil organic carbon as an area for technical improvement.

(s3) Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included?

The submitted FREL includes only CO2 emissions and does not cover emissions of other GHGs. The FREL states that CO2 contributes more than 90 per cent to total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. AT noted that, according to Indonesia's first BOR as well as its second national communication, peatland fires are a significant source and these could result in large amounts of non-CO2 emissions, particularly on peatland forests. In addition, the TAR notes that the submission does not include emissions from land use changes.

According to the first BOR, peatland fires make up 30-6 per cent of emissions from land use change and deforestation, conversion and grassland conversion, which total 239 per cent. In the submission Indonesia states that it is difficult to provide estimates in the FREL owing to the complexity and high uncertainty of related activity data, but that the Party has plans to include peatland fires in the FREL.

The AF considers the inclusion of non-CO2 GHG emissions an area for technical improvement.


---
**Page 112**
---

(sii) Is the information provided in the construction of the FREL/FRL (data, methodologies and estimated) guided by the most recent applicable IPCC guidance and guidelines as adopted by the Conference of the Parties?

The TAR states that the methods used by Indonesia are consistent with the IPCC Good Practice Guidance on Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF), 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories, as applied to the construction of the FREL. The estimated emissions from peat decomposition in the 2013 Supplement to the 2006 Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories: Wetlands was used.

(siii) Have any significant issues related to the application of IPCC GLA/GPGs been raised in the TAR report?

No significant issues were raised

(ivii) What is the reference period for the FREL/FRL?

The FREL proposed by Indonesia as presented in the TAR is based on the baseline period 1987–2005. However, footnote 22 of the TOIR for the pilot projects states that counting periods should be no shorter than 10 years or longer reference periods as of the launch of this RFP, or those submitting for assessment in 2016 may provide a recalculation of the FREL/FRL.

The Indonesia FREL was submitted on 3 January 2016. Indonesia submitted a recalculated FREL in the FP with a reference period of 20 years (1995-2012)

(iviv) How does the reference level for the results included in the proposal compare to the previous reference level that applies to the same area?

No previous FREL have been submitted

(ivv) Has the country provided information on aggregate uncertainties, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances?

Indonesia carries out an uncertainty analysis of its FREL concluding that the average uncertainty of the emission during the period covered by the FREL is 16.1%


---
**Page 113**
---

(j) Are the reported results in the technical annex to the BUR consistent with the FREL/FRL (including the same pools, activities and gases)?

The TATR states that the LULUCF experts noted that Indonesia ensured overall consistency between its FREL and its estimation of the residual emissions from the activities of reducing emissions from deforestation and reducing emissions from forest degradation in 2013-2017 by:

(a) Using consistent methodologies and data to generate AD on the deforestation area, the forest degradation area and the degraded peatland area where deforestation or forest degradation have occurred.

(b) Using consistent methodologies and data to generate EPs, in particular the same EPs associated with specific land cover changes based on the reference level of deforestation;

(c) Including the same carbon pools above ground biomass and SOC;

(d) Including the same gases: CO2 only;

(e) Covering the same area: entire national territory.

(f) Using the same assumption that all peatland where deforestation or forest degradation has occurred is considered as degraded (degraded);

(g) Using the same forest definition as that used in constructing its FREL.

It noted that according to the FAR, Indonesia identified in the technical annex as inadequate in the reference level of emissions from post decomposition. In the FREL, the reference level of peat decomposition was estimated using a linear regression approach, which was estimated using a linear regression of historical emissions during 2010-2012. The projected emissions for 2013–2017 were larger than before; the linear regression approach estimated projected emissions in 2013-2015 to be smaller than the actual emissions from peat decomposition. Therefore, the reference level of peat decomposition are the total degraded peatland area in each year and the new degraded area is added to the total degraded area from the previous year. The AD and the associated emissions do not decrease


---
**Page 114**
---

unless those degraded peatlands are restored or rewetted.

The LULUCF experts agreed with the Party on the adequacy of the FREL and acknowledged that it was a good basis for the improvement of the pre-estimates included in the FREL.

## (ii) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex transparent? [Has information been provided to allow an understanding of how UNFCCC guidance on results reporting has been addressed?]

The TATR states that the data and information provided in the technical annexes are complete, accurate to the extent possible.

## (iii) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex complete? [Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the results?]

The LULUCF experts concluded that Indonesia provided the necessary data and information to allow for the reconstruction of the results of the implementation of the activities reducing emissions from deforestation and reducing emissions from forest degradation. Although the TATR notes that the data submitted by Indonesia allowing for the reconstruction of results were not explicitly described in the technical annexes, the LULUCF experts noted that these assumptions may affect the accuracy of the estimates included in the FREL.

## (iv) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex consistent? [Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the results time series?]

The TATR concludes that the method and procedure of AD collection are consistent in both the construction of the FREL and the calculation of the baseline. The same methodology was used for the calculations for the FREL and the results of the activities.

## (v) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex accurate? [Does the annex over- or under-nur estimate emissions and/or removals?]

The TATR notes that both the FREL and the results obtained for 2017–2035 are based on the assumption that the amount of above-ground carbon stocks is constant throughout the reference period. All losses of carbon in above-ground biomass are estimated as CO2 emissions, while all gains (e.g., regrowth after logging, instantaneous oxidation), regrowth after land cover change is not considered, and all peatland areas where deforestation or forest degradation has occurred are assumed to be degraded. The LULUCF experts noted that these assumptions may affect the accuracy of the estimates included in the FREL.


---
**Page 115**
---

may be limited because such effects are likely cancelled out by comparing the FRL and the actual emissions.

Furthermore, it was noted that Indonesia expressed concerns about the accuracy of the estimated emissions from peat decomposition expressed as increases of emissions compared with the reference level of peat decomposition in the FRL. The Indonesian authorities stated that until the end of the reporting period for which a country is proposing to receive payments from GCF+ and the results of the activities is ensured, and the LULUCF experts concluded that the results are accurate to the extent possible

### (vi) How many years are there between the last year of the FRL period, and the year corresponding to the results being proposed for payments?

The recalculated FRL covers the period 1999-2012. Payments are requested for the years 2014-2016.

The technical annex discusses uncertainty of selected parameters but does not provide information on aggregate uncertainties.

The FP states that as of now, Indonesia has not yet received any REDD+ results-based payments from any donors or other sources.

### (ix) Are the results proposed to GCF for payment included in a registry or similar system, that tracks emission reductions and corresponding payments? To ensure there is no past or future double payment (or use of such emissions reduction)?

However, the FP mentions payments that are expected to be received. Under the Indonesia-Norway Partnership, Indonesia has reported 7.4 MioCO2eq as results for the year 2017. The FRL+ has established a National Registry System (NRS) and there is no risk of double payments for this year. The exclusion of 2017 was a decision made by the GCF.

Indonesia has established a National Registry System (or SRN) for the implementation of REDD+ at national and subnational levels. The SRN is intended for data collection action and support for climate change initiatives, including the prevention of double counting and avoidance of double counting. The SRN includes data and information on the FRL/FRL+, MRF of results, as well as the contribution to achievement


---
**Page 116**
---

of the NDC. The National Registry System Manager is responsible for maintaining consistency between data and information on the implementation of projects at the national and subnational levels and avoidance of double counting.

**(B.1)** Consistency of the FREL/FRL (data and methodologies applied). During the TA, Indonesia provided a large amount of background information and material including land-cover matrices for potentials. The AT observed some discrepancy in these data with respect to the land cover map used as reference during the FREL submission; land-cover maps were derived largely using visual interpretation, with a large number of image interpreters contributing to the task. The AT acknowledged the immaturity of the Indonesian land cover map and the lack of consistency between it and the FREL/FRL data. The AT also noted that maintaining a consistent interpretation approach across diverse interpretors is very difficult in Indonesia due to the diversity of terrain, vegetation types, and climate. Indonesia explained that it is exploring ways to develop the existing National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) to improve land cover mapping using remote sensing and visual interpretation, in order to improve the land-cover data. In line with this, in the view of the AT, the FREL/FRL data should be used as a reference for the land cover map (see para 10 of the TAR). Moreover, in paragraph 20 of the TAR, the AT noted that in order to identify active forest degradation and deforestation, areas subject to forest degradation or deforestation or forest degradation, the FREL submission overlaid land cover data with Indonesia's peatland map. For deforestation and forest degradation occurring on peatlands, the FREL/FRL data were used as a reference for the land cover map.

These two data sets are not perfectly harmonized. During the TA, Indonesia acknowledged that there is no perfect consistency between the land-cover map and the FREL/FRL. While maps and definitions could technically be harmonised, Indonesia explained that fully harmonizing these two datasets would require significant investment in resources at the ministry-level agencies and is therefore not easy to achieve. The AT acknowledged that Indonesia has made progress towards improving the level of consistency between the land cover map and the peatland map in an area for improvement. Consequently, the score is 1. Significant issues were raised and were resolved; or issues that were unresolved due to limitations of time and data but were not material to the accuracy of the FREL/FRL and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to resolve them.

**(B.1)** Consistency of the FREL/FRL (data and methodologies applied). The AT pointed out a couple of areas for future technical improvement related to the accuracy of the FREL: [i] to improve accuracy and consistency between land cover map and FREL/FRL data, it is recommended that directly compared and automated image processing techniques could be introduced (see para. 10 of the TAR); [ii] to improve accuracy and consistency in change detection analysis to cover further potential sources of error and differentiate between land-cover changes due to natural causes and those caused by human activities (see para. 23 of the TAR). Additionally, the review of the funding proposal by the independent TAP found that both the FREL and FRL data contain calculation errors. The FREL/FRL data contains calculation errors that significantly modify the FREL; in both FRESs, Indonesia calculates the historical average emissions from deforestation and degradation by dividing the sum of annual emissions over a period (e.g., 1980–2015) by the length of the period's length in years, by the number of measurement periods (i.e. seven). Instead, the FREL should be calculated as the average of annual emissions over the period divided by the number of years in each. The differences arising from this error were estimated to be 378,045 metric tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) for 2014; 37,880,345 CO2e for 2015 and 37,799,034 CO2e for 2016. The average annual emissions over the period were calculated as used by the independent TAP. In its revised funding proposal, Indonesia revised the FREL so it represented an annual average over the historical period. Therefore, the score of 1 is maintained; however, significant issues were raised and were resolved due to the limitation of time and data but were not material to the accuracy of the FREL/FRL

The FREL/FRL, and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome these?

(a) B.1 (a) Inclusion of the most significant pools in the FREL/FRL. The FREL limits its scope to above-ground biomass and soil organic carbon on organic soils, which are the most significant pools for peatland ecosystems. However, the AT considered the inclusion of other significant pools, such as below-ground biomass, dead wood, and litter, as well as land use change and forest management improvement (see para 4(c) of the TARI). Moreover, in its funding proposal (section B.1.1), Indonesia also included peatland fire emissions as a significant pool for inclusion in the resumption of the national FREL to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) planned for 2020. Consequently, the score is 1. "No, but" because the country has not yet submitted its updated land use change and forest management emissions or underestimates removals, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include these in future FRELs.

(b) B.1 (b) Inclusion of all significant sources of emissions. The submitted FREL includes only CO2 emissions and does not cover emissions of other GHGs. The submission states that the GEF/UNDP TA is currently being updated to include methane, nitrous oxide, and fluorinated gases. The AT noted that, according to Indonesia's first BUR as well as its second national communication (NDC), peatland fires are a significant source of emissions with large amounts of NOx CO2 emissions, in particular on peatland forests. Consequently, the AT considered that the FREL should include peatland fire emissions and other GHGs (see para 31 of the TARI). As shared with the LULUCF experts during the TA, the funding proposal submitted by Indonesia to the GEF/UNDP TA includes a plan for progress on peatland fire emission estimates by collating new findings from published peer reviewed literature. Advancing remote-sensing technology to improve burned scar and post-fire vegetation cover mapping is also considered as an area of improvement in annual emissions calculations so that these estimates may be included as an improvement in a future FREL. The AT noted that the GEF/UNDP TA has also been updated with the 2019 annual analysis of Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer(MODIS) data for "hot spots" and peatland fires, which are used to identify areas where fire is likely to occur due to lack of data and/or the emissions do not overestimate emissions, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include these in future FRELs.

(c) B.1 (c) Significant issues related to the application of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Guidelines (GLs)/Good Practice Guidance (GPGs) in the country's FREL/FRL. The AT noted that, according to Indonesia's first BUR and emissions from peatland decomposition associated with deforestation and forest degradation are not included in the FREL because these emissions are considered by the wetlands supplement are intended to be applied only to "drained organic soils". The FREL does not distinguish areas with and without drainage. During the facilitative exchange for the TA, Indonesia also noted that peatland fires on drained peatlands are usually accompanied by deforestation. Indonesia also included information in the supplementary document that the majority of the country's peatlands are underdrained and the negligibly small areas of undrained secondary peat forest, and therefore Indonesia has not included emissions from these areas in its FREL for drained forests. The AT noted that the FREL submission does not currently include data to substantiate this statement. The AT also considered that there is a need for further analysis to improve the data on peatlands, chiefly about emission factors and their dependency on the surface area of the peatland, as well as the extent of drainage. Indonesia has more detailed data on the management of peatlands in its area as an area for improvement (see para 21 of the TARI). # Impact potential

## Scale: High

### Indonesia

1. **Population**: The world’s fourth most populous nation with a population of about 267.3 million as of 2018.
2. **Economic Growth**: The economic growth of the country has been accompanied by deforestation, particularly in Sumatra and Kalimantan.
3. **Natural Resources**: Indonesia is home to more than 360 ethnic groups and is one of 17 "mega diverse" countries with a rich biodiversity.
4. **Land Use**: Natural-resource-based production (minerals, energy, agriculture, forestry, and fishing) accounted for one-fifth of value added in 2017.

### Key Points

- **Forest Loss**: Indonesia has lost more forest area than any other country since 2000, with the largest loss occurring after that of Brazil.
- **Land Use Change**: Forest and peatland fires were used to clear land, notably for oil palm and timber plantations. The Indonesian government has been affected by deforestation on these two islands alone accounting for almost 90 percent of national forest loss.

### Actions

1. **New Concessions**: A new concession for plantations and logging of primary forests and peatlands has been in place since 2011 but has not been fully effective (Justin et al., 2017).
2. **Better Law Enforcement**: Better law enforcement would help control deforestation.
3. **REDD+ Progress**: In the latest announcement by the UNFCCC, Indonesia reports the results of implementing REDD+ activities for 2013–2017, calculated against the PREL, which amount to emission reductions equivalent to 458 million tons (annual emissions) and 244.992.135 tCO₂eq as the total for 2013–2017.

### References

- [Justin et al., 2017] Justin, T., et al. "The Indonesian Forest Sector: A Study of Indonesia’s Forests." (2017).
- [UNFCCC] UN Framework Convention on Climate Change.

18. In calculating the FREL, it is worth pointing out that past decomposition accounts for a large part of Indonesia’s emissions. Indonesia has included soil organic carbon emissions from peat decomposition in the UNFCCC FREL in the spirit of including all significant pools and sources of greenhouse gas emissions. However, these estimates are considered to be highly uncertain due to the high level of uncertainty associated with these emissions. However, emission factors from peatland still require a lot of improvement, as current estimates were based on IPCC default values. In addition, it is important to note that the inclusion of soil organic carbon emissions from peat decomposition in drained peatland areas generates CO2 emissions for long periods of time. Indonesia has therefore decided not to include these emissions in its FREL and has instead included them in the FREL submitted to the UNFCCC.

19. Considering the above, the independent TAP noted that the requirements of the GCF pilot project are not sufficient for increasing FREL emissions from non-high forest low deforestation countries. Following the independent TAP assessment, this has been modified to represent Indonesia’s commitment to increase its FREL by 2030, with a target of reaching 568,988,033 tCO2e by 2020 for the purpose of this funding proposal.

17. With regard to Indonesia's remark in highlighting that the scorecard does not consider this very high level of uncertainty associated with peat emissions, the GCF Secretariat and APF would like to suggest that the GCF Secretariat and the Board revise the scorecard, which has already shown a lack of flexibility for countries with high levels of uncertainty. In addition, it should be noted that limiting the use of increasing FREL due to the country’s status as high forest low deforestation countries is not in line with the spirit of the GCF Pilot Project, which is flexible and subject to national circumstances to justify FREL adjustments.

18. Based on a well-developed National REDD+ Strategy (PTTAMAS) initiated in 2012, Indonesia has been implementing its REDD+ strategy since 2014-2016, aside from the setback in 2015 when massive fires burned large swaths of peatland. The 2015 fires also led to Indonesia taking numerous actions including the introduction of new forest management and conservation regulations, the establishment of a Peatland Restoration Agency, and increased policy priorities for forests and land use. Since 2015 onwards, fires can be delineated from Landsat, with initial indications of the hotspot.

19. The use of the proceeds will further strengthen the Government of Indonesia’s capacity to coordinate and implement REDD+ at national level. In addition, the funds will help further develop the overall REDD+ architecture including the areas of PRL national forest monitoring and reporting, the development of a national forest information system, among others. It will also aim to strengthen the Government of Indonesia's capacity for REDD+ plus coordination and implementation at the national, provincial and local levels.

20. The proceeds will also be used to further extend and enhance the operationalization of social forestry in Indonesia. This includes the development of community-based forest management and rehabilitation as well as community empowerment and poverty alleviation. Both social forestry and REDD+ will improve forest governance, helping address the rate of deforestation and degradation in Indonesia. The proceeds from this project component will devolve access to and management of forest land resources to communities in adequate areas, contributing to REDD+.

21. RBPs provide Indonesia with a major opportunity in the fight against deforestation, complementing the overall strategy of the country, which has proven to be effective. RBPs alone cannot completely solve the problem; however, they can be a substitute for decisive political support and a high level of ambition.

20. The impact of the RBP cannot only be judged by the results of the past, but it can be judged mainly for the continuous improvement of the country's deforestation strategy and the


---
**Page 130**
---

effective use of proceeds. The refinement of the REDD+ plan architecture, together with the strengthening of the FMsIs and the passing of more responsibility to communities in different regions of Indonesia to manage forest land, will certainly have the potential to have an impact on the fight against deforestation. The Indonesian government has shown itself to be as successful as expected so far, and it therefore needs to be accelerated and scaled up. Moreover, as communities are not the ones making the biggest impacts in terms of deforestation, there is now a need for more support from the private sector, including timber companies, forest concessions, uniting communities and enterprises located in the selected areas in a common effort to reduce deforestation.

It is reassuring to note that the official deforestation numbers of the Government of Indonesia show that the rate of forest loss has continued its decline after 2014. The data reported by the Ministry of Forestry indicates a slight decrease from an annual figure of 840,000 hectares. Indonesia seems to be moving in the right direction to achieve its NDCs and REDD+ targets for the period between 2015-2030.

However, Indonesia is so big and diverse that there is also a need to look at specific regions and target the main hotspots of deforestation. For example, the provinces of East Kalimantan, Maluku and West Papua experienced a 43 per cent, 40 per cent, and 28 per cent increase in forest loss between 2015-2016 compared to the previous year. Similarly, Central Kalimantan, Riau and West Kalimantan have seen the most loss of forests by total area. The funding provided under the REDD+ program is not enough to support all these provinces with strategies and action plans (SAPs) to support regional interventions: Aceh, East Kalimantan, Central Kalimantan, North Sumatra, Riau, West Kalimantan, South Sulawesi, West Kalimantan and West Sumatera. Efforts to save the rich biodiversity and ecosystems of these forest areas is of crucial importance.

Finally, a moratorium on logging should be strongly enforced to avoid leakage, especially by avoiding pressure to clear forests in peatlands.


## 2.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

Indonesia's vast forest areas and rich biodiversity make it a unique country to prove the efficacy of the REDD+ plus program. Compared to other countries, Indonesia has made great progress to reduce deforestation in recent years. It has been one of the few exceptions of the GEF REDD+ projects that have shown positive results. However, as with many other countries continues to slow deforestation rates. The country has been implementing STRANAS since 2015 and has also committed itself to a moratorium on logging in forest concessions plus provinces.

Indonesia made an early commitment in 2009 to reduce GHG emissions by 26 per cent by 2020, or by 41 per cent with international assistance was forthcoming. In 2011, the government of Indonesia decided to issue a presidential instruction (hereinafter the "moratorium") postponing logging activities in primary forests and peatlands for 30 years.

This moratorium was extended on four occasions: (in 2011, 2013, 2015 and 2017) and was recently renewed until August 2019. The extension of the moratorium on forest concessions has been applauded by many, hoping that it achieves further reductions in deforestation.


---
**Page 131**
---

20 However, as noted by the World Resources Institute Indonesia has focused more on REDD+ less on addressing the "plus" components which include forest conservation. For example a recent analysis found that protecting the primary forest of Papua, coupled with the restoration of degraded forests in Sumatra and Kalimantan could reduce Indonesia's emissions below the baseline emissions projected for 2030 in Indonesia's NOC Failing to protect these primary forests could put the government's emission reduction target out of reach. The same analysis by the World Resources Institute Indonesia found that if the government were to focus on biodiversity hotspots area there would be a need to expand the moratorium policy including high biodiversity areas and peatlands. 21 Some of the "plus" components of the proposal will target important biodiversity areas as noted above but more effort should be given to these areas.

25 The use of the proceeds of the proposal will target two different but related outcomes. The first outcome will be to continue updating building and strengthening its REDD+ plus architecture, including the development of a national forest monitoring system and management REDD-plus nationally Within this framework there could be elements of innovation such as the development of new tools for the implementation of the national forest law, performance calculations at the national and subnational level, the development and operationalization of the national REDD-plus reporting system, the development and improvement and operationalization of the national and subprovincial (local to provincial) REDDplus reporting systems. The size of the country and the diversity of its areas and the evident complexity of the Indonesian forest landscape control of the use of forest resources mean that effective tools to monitor deforestation and forest degradation are required.

26 Indonesia has a long history of decentralization and the establishment of decentralized sustainable forest governance. The Government of Indonesia is rightly advancing to decentralize the sustainable management of forests through the operationalization of FMAs. The country needs to have an accurate understanding of its forests, including its timber and other natural resources in an accurate manner. The FMAs are responsible for developing implementing and enforcing forest management plans, conducting forest inventories, preparing participatory plans, enforcing forest regulations such as forest fire control and other illegal practices. The FMAs will also be responsible for the implementation of the agreements available under the social forestry programme. The idea is have joint authority by central government and local communities to manage forests in a sustainable manner the effective implementation of the national policies while allowing local governments to act. The independent role of the provincial governments in the operationalization of the national REDD-plus scheme will certainly be innovative especially if the Government of Indonesia retains financial leverage through the recently established environmental fund to give a clear political signal that it is committed to the sustainable management of forests at both central and provincial REDD-plus strategies and action plans.

27 Indonesia has made significant progress in its understanding of social forestry since 1975. The country started a community-based management forestry programme targeting 2.5 million hectares of social forests which was scaled up in 2014 to 12.7 million hectares between 2015 and 2018. This has resulted in the empowerment of communities, especially among forest-dependent people while reducing deforestation and forest degradation improving land management practices and increasing community income. According to data from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry there are 25,963 villages located in or around forest areas (2017) with approximately 48% of these villages being classified as poor. In total which approximately 1.7 million are classified as poor The programme involves selecting democratizing and registering access and/or ownership rights as well as supporting the


---
**Page 132**
---

23 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The historic and contemporary rates of deforestation in Indonesia suggest a changing trend in the government approach, which is seeing the consequences of alarming deforestation rates in ecosystem services and is acting to prevent further forest and biodiversity loss.

Community Forests are an important part of this new approach. These forests are not only for the sustainable development of the country but also for the world at large. These forests are not only vital for the conservation of Indonesia's rich biodiversity, but they are also a major carbon sink on the planet. As noted above, the use of the proceeds and the REDD-plus strategy should focus more in the conservation component, especially in rich biodiversity hotspots in the country.

The community forest is a unique model that provides both environmental protection for consumption and agriculture. Ensuring water for agriculture is only possible if the ecosystem services provided by forests are guaranteed. Other non-carbon benefits include the mitigation of soil erosion and soil stabilization.

Improved forest governance will not only increase transparency, but it will also allow for a multi-stakeholder approach to forest management. The national and regional governments, along with the community and individuals, are able to be encouraged to participate in forestry supervision.

The government has signed an agreement for a Forest Partnership Agreement bringing private sector individuals (or groups) and local communities or villages together to work on determining the boundaries of their respective land tenure rights within distinct areas of state forests. The agreements will serve to avoid potential conflicts between the communities that are dependent on the natural resources for their livelihoods and the private sector users of these same resources.

It is also recognized that land tenure security, traditional knowledge, and the distinct ways of life of forest-dependent communities are important in achieving national objectives of climate change mitigation through reduced GHG emissions.

The social forestry programme will support the recognition of land tenure of several communities in the form of customary rights (Adat) or other forms of tenure as recognition of their customary Adat forests (per titles). This is important not only to protect the forests but also to ensure that they are managed and protected in a way that respects the traditional knowledge and cultural identity that is so incredibly diverse in Indonesia.


---
**Page 133**
---

### 2.4 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

In recent decades, Indonesia’s living standards have risen and the economy is more resilient. Gross domestic product per capita has risen by 70 per cent during the past two decades. The country has made progress in implementing economic policies and progress in structural reforms have been recognised by credit rating agencies, and Indonesia has become a leading producer of palm oil and rubber in the world environment.* Economic growth in recent years, before the coronavirus disease 19 (COVID-19) crisis, had averaged around 5 per cent. The country’s economy is dominated by agriculture, which accounts for about 12.8 per cent to the country’s gross domestic product and employs 30.2 per cent of the active population. Indonesia is the largest producer of palm oil and the second-largest producer of rubber in the world.

For many years, Indonesia favoured economic growth at the expense of losing wealth in forests and biodiversity. However, since 1997, the government has implemented a policy model that for years favoured private sector enterprises dedicated to palm oil, forest concessions and

**Page 134**
