## Alignment with national climate strategy

### Scale: High

25.1 Ownership

The government enacted the moratorium in a context of a national strategy on REDD+, a USD 3 billion co-operative agreement with Norway signed in 2010. Even though this agreement was not renewed, Indonesia has continued to implement it and is proceeding with a first payment. Other governments and donors have also supported the ongoing efforts by providing financial resources.

The fact that Indonesia has more than 300 islands spread over a vast geographical area makes it even harder to control deforestation. The resources from the GCF RFPs will represent just a small part of what is needed for the country to meet its commitments under REDD+ and BUR, which includes a conservation estimate on financial needs from 2018–2030 to meet the overall climate change mitigation goals.

Based on the size of the country, the degree of vulnerability of many different communities in the many islands of the country and the country's exposure to climate risks, the independent TAP believes there is sufficient justification with regard to the need to support Indonesia.


---
**Page 135**
---

### Indonesia’s National Action Plan for Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions (2015–2030)

In 2015, Indonesia adopted a National Action Plan for Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions (2015-2030) (RAN-GGR) that established the national emissions reduction target of 26 per cent below business as usual by 2020, and up to 41 per cent below business as usual with adequate international support. The RAN-GGR is a comprehensive framework for implementation of various mitigation actions in the forestry, agriculture, energy, transport, industry and waste sectors. It sets for the forestry sector: land and forest restoration targets, and social forestry targets.

Within the RAN-GGR, the country delivered a National Medium-Term Development Plan (2017–2035) that includes mitigation actions in five key sectors (forestry and peat lands; agriculture; energy and transportation; industry; and waste).

Moreover, in 2012 Indonesia released STRASMAS, structured around five pillars: (1) institutional strengthening, (2) capacity building, (3) policy development, (4) land-use shifts, and (5) multi-stakeholder involvement. The project presents a comprehensive table that summarizes the actions under each of pillar, presenting several policies and measures that Indonesia has implemented.

The STRASMAS is complemented by the SREPs in the 11 REDD-plus pilot provinces: SREPs document the institutional arrangements for implementing REDD-plus activities, summarize them, and plans for actions for REDD-plus implementation based on the specific circumstances of each province. The SREPs also provide a framework to change REDD-plus into subnational development planning processes and systems.

Aside from policies, one of the most important instruments has been the moratorium on the issuance of new concessions in peatlands and forests with high carbon stock, conditional instruction every two years (2011, 2013, 2015), and most recently made permanent† in August 2017. This moratorium was a result of the national-level discussion about the importance of protecting peatlands, and it resulted in substantial emissions reductions.

The social forestry programme is part of President Joko Widodo’s “just economy” policy, which is intended to reduce poverty by providing access to land for smallholders. The government intends to redistribute control over 21.7 million hectares of land to communities (about 12 per cent of the nation's land area). Of this, 16.8 million hectares are on forest land, including the 1.27 million hectares that were previously under concession and are now being converted into sustainable forest management.

The Ministry of Environment and Forestry TAP believes that the country has the right policies and institutional frameworks in place to move STRASMAS forward in line with the purpose of the proposed project.


### Capacity of Accredited entity and executing entities to deliver

The Ministry of Finance will be the executing entity (i.e. implementing partner) responsible for project management, monitoring and evaluation of the project interventions, and ensuring outcomes in line with the effective use of GEF resources. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry TAP is the implementing agency. Project activities are coordinated by the REDD+ Sub-Directorate under the Directorate General of Climate Change under this MOU.

The experience of MOEF includes the development of STRASMAS, the FREL endorsed by the UNFCCC and the safeguards information system (SIS). The GEF project will build on the vast experience of MOEF in sustainable forest management and sustainable land use.


---
**Page 136**
---

programmes including through community forestry schemes, the rehabilitation of degraded land and peatland restoration, increasing climate change adaptation capacity and GHG emission reductions. In 2016, UNDP managed a budget of IDR 5.95 trillion (around USD 450 million) for REDD+ projects.

At as accredited entity to GCF, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) will be responsible for supervising and providing technical backstopping during project implementation. UNDP will provide overall guidance on the implementation of the project, as well as projects related to forested REDD+. UNDP has been a partner of GCF since 2009, supporting the global and national level in developing strategies, policies, and frameworks for climate change adaptation and enhancing forest governance structures and practices with several programmes.

There will be a project board composed of representatives of the Ministry of Finance, MoFID (Indonesia’s National Development Planning Agency), UNDP, and GCF. The board will provide overall managerial guidance for project execution. UNDP will play the role of ensuring quality assurance and monitoring by the project board by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions.

The project national director will be a senior staff member of the Ministry of Finance and will report to the project board. The project national director is responsible for providing technical feasibility of the project and ensuring that its implementation leads to the achievement of the project’s objectives. The project national director will provide input on a day-to-day basis within the constraints laid down by the project board. This will be done in close supervision with UNDP and the Ministry of Finance.

The project will be implemented through the newly established Environmental Fund Management Agency (BPJU), under Directorate General Treasury of the Ministry of Finance, which is responsible for managing the national budget. The BPJU will provide supporting REDD+ nationally and in further mainstreaming and strengthening REDD+ within Indonesia’s public institutions and processes. Part of the activities of the funding proposal, is to strengthen the capacity of the BPJU to manage the project and to ensure that it has access for “direct access”.

The Technical Committee, which already exists and is chaired by MoFID, will provide technical support to the project board. It includes relevant partners, technical experts and other stakeholders such as civil society organizations, academies, indigenous peoples, local communities, and private sector representatives. The Technical Committee will be responsible for and address the activities that will be supported by the use of the proceeds, key partners supporting the REDD+ programme in Indonesia.

The implementation of this project will be closely coordinated with other relevant projects and initiatives supporting the national and subnational REDD+ processes, as well as those supporting the GCF’s Strategic Plan. The Technical Committee is responsible for coordinating directly by the various General Directorates of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, which is mandated to oversee these thematic areas, as well as through the Technical Committee.

### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

Indonesia has been engaging stakeholders in REDD+ since 2011 for the preparation of STRASMs with focus on identifying how to address potential social and environmental impacts and compliance with the Cancun safeguards. Multi-stakeholder consultations were held in every province, district, and sub-district level as well as at national and international levels.


---
**Page 137**
---

70. Furthermore, 11 provinces (Aceh, Central Kalimantan, Central Sulawesi, East Kalimantan, Jambi, Papua, Riau, South Sumatera, West Kalimantan, West Papua, and West Sumatra) have formulated provincial SRAPs involving relevant stakeholders in REDD+ plus planning stages. These SRAP development processes uphold the principle of gender equity and social inclusion.

72. Moreover, in early 2011, the previous Ministry of Forestry commenced a multi-stakeholder process to develop a national SRAP for Indonesia. The SRAP, which translates them for the Indonesian context, and drafts a structure and mechanism for SIS-REDD+ plus and REDD+ Plus implementation at the sub-national level. This document, in a conversation to the SIS and allows all relevant stakeholders to be involved and informed.

73. The proposal places great emphasis on continuing engagement with stakeholders throughout the process of developing and implementing the national SRAP, working together with Indonesia's STRIMAS, using several multi-stakeholder forums already in place. The project will strengthen local governance structures at the sub-national level, including through village and sub-village and Adat communities, as well as local governments. The programme also fosters strengthening local access to a grievance mechanism and will bring together stakeholders in forest management and conservation.

74. Overall, the independent TAP believes that the Government of Indonesia is deeply involved in the proposed project, with strong institutions and policies in place.


### 2.6 Clarity and effectiveness Scale: High

75. Indonesia has presented the payment for results report, accounting for 18 per cent of the total volume of REDD-plus results between 2014 and 2016 (measured against FREL), corresponding to 39 million tonnes of CO2e. This is equivalent to US$750 million or US$18 per tonne, which is equivalent to a total of $44,789,836 at CERs. The evaluation of the scorecard reduced the total cost by 20 per cent and, as relevantly submitted emission reductions were reduced to 20,250,000 tCO2e.

76. Payment is equivalent to US$183,791,250 at USD $5 per tCO2e, including 2.5 per cent for non-conformity with the scorecard.

77. The effectiveness of the use of proceeds can only be evaluated in the future, upon understanding the results of the evaluation of the implementation of the FMIs and when there is sufficient evidence to assess the success of the implementation of the FMIs in the 11 selected regions as well as the amount of forest hectares in the hands of communities through the process of land titling.

78. So far, the Government of Indonesia has made ambitious plans with targets that have been difficult to achieve. The government's National Forest Management Action Plan for 2015-2019 aims to complete 100 per cent of forest demarcation, establish 623 Mha and make them operational, and foster social forestry partnerships across 12.7 million hectares (including 4.8 million hectares in the form of community forests). Although progress has been marked, only 3.4 million hectares of forests have been designated for social forestry by October 2015 (of which 69 per cent is in the form of community forests), and only 7.5 million hectares nationwide (2018), covering a total area of about 83 million hectares (i.e., nearly the entirety of the forest estate).

79. In terms of budget allocation, the project will invest around 7.7 per cent of the proceeds for continuing to update, build and strengthen its REDD-plus architecture, as well as further strengthening the institutional framework. The remaining 92.3 per cent of the budget is allocated to land titling nationwide (around 90 per cent of the budget will be used to further extend and enhance social forestry and FMIs

During the TA Indonesia provided a large amount of background information and material including land cover matrices for peatlands. The AT observed some discontinuity in these data that may reduce opportunities in ensuring consistency. According to the FRL submission, land-cover maps are subject to significant visual interpretation, with a large number of image interpreters contributing to the maps. This is an area where a great deal of effort went into building the time series of land cover maps, but noted that maintaining consistency between the land cover map and image interpreters is very difficult and that this is a potential source of the observed discontinuity in the data. The AT also noted that it is exploring ways to develop the existing NPMI for example by combining the FRL with other datasets such as remote sensing to improve the land-cover data. In line with this, the view of the AT, improving quality management is an area for improvement (targeted action 10).

Moreover, the AT noted (paragraph 28 of the TAR) that to identify activity data for soil organic carbon emissions from peatland drained for agriculture and other uses, it would be necessary to overlay land cover maps with the FRL submission overlays landcover maps with Indonesia's peatland map. For this reason, the AT recommended that the definition of peatlands, additional emissions from the soil organic carbon pool are their old carbon stocks, should be harmonized.

During the TA, Indonesia acknowledged that such imperfect harmonization between the land cover and peatland definitions could technically be harmonized. Indonesia explained that fully harmonizing data sets would require collaboration and arrangements with other countries and agencies which were therefore not easy to achieve. The AT acknowledged that Indonesia has been working on improving the quality of data and ensuring consistency between the land-cover map and the peatland map is an area for improvement.

**Page 145**

The AT pointed out a couple of areas for future technical improvement of the FREL:
- Improving accuracy and consistency in satellite image interpretation, time series satellite images could be used to create composite images using satellite images for time points individually by overlaying them on each other. Advanced image processing techniques could be introduced (e.g., change detection).
- Broadening the scope of the uncertainty analysis to cover further potential sources of error, and differentiating between uncertainties associated with regard to emissions factors and activity data (e.g., land use changes).

Additionally, the review of the funding proposal by the independent TAF found that both the FREL measured by AF and the one included in the revised funding proposal significantly modify the FREL. In both FRELs, indicator values are calculated as the historical average annual emissions divided by dividing the sum of annual average emissions in each measurement period (i.e., the number of years since 1990) by the number of measurement periods (7). Instead, the FREL should have been calculated as the sum of annual averages over the reference period divided the number of years in such period. The differences arising from this error were estimated by the independent TAF to be -385,246 tCO2eq for 2014, 37,800,345 tCO2eq for 2015 and 37,799,834 tCO2eq for 2016. In all cases the FREL should be calculated as the average value calculated by the independent TAF. In its revised funding proposal, Independent TAF used a FREL to fit represent an annual average over the historical period.

This would be useful for estimating the opposite conversions, which would lead to a carbon sink rather than a source (paragraph 30 of the TAF). Indonesia plans to include additional REDD-plus activities and consider this inclusion as an area for improvement (paragraph 28 of the TAF).

The FREL includes all significant pools of organic carbon on organic soils, which are the most significant pools in terms of emissions from forests (paragraph 17 of the TAF). The belowground biomass, deadwood and soil organic carbon, as an area for which Indonesia could explore where data sets used for estimating opposite conversions are available. ### Actions to address the risk of reversals

In its funding proposal, Indonesia summarizes a number of policies and measures it has put in place to address the risk of reversals. It also states that the amount of compensation for forest area not yet paid by the GCF is relatively low (in the sense that they do not represent a significant share of the total area of forests) compared with what was achieved by Indonesia in the period 2014-2017 and that it could be considered as an indicator of the risk of reversals, which has been being paid for by the GCF. In this respect, it is important to note that such compensation is not ensured, as nothing prevents the government of Indonesia from using the funds obtained in 2014-2017 (e.g., there is no proposal to create a buffer or reserve fund). The amount of compensation will be backed up in an amount reflecting the risk of reversals.

# Impact potential Evaluation

- The relevant mitigation and/or adaptation impact is specified.
- The GCF core indicators (and other indicators) are provided with specific values.
- The methodologies provided for calculating non-GHG indicators are clear and robust.
- The programme compares indicator values against appropriate benchmarks to demonstrate the impact potential.

The proposal clearly:
- Describes the potential for scaling up to the country's NDC, national REDD-plus strategy or development plans and policies.
- Explains how the programme contributes to strengthening knowledge and learning.
- Describes how proposed measures will create an enabling environment and contribute to sustainable development.
- Explains how the programme strengthens the regulatory framework and policies.
- Demonstrates paradigm shift potential for catalyzing impact beyond a one-off payment.

The proposal demonstrates environmental, social and economic impact, including the gender-sensitive development impact.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP130)

Proposal name: Indonesia REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country(s): Indonesia

Project/programme size: Medium

## Impact potential

UNDP welcomes ITAP findings that the proposal has a High Impact potential.

UNDP agrees with the need to focus investments and ITAP acknowledgement of the 11 pilot provinces. It is important to highlight, however, that the use of proceeds will be considered by the government in coordination with other sources of funding (of which the vast majority originate from international donors).

Regarding strong enforcement of the forest moratorium, UNDP agrees on its importance and refer to the role of the Indonesian Cabinet Secretary Office that coordinates and tracks progress at the national ministry and agency with assigned responsibilities to implement this Presidential Instruction.

## Paradigm shift potential

UNDP is in agreement with ITAP assessment findings that the proposal has a High Paradigm Shift potential, thanks to the strong support to decentralized sustainable management of forests through the operationalization of Forest Management Units (FMUs) - as well social forestry (SF) - and the effective decentralization of the governance of the REDD+.

UNDP agrees and confirms that the government of Indonesia is committed, including through this proposal, to address regulatory bottlenecks and strengthen the Unit specifically responsible to oversee SF Program.

## Sustainable development potential

UNDP agrees with ITAP assessment findings that the proposal has a High-Sustainable development potential, thanks to the social forestry programme - and PMUs - in combination with further improvement and operationalization of the REDD+ architecture including tools to better monitor and act to reduce deforestation and forest degradation.


---
**Page 160**
---

UNDP fully agrees with ITAP assessment findings regarding the large needs of the recipient considering the sheer size of the country, which are further worsened by the current COVID-19 crisis. UNDP believes that the proposed use of REDD+ RBPs can contribute to providing relief to targeted vulnerable communities and support sustainable recovery.

### Country ownership

UNDP agrees with ITAP assessment that Indonesia has the right policies, strong institutional framework and socially inclusive processes in place to advance the National REDD+ Strategy in line with the purpose of the proposed project.

The Indonesian proposal was the first to apply for the guidance for FREL reference periods exceeding 20 years (Article 22 of the ToR). In doing so, maintaining full consistency with the UNFCCC assessed FREL without changing annualized estimations proved challenging. The assessment team notes that this is a normal phenomenon at this level of uncertainty and tends to grow over time. Indonesia is improving the accuracy of this data noting that this is an important aspect of its national circumstances. We welcome ITAP's recommendation for the revised reference to better reflect national circumstances.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

UNDP fully supports ITAP assessment findings that GGP BRP is particularly timely (particularly in the context of COVID-19), the budget balanced, and that Indonesia has the institutional capacity to put it into use as effective mechanisms for use of these resources, including the important role of the BPDPK (Environmental Fund Management Agency).

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

UNDP is in general agreement with ITAP assessment findings and recommendations.

UNDP noted that the ITAP concluded that Indonesia did not provide information on aggregate uncertainties and therefore assigned a score of zero in the case of both the FREL and results. Noting this, UNDP agrees with ITAP's recommendation to further develop the BOR technical annex. These covered selected sources of error, while the AT saw broadening the scope of the uncertainty analysis to cover further potential sources of errors as an area for technical development. The ITAP also recommended that Indonesia should take several steps to address the recommendations from the Technical AT; further improve its FREL and REDD+ RBPs processes, including the BOR annexes. UNDP believes that the pilot process from this RBP pilot-program will provide much welcome additional support to the effort.

UNDP reiterates that REDD+ RBPs projects are inherently very different from usual projects - proceeds are provided for results already achieved in the past (i.e., emission reductions expressed as carbon credits). The design of the project should properly reflect this fact.


---
**Page 161**
---

### Implementation of the Indonesia National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS), sustainable management of forests and improved decentralization forest governance.

The GCF will be paying for about 19% of the total volume of emission reductions achieved reducing deforestation in Indonesia in years 2014 to 2016. This means that the risk of reversals is significant, especially if there are no additional measures implemented with surplus emissions that are not being paid for by the GCF. The government of Indonesia has nevertheless invested in several policies and measures to address the risk of reversals; these were presented in the funding proposal.

1. **Introduction**

The Green Climate Fund recognizes the central importance of gender considerations in terms of both impact and access to climate funding, and requires a Gender Assessment and Action Plan to be submitted as part of the project proposal.

The purpose of this Annex is to screen the gender aspects of the GCF project, and to subsequently strengthen the general recommendations made by the Gender Analysis. The Gender Assessment will provide an overview of the gender dynamics in Indonesia, with a focus on REDD+ related thematic areas, specifically as they refer to the results period of 2015 to 2016 as well as the investment process of the project.

This gender assessment also identifies gender issues that are relevant to the project and examines potential gender mainstreaming opportunities. The assessment was based upon available data from studies commissioned by the Government of Indonesia, international organizations, and included:

- Undertaking a desktop review and aligning approaches in this proposal with the national priorities of Indonesia.
- Reviewing emerging national aspirations as detailed in the national policies and agenda on gender and women's empowerment.
- Incorporating lessons learned and insights from past studies and assessments on gender in Indonesia from the Government of Indonesia, the United Nations and international development organizations.
- Integrating gender considerations into the project indicators, targets, budget and activities, and identifying women as leaders and decision-makers.

2. **Existing Gender Dynamics and Inequalities in Indonesia**

According to Indonesia's 2010 Population Census results, there are 237,641,326 people in Indonesia, of whom 89.7% are women. In total, 52.6 million households (84 percent) are headed by men, while 8.5 million households (14 percent) are headed by women. This is an increase from 2005, in which the number of female-headed households was only 3.7 million.

Among both male and female household heads, there were almost evenly distributed between urban and rural areas. Among the female household heads, there was relatively more youth (around 0.7 million out of 8.5 million or around 8 percent) than among male household heads (around 1.2 million or 3.6 percent). In addition, when comparing the proportion of male and female-headed households, female-headed households were found to be slightly poorer than male-headed households within both the urban and rural areas. This assessment was based on the dwelling unit condition and home

**Page 164**
---

# Annex XIII (c)-Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

facilities, such as floor area and materials and having toilet facilities, telephone, access to internet and unprotected water sources.

Although a girl cannot legally marry before the age of 16 in Indonesia, many below that age were still recorded as married in the 2010 Population Census. Based on this data, the 2015 United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) Monograph on Child Marriage in Indonesia shows that a small percentage of women had been married before the age of fifteen. Moreover, about 43.2 percent of women have been married under the age of eighteen. Women in rural areas are more likely to be a child bride due to poverty, limited access to education and information, and certain religious views.

In addition, Indonesia's National Convention on Violence against Women (Kemnaker Perempuan) published its annual report (2016) on violence against women in 2015. This Annual Note is the yearly data compiled from the real cases reported directly from the victims to Kemnaker Perempuan, cases handled by community service institutions for women victims of violence and cases reported to state institutions. The total number of cases reported to Kemnaker Perempuan and its partner service providers in 2015 was 468,736 cases. The CATAMA reported 468,736 cases, which is an increase from 2018 and 2017, in which 348,466 and 293,150 cases were reported respectively.

Within this context, as will be illustrated below, Indonesian women and girls have nevertheless made some strides forward over the years. This has been achieved through increased economic growth as well as government efforts to promote gender inclusivity, as seen through its issuance of the Gender Mainstreaming decree in 2000 (discussed more in Section 3 below). However, despite these achievements, significant gaps remain between men and women on the Gender Inequality Index. These gender gaps, for example, continue to be seen in education, employment and wages and will most likely continue unless policies are put in place to address such gender inequalities and inequities.

### 2.1 Gender Inequality Index

Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) (Gender and Development Indicators) as a composite measure of gender gaps in reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market while the GDI measures achievement in human development in three areas: health, education, and command over economic resources. The GDI considers the gender gaps on human development between men and women.

Indonesia has a GII of 0.453, ranking it 104 out of 160 countries in the 2017 Index. The 2017 female Human Development Index (HDI) value for Indonesia is 0.666 in contrast with 0.715 for males, resulting in a GDI value of 0.392, which places the country into Group 3 (medium equality in HDI achievements between women and men).*

The Global Gender Gap index (GGGI) of the World Economic Forum examines the gap between men and women in four areas: education, health and survival, economic participation and opportunity, and political empowerment. The Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), a composite index that scores countries (i.e., 0 to 1 on 11) into five sub-indexes: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties to measure the discrimination against women in social institutions. Out of 165 countries, The 2018 SIGI value for Indonesia is 0.493, suggesting that discrimination against women is high.*

### 2.2 Education

Prior to 1950, women in Indonesia were given fewer opportunities than men to develop themselves, including in terms of education. During this time they were generally not allowed to go to school. After independence and due to Indonesia's various constitutions, legislations and policies, which guarantee the right of every Indonesian citizen to receive an education, women have been able to attend schools and receive an education. Since the 1970s, there have also been a series of policies implemented to remove various barriers to education. The outcome of these policies has been positive, with women also showing greater interest in attending school. Various government programmes over the years have included the Special Instruction (Inspiron) law on all construction of primary schools building and Wijaya (Compulsory Education) for 6 Years, 9 Years and 12 Years.

# Annex XIII (c) Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

According to data within Indonesia’s 2010 Population Census, 95.3 percent of children aged 7–12 years and 85 percent of children aged 13–15 actively went to school. However, senior-secondary school-aged children and university-aged youth rates were much lower: at 52.8 percent, and 15.1 percent, respectively. The gender gap in education is also evident; girls are more likely than boys to be enrolled in school. This data also revealed that both girls and boys suffer from inequality when it comes to geographical location of education facilities, where there is a marked disparity between urban and rural regions.

This suggests that the quality of education for children varies by region. For example, for the 13–15 age cohort, 87.4 percent of males were enrolled in school compared with only 69.6 percent of females. Similarly, for the 10–12 age cohort, 94.3 percent of males and 95.3 percent of females were enrolled in school. The opposite is true in urban areas where there was a small gender gap (87.4 percent of males versus 86.9 percent of females) for the 10–12 age cohort, but a larger gender gap (94.3 percent of males and 95.3 percent of females were enrolled in school).

This data also revealed that both girls and boys suffer from inequality when it comes to geographical location of education facilities, where there is a marked disparity between urban and rural regions.

### 2.3 Health

Per data from 2012, maternal and child health concepts require attention; whereas progress on maternal health has slowed in recent years, its maternal mortality ratio, estimated at around 228 per 100,000 live births, has remained above 200 over the past decade, despite efforts to improve maternal health services. The neonatal mortality rate is also high, with an estimated 35 deaths for every 1,000 live births in the neonatal period: 15 per thousand between 0 and 1 months and 10 per thousand between 2 and 4 years.

Due to improvements in mothers’ education, household and environmental hygiene, income and access to health services, Indonesia’s child mortality rate due to infections and other childhood illnesses has declined. Neonatal mortality remains the main hurdle in reducing further child deaths, where most of the causes of neonatal death are preventable. In both rural and urban areas and across all wealth quintiles, neonatal mortality is higher than infant mortality.

And while rural households still have an under-five mortality rate which is one-third higher than that in urban households, in some cases, rural mortality rates are falling faster than urban rates. This could be due to the fact that many rural women have access to better health services and living conditions amongst the poor urban, then paired with the loss of traditional social safety nets as well.

Indonesia continues to also suffer from issues with malnutrition, which in turn, has large impacts on women and children. For example, although data is limited and a bit dated, health surveys conducted in 2007–2013 show that while the prevalence of stunting among children under five years old dropped from 40.1 percent in 2003 to 24.5 percent in 2007, many mothers still do not take sufficient supplements. Additionally, infant and child feeding practices often remain inadequate. Only 43.9 percent of infants aged six months or younger received adequate complementary food; young children receive complementary food too early. For example, it was found that at 6–8 months old, only 12.9 percent are

receiving a form of solid or semi-solid food.

In addition, Indonesia is considered to have one of the fastest growing AIDS epidemics in Asia. While the total number of HIV infected individuals remains low, the rate of the increase is a concern. The new cases are concentrated in key populations, such as sex workers (who are mostly women) and their male clients. To make the situation worse, often female sex workers have limited access to HIV testing.

### 2.4 Political participation, decision-making and agency

Per the Constitution, women have the same rights as men to hold political and public office, and there are no formal barriers to women's political participation. Nevertheless, traditionally, the political arena has been dominated by men. In response to this low level of political participation and the need to increase women’s political participation, the Government of Indonesia passed Law No. 10/2008 which made it compulsory for parties to field at least one woman candidate out of every three top ranked candidates. The revised law also requires parties to place at least one woman among their top three ranked candidates.

There are, however, no specific sanctions for non-compliance.

In addition, Law No 17/2013 (Article 55) on General Elections requires that the list of nominees for members of the House of Representatives contain at least 30 percent of women's representatives. However, despite this law, the representation of women in Indonesia’s national and regional parliaments has not been reached in either the national or regional parliaments. Female participation in politics has nevertheless increased over the last decade, and with it a diversity of perspectives on policy issues. In 2013, there were 54 women members out of 567 seats (9.5 percent). In 2017, it was reported that there were nine women ministers (28.6 percent), two Vice-Presidents (one Vice-President and Director General [21 percent] and 2295 Women Senators or Head of Women’s parliaments) in political institutions. In addition, 76 women have also been elected as regents/mayors (14.78 percent).

There does, though, remain a preference for male candidates by many voters. For example, during election campaigns in the country, it is common for community and religious leaders and for male legislators to speak on behalf of female candidates. In addition, women are often expected to conform to these perspectives even more prominently, wherein there are various laws regulating women’s appearance and conduct. Laws discriminating against women, typically promoted in the name of religious and moral decrees, include dress codes, the public segregation of men and women, and rules

Furthermore, in the absence of public campaign funding, candidates need the support from the business community. Given the cultural stigma with women running for office, this funding is crucial to help women overcome these barriers and enter into political office in Indonesia, as men are generally better connected within such business circles.

While gender inequalities exist in many facets of Indonesian society, as discussed above, women's collective action within groups and civil society is helping to break down unequal social norms and structural barriers. Women's groups have been working with the government on various issues, including education, health, and economic empowerment. In addition, women are also working to help marginalized women by working with civil society organizations (CSOs) to empower them through training and capacity building.

The Government of Indonesia (GoI), in collaboration with the Australian Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade (DFAT) and the Government of Indonesia (BAPPENAS) partnered with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to support women's groups. Through this service delivery collaboration alone, this grassroots work has reached some 900 villages across 27 provinces. As of mid-2016, some 1,300 women’s groups with 32,000 members have directly or indirectly benefited from this work.

### 2.5 Labour force participation and earnings

While the Constitution notes that every citizen entitled to employment and a decent living, this entitlement is not always realized in practice. In Indonesia, women are more likely than men to be unemployed. Many women take on unpaid work at home, including managing the household and raising children, instead of paid work outside the home. It is commonly held that the reasons for why a woman does not enter into the labour force include lack of education, poor health, and low educational qualifications demanded of the job, or she has freely chosen to remain at home to fulfil her household obligations.

According to data from Indonesia’s 2010 Population Census, men of the working-age group were mostly employed (79.4 percent), whereas only less than half of the women (65.3 percent) of the same age were employed, with the other half engaged in household duties. To note, in comparison to data from 1990, more women are entering the labour force. In Indonesia, as in 1990, only 37.7 percent of working-age women were in the workforce.

Women’s involvement in the labor force is also more dependent on her age than it is for men. Working-aged men from three groups - youth, adult and elderly – were generally involved in income-earning activities. However, only women belonging to the adult subgroups were involved in income-earning activities. Women who were at youth and elder subgroup were instead engaged in household duties, with the percentage of adult females (37.8 percent) not in labour force shown to be more than that of males (16.6 percent).

# Annex XIII (c)-Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Green Climate Fund Proposal Document

Again, according to Indonesia’s 2010 Population Census, employed women and men both contributed as much as 46 percent in the agricultural sector. To note, employment in agriculture does not mean only working in farms but can also include, in the case of some women, of being involved in the decision-making about agriculture. The remaining 60 percent of men worked in the following five sectors: construction and transport (38.1 percent), manufacturing (24.5 percent), trade (9.7 percent), construction and transportation (8.1 percent); while the remaining 60 percent of women were employed in the following three sectors: trade (23.5 percent), services (19.0 percent) and agriculture (17.1 percent).

In Indonesia, employers are obliged to pay each employee at least the set minimum wage; however, this regulation has yet to be extended to cover those in the informal sector. When disaggregating wage by educational attainment and sex, it appears that the higher the education attainment the less wage difference, in relative terms, between men and women, other things held constant. In general, the average wage received by women with a bachelor’s degree was IDR 10,275,000 while the wage of women was IDR 1.4 million while that of men was IDR 1.7 million. The lowest wage was for workers who had no schooling; in this group the average wage received by women was inferior to their male counterparts by a factor of about four times (i.e., IDR 2,035,000 and IDR 8,496,000). In other words, women’s average wage was only 56.5 percent that of men. The wage received by women with a master’s degree was IDR 17,000,000 while the wage for men was at IDR 23,5 million and IDR 31.6 million, respectively. The one exception to this trend can be seen in the construction, transport and services/finance sectors, wherein women often received wages greater than male as they were relatively better educated than the men within them.

## 2.6 Access to Land and Property

Per various civil codes and laws, married and unmarried women on paper have the same rights as men to use, make decisions and use land and non-lands assets as collateral. The default marital property regime in Indonesia is joint ownership of property acquired during marriage. This joint administration of marital property despite this concept of joint ownership of property purchased during marriage, it is rarely the case that land is registered in the joint name of husband and wife. It is a common practice for the husband to register his name as owner of the land. There has been little awareness that title certificates for marital property can be issued in the name of both spouses. About 38.2% of married women aged 15 to 49 years own land individually or jointly as opposed to 54.6% of men.

In the case of divorce, the law stipulates that both spouses keep the properties they owned individually prior to the marriage and must divide equally any joint property. Per Islamic Law, under registered marriages, in the case of divorce, each wife to a polygamous marriage also has an equal right to property that was acquired during marriage. However, these rights are not exercised in


---
**Page 170**
---

# Annex XIII (c)-Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

practice wherein they are highly likely to lose their land rights upon widowhood, divorce, or desertion.[23]

In terms of forests, according to the Forestry Law No. 41 of 1999, nearly all the entire forestland is owned by the state. Less than two percent of the forest area is formally designated for communities and indigenous peoples or ownership of individuals. However, there are several regulations that allow local communities to engage in forest management activities. These include community forests, village forest, village timber plantations, partnership, and customary forest. The permits for such activities are issued by the Ministry of Forestry and Natural Resources Conservation to institutions that form local institutions, such as cooperatives and farmer forest groups. This arrangement has gender implications, as most of the members and leaders of such institutions are men. As such, women have limited involvement in decision-making processes, leading to their limited access to the benefits from the activities, such as access to knowledge and capacity-building activities as well as funds and payments.[24]

While there is no legal restriction on married women's rights to open a bank account at formal financial institution. Article 138 of the Civil Code creates a liability for the acquisition of assets, as it prevents the husband from disposing of or alienating property on which he has a usufruct right without any payment from the latter without consent of the husband. Although there have been recent amendments to this law, which requires the same consent of co-signing for the husbands, this law is not always enforced.

Furthermore, there is no law prohibiting discrimination by creditors on the basis of gender in access to credit. To note, in the economic sector, there are various credit schemes available for women, such as periksa (credit schemes specifically for women in micro and small enterprises). In the rural sector, the business development scheme is also available for women. However, this scheme is not widely known and does not often target women. In addition, many women entrepreneurs have limited knowledge in business development and face discrimination in dealing with government staff. Women also tend to register their business in their husband's name as the man are seen as the head of the household.[25]

## 3. Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Women and Promoting Gender Equality

### 3.1 Key International Protocols and Frameworks Ratified by Indonesia in Support of Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment and Human Rights

Indonesia has ratified and/or signed many key international conventions, treaties and plans of actions


---
**Page 171**
---

# Annex XIII (c)- Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

on gender equality, women empowerment and human rights. Critical among them are:[2]

- United Nations Convention on the Political Rights of Women, ratified by Law number 68/1958;
- United Nations Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), ratified by Law number 77/1984;
- Optional Protocol to the CEDAW, signed by the Government in 2003;
- International Labour Organization Convention number 100 on Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers for Work of Equal Value, ratified by Law number 80/1957;
- International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights, signed by the Government in 2006.
- International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights signed by the Government in 2006.

The country has also committed itself to acting upon the recommendations of the 1994 Copenhagen Declaration on Social Development, the 1995 Cairo International Conference on Population and Development and the 2005 Beijing Platform for Action.[3]

## 3.2 Key National Frameworks, Policies, Laws and Programs Supporting Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment

### 3.2.1 Policies, Laws and Frameworks

While gender gaps in national policies and laws are still present, Indonesia does continue to improve its legal frameworks at national and local levels, particularly in terms of intervening policies and programs for women and girls with disabilities (WGD), persons living with HIV/AIDS, children, persons with disabilities (PWDs), and older persons.[4] Listed below are key policies, laws and regulations (PLH) in Indonesia that ensure the protection of rights, freedom and welfare of men and women.

**1945 Constitution**

The 1945 Constitution of the Republic of Indonesia states: "every citizen enjoys equal status before the law and government and is obliged to uphold this status without exception"[Paragraph 1, Article 27]. Furthermore, it also stipulates that every person has a right to life, liberty, security and protection against conditions of life commensurate with human dignity.[5] The democratic reforms of 1999 caused the Government to assess and more intensively track human rights issues as well as refine the Constitution. In response, the Constitutional Commission issued a draft of the new Constitution in 2000 which recognizes that "every person shall have the right to be free from discriminatory treatment based upon any ground whatsoever and shall have the right to protection from such

**discriminatory treatment.**

Although it does not specifically distinguish the citizens into female and male citizens within it, the Constitution is one of the basic legal foundations for women's rights in Indonesia. While it does provide a sound foundation for promoting equal rights between men and women, as this study reveals, much remains to be done in ensuring these foundations are all fact followed and put into practice.

### 1984 Law on the Ratification of CEDAW

In line with the ratification of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and its implementation, Indonesia showed its support and approval in the participation of international efforts to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women. In the text of the law to ratify CEDAW, it is noted that in its implementation, the provisions of CEDAW are harmonized with the existing laws, including customary laws, religious norms, and values, which are widely followed by the Indonesian people.

### Presidential Instruction No. 5/2008 on Gender Mainstreaming in National Development

This instruction aims to enhance the position, role, and qualities of women to achieve gender equality in the family, society, and the nation. It instructs all government bodies to implement gender mainstreaming for planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting of national development plans and programs in accordance with their responsibilities, functions, and authorities. It also guides the State Minister of Women's Empowerment to give gender mainstreaming technical assistance to government agencies and institutions at both the national and subnational level as well as to report on progress of gender mainstreaming implementation to the President.

### Law No. 8/2013 on General Elections

Under this law, the general election of the members of the House of Representatives, People's Representative Council, and Regional House of Representatives political parties are required to have a minimum proportion of women candidates in their lists for parliamentary elections. In 2014, there were 97 women serving in the parliament (17.23 percent). Thus, to work towards achieving this target, three aspects were also introduced within the law. Firstly, the law encourages the appointment of more women as political party leaders and candidates. Secondly, it stipulates mandatory gender mainstreaming curricula in all compulsory trainings and education at all levels. Lastly, it calls for the establishment of a special gender mapping in politics to assess the representation needs of women, as voters and candidates, and help design effective information sharing and training programs on policies and leadership for women.

### National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN) of 2015-2019

One of the Plan's eight objectives is for its vision integrates gender considerations. It calls for equitable development in which discrimination is various aspects, including gender. It is added that, in addition, the Plan states five challenges to human resources development, of which have direct linkages to


---
**Page 173**
---

gender-related issues. For example, it notes that there is a challenge on accelerating the promotion of gender equality. However, it also highlights how women’s role in development is helping to address this issue by increasing the understanding, commitment, and ability of decision makers and development practitioners to mainstream gender into all aspects of development, including economic development, strengthening gender mainstreaming institutions including gender responsive planning and financing in central and regional levels. It also notes that the efforts to increase the protection of women against violence and other wrongdoings is changing society’s patriarchal attitude and other cultural practices.

Furthermore, the Plan includes gender equality and women's empowerment as part of its human and social development targets, which in two indicators on gender have been established. It also promotes women's participation in decision-making. The key components of its national developmental agenda are increasing the role and representation of women in politics and development and protecting women, children, and marginalized groups.

### 3.2.2 Ministerial Policies and Programs on Gender

The Ministry of Women Empowerment and Child Protection (MONEMP) launched a flagship program called Bimasakti which focuses on four issues on end: (i) violence against women and children; (ii) human trafficking; and (iii) barriers to economic justice for women. The strategy to end violence against women and children includes: (i) ensuring the accessibility of information on the rights of women and children in all public places, including at the district level; (ii) enhancing the capacity of local integrated task force on women and children; and (iv) mobilizing community participation through awareness campaigns.

The strategy to end trafficking of women and girls includes: (i) developing early detection system on trafficking in persons (TIP); (ii) raising the awareness of the community, including men and boys, and encouraging them to report cases of TIP; (iii) strengthening the capacity of law enforcement agencies through synergies within the National Task Force for Trafficking in accordance with the TIP Act; and (iv) ensuring full and mandatory training for all prospective migrant workers. Lastly, the strategy to end barriers to access economic justice includes: (i) providing financial services to women entrepreneurs; (ii) expanding capacity building for women entrepreneurs; (iii) expanding access and ensuring rights of all women to economic resources, including business financing such as loan and credit from financial institutions; (iv) promoting women's entrepreneurship through the establishment of women's cooperatives and enterprises; and (v) building funding support and alternative facilities for women innovators in economic sectors.

Particularly relevant is the context of gender and REDD+ in Indonesia is the "Regulation of the Minister of Environment and Forestry Number P.316/Menhut/SET Anp/KL.05/2017 on Guidelines on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forestry Sector" issued by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MAEF) in May 2017. The regulation recognizes that acceleration is required in regard to the promotion, effectiveness and optimization gender mainstreaming in MAE policies, programs, and activities in the environment and forestry sector and emphasizes

### Annex XIII (c)- Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

#### Implementation in the field on the Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming The guidelines themselves issued as a Presidential Instruction in 2000 was a strategy aimed at closing the gendered disparity in access, participation, control and benefits of development between women and men. The annex to the regulation that is now being proposed will not only mainstream gender equality but also increase the social inclusion of other marginalized groups and resolve the disparity of access, participation, control and benefits of development of children, the elderly, persons with disabilities, communities and other groups. An MoU was also signed between the MCFD and the MoWECP in 2016.

In addition to ensure proper implementation of Presidential Instruction No.9/2000 on Gender Mainstreaming, the Ministry of Finance (MoF), together with MoWECP, Ministry of Home Affairs, and the National Commission for Violence Against Women (NCVW) have established four gender-responsive planning and budgeting within all ministerial/governmental units at the national level (SE.13/MA.02/2012). These four ministries are now also in the process of preparing a draft Presidential Regulation to ensure the institutionalization of gender mainstreaming. This regulation will be legally enforced and will replace the current Presidential Instruction, which only serves as a policy instrument.

#### 3.3 Institutional Arrangements

The Government of Indonesia established the State's Ministry for Women's Empowerment in 1978, which was later changed into MoWECP, as the national machinery to ensure the implementation of gender equality and women's empowerment. In line with CEDAW and per Presidential Instruction No.9/2000 noted above, MoWECP is the lead government agency with a mandate for mainstreaming gender in all development plans and programs at the central level and also coordinates with other ministries to ensure that gender is maintained in their development plans and that this progress is then reported regularly to the President.

The Government has too established the National Commission on Violence Against Women in 1998. The Commission's mandate is to coordinate, monitor, evaluate, and reporting efforts to eliminate all forms of violence against women and to protect women's rights in Indonesia.

Indonesia has also established units or focal points on women and children under relevant Ministries/Agencies. These units have been established within the structure of the Coordinating Ministry of Human Development and Culture, MoWECP, Ministry of Social Affairs, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Home Affairs, Ministry of Manpower and the National Police.

In terms of the forestry sector, the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MAEF) incorporates gender

## 4. Gender Issues Around Forests and REDD++

Forest resources are a major source of income for the poor in the country, being the source of the country's economic development but also contribute to rural poor communities' livelihood needs. Women of all rural households are engaged in meeting their families' subsistence and livelihood needs, and given that 40 percent of households depend on forest products as their main source of income, the availability of these resources remains necessary for poor rural communities to fulfill their subsistence needs. Rural communities that are highly dependent on forests, are most directly and significantly impact by forest degradation and deforestation. Women in particular are more vulnerable than men because they often have a higher degree of dependence on the environment in order to manage daily household tasks. As noted in the Gold Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forests, women's access to and control over natural resources is critical to their livelihoods, which they have to find water at a greater distance or purchase it at a high price, in order to cook for their families.

In Indonesia, women play pivotal roles in forest use and management. While there are variations across the country, in general, women engage in numerous activities around forests, such as collecting firewood, harvesting non-timber forest products (NTFPs) (e.g., honey, medicinal plants, food animal fodder), and forest protection, amongst others. In some instances, women also manage home gardens and nearby forest and fallow land. In this, they allow them to combine child rearing and domestic work.

## 5. Gender Integration within forest conservation efforts and REDD+ action in Indonesia

### S.1 Design Overview

From 2010-2013, the Government of Indonesia received support from the UN-REDD Programme to help it attain REDD-readiness. Under the overall objective of this support, 3 outcomes were expected: Outcome 1 was linked to the policy in the national context; Outcome 2 was related to the activities conducted at the pilot province (Central Sulawesi); while Outcome 3 dealt with empowerment and capacity building.

Gender elements were for the most inadvertently left out in the design consultations held for the Programme. To address this oversight, during implementation of the Programme various efforts to


---
**Page 177**
---

# Annex XIII (c)-Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Proposal

### Integrating gender in project management

Several Project Management Unit (PMU) staff participated in a two-day gender-mainstreaming workshop held by UNDP Indonesia to enhance their understanding on gender issues, develop gender-specific indicators and design relevant activities to be implemented. The PMU staff also attended the same workshop as part of the capacity building for gender mainstreaming. Interviews with PMU representatives revealed that mainstreaming gender into programme implementation was not an easy task.

### Drafting the National REDD+ Strategy (STRAMAS)

In addition, during the drafting of the National REDD+ Strategy (STRAMAS), efforts were taken to engage the public and civil society organizations and provide equal access to knowledge and information on REDD+. A workshop on "Gender Safeguards in REDD+ Initiative" was held in Jakarta in February 2012 to provide inputs and guidance on how to integrate gender into the safeguards. The STRAMAS was developed by the REDD+ Taskforce in 2012, with a series of workshops held in Central Sulawesi to gain inputs from local communities. The Programme also implemented Free, Prior, Informed and Consent (FPIC) activities in several pilot sites in Central Sulawesi which considered gender issues as part of their social safeguards.

### Evaluation of the STRAMAS

The evaluation of the programme concluded that it followed FPIC principles, wherein nearly all conceptual preparation was done with the participation of women. The consultations were held at group discussion with a myriad of stakeholders from the national and provincial level. However, it did note that the participation rates of women were less than ideal. In the workshops, consultations, focus group discussions, and other activities, the majority of participants were men. Although some women participants were skewed more toward men. In line with the gender imbalances and inequalities faced in the country, men still outnumbered women within the Programme's personnel team as well.

### S.2 REDD+ National Strategy (STRAMAS)

Developed by the REDD+ Taskforce in 2012, the REDD+ National Strategy (STRAMAS) was formulated with the following intent:

- To develop an effective institutional system to implement the REDD+ program.
- To provide a basis and direction for integrated governance and regulatory systems to ensure the implementation of the REDD+ scheme.
- Develop a comprehensive policy process and approach to save Indonesia's natural forests and the flora and fauna within them.
- Provide a reference for the expansion of investment in the utilization of forests and peatlands for their conservation, sustainable use by local communities, and the provision of ecosystem services that include the conservation and accumulation of carbon stocks.


---
**Page 178**
---

The drafting process of the National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS) included a series of public consultation meetings in seven regions: Jawa Barat/Denpasar, Sulawesi, Sumatera II, Riau, Mukah and Nusa Tenggara, Papua, engaging 387 participants with representatives of government (46 percent), civil society (42 percent), academics (nine percent) and private sector (three percent). The draft was also posted on a website to get feedback from other audiences. The National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS) consultation process was resumed further during a National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS) consultation held by members of the REDD+ Task Force with representatives of the MoE/WEF, there was an emphasis on gender equality and women’s rights in forest management, including REDD+, but not yet to consider REDD as an important issue that MoE/WEF should be engaged in.12

Despite this, the National REDD+ Strategy (STRANAS) does incorporate gender considerations into various aspects, including in its principles. These references are noted below:44

- The principle on fairness of REDD+ implementation notes “REDD+ is implemented on the basis of the principles of equality for all and human rights protection in forest management, including for women and communities vulnerable to socio-economic and environmental change” (p 28).
- One principle to be adopted to change the culture and paradigm in the country is “gender sensitivity” which calls for the attention to equity rules, needs, and responsibilities of men and women (p.28).
- Efforts to strengthen forest and resources governance calls for accurate information to be available on forest resources and their uses, including for REDD+ implementation, especially women and other vulnerable groups, to improve: (i) understanding of available information; and (ii) participation in decision-making processes (p.23);
- The national gender equality strategy for the forestry sector, which includes gender topics includes assurance of gender equality and the right of vulnerable groups to participate equally in REDD+ implementation (p 32).
- Requirements under the safeguards framework is designed in such a manner to protect and benefit vulnerable groups, including: (1) indigenous peoples and local communities living in and around forests, whose livelihoods depend on forest resources; (2) women, who face the full brunt of deforestation and forest degradation; and (3) people with disabilities. These social and political status put them in a weak position in terms of fulfillment of their human rights (p 29).
- The guidance noted on the social safeguards framework calls for the inclusion of indicators that evaluate the “accountability of verification results relating to the distribution of benefits from the implementation of REDD+ activities among groups of men and women” (p.31).

Anxix XIII (c)-Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

Finally, efforts by other non-government actors has also provided ongoing contributions to improve gender equality in Indonesia's forest governance more broadly, which are directly relevant to REDD+ activities. One such example is the Asia Foundation’s environmental justice program in Indonesia, known as “Hearing Women’s Voices in Managing Natural Resources” (2018). This project works with women whose livelihoods depend on traditional forest resources.

The second phase of the program (starting in 2015) facilitated a dialogue with the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) in 2016, that resulted in three key recommendations: 1) to include gender considerations in Environmental Impact Assessments; 2) to allow women to be recognized as heads of household under the Social Forestery program; 3) to strengthen women’s role in traditional forest management.

### 5.3 REDD+ Safeguards in Indonesia: IS-SREDD

Indonesia has developed several safeguards related to REDD+, including PRISAI (Principles of Criteria for Indonesian Sustainable Acceleration), Strategic Environment and Social Assessment (SESA) and Social Environmental Framework for REDD+. In 2016, MoEF established the Safeguards Information System for REDD+ (SIS-REDD+) as Indonesia’s official REDD+ safeguards system, with the main principles based on the Cancun Agreement.

The SIS-REDD+ contains: 7 principles, 17 criteria and 32 indicators, that are adapted from the implementation of the Joint Implementation Safeguards Information System (JISI), Assessment (JAMAL), Strategic Environmental Assessment (KLS), Timber Legality Assurance System for Sustainable Forest Management (SFSM), Social Forestry Monitoring System (SMF), Carbon Market Certification (SMC), High Conservation Value (HCV), Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC), and Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA). The SIS-REDD+ web-platform consists of two parts:

1. Database to collect, compile and manage data and information on REDD+ safeguards implementation.
2. Web platform to display the information on safeguards implementation.

Collection of data, management and provision of information on the implementation of safeguards is conducted by actors at site level through self-assessment process by completing a Safeguards Implementation Assessment (SIA) tool. The aim for this SIS-REDD+ is to provide a simple, transparent, accountable, comprehensive and comparable system. The aim is for this information on safeguards from the site level to be progressively centralized to the SI management in districts or provinces. It should be noted that the SIS-REDD+ does not contain any reference to gender, either in its principles, criteria or indicators, or the APPS tool. As such, future application of the SIS-REDD+, the principles, criteria and indicators, the APPS tool, and in general, the REDD+ safeguards system should be adapted to reflect the fact that women often use natural resources and are impacted by changes in natural resource uses in manners that are different than men.

# Recommendations

This preliminary gender analysis acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout design and implementation of the proposed project. The results and findings of the gender analysis has also informed and guided the development of a Gender Action Plan (please see Section 7 below).

This gender analysis, conducted through desk review, resulted in the following actions:

- Identification of broader national level gender inequalities and challenges and risks faced by women and other marginalized groups in Indonesia around thematic areas of relevance to REDD+ (e.g., land tenure, value chains, violence against women), decision-making processes, agricultural production, forest use, land tenure, education, etc.)
- Identification of gaps, entry points and opportunities for mainstreaming gender in the proposed project, including implementation of existing REDD+ action and governance of natural resources in Indonesia more broadly.
- Development of the gender approach used within this proposal effectively programmatic and sectoral development considerations within a socio-economic, political and local context within the country
- Demonstration of the need to ensure that gender responsive environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA), in which the need for additional gender-sensitive and sex-disaggregated baseline data (e.g. on land tenure, value chains, violence against women, etc.) is also assessed (and quantified) as part of the ESIA.
- Demonstration of the need to ensure the stakeholder consultations and engagement efforts for the ESIA, and for FPCD activities are designed and undertaken using a gender approach and equally involving representatives from more marginalized groups, including women, youth, single-family households.
- Establishment of recommendations to incorporate into the preliminary Gender Action Plan.

More specifically, based on the above analysis and document review, the key entry points and recommendations for gender action within programmatic design and implementation are listed below. It should be noted that these recommendations are based upon preliminary findings of this assessment (based solely on a desk review). Thus, the findings and recommendations of this gender assessment, including those related to data gaps and baseline information, as well as the recommendations listed below, will need to be re-assessed and revised as necessary during the ESIA process, as well will need to be consulted with and validated by state and non-state stakeholders, including those more marginalized groups (e.g., women, youth, single-family households). The MoF is currently developing a strategic plan to mainstream gender for the period 2020 to 2025, which, if available, will be taken into consideration during the revision of the Gender Action Plan.

- It is essential that field-based data-collection and consultations take place in order to conduct a contextualized gender analysis at the local level, in order to capture huge variety of social

# Annex XIII (c)- Preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

### Introduction

The gender dimensions and gaps identified within this assessment and implementing the corresponding recommendations noted above and gender-responsive actions noted below in the Gender Action Plan will be taken into account during design and implementation with the aim to ensure that the project provides gender-responsive results.

### Objectives

- To support the effective implementation of the GAP, a gender consultant/NGO should be hired to work with and support/backstop the gender focal points/working groups of the MoF and MoEF.
  - Where there is internal support on gender issues within the Ministry, it is important to ensure these teams have adequate knowledge on REDD+ and the role of women in relation to it. As such, capacity of these teams should be built accordingly to support effective mainstreaming of gender perspectives into project implementation as well as to encourage sustainability of project outcomes once the project is finished.
- Furthermore, given that Indonesia has a decentralized system of governance, it is also important to ensure that there are mechanisms in place at the local level so that gender equitable policies from the national level are implemented at the local level.

### Stakeholder Involvement

- Direct involvement of stakeholders, particularly women, in decision-making processes in regard to use of proceeds must lead to adequate support for social equity objectives to household needs.
  - It should be noted that the role played by women on decentralized levels does not always translate into their influence on decisions and thus setting up a mechanism for their participation should be accompanied by efforts in regard to norm change, as well as establishing mechanisms to ensure that they have greater voice and agency.

### Data Collection

- The collection of gender-disaggregated data in the implementation of REDD+ actions and the use of proceeds should be a mandatory contribution to available national and local-level databases on gender equality and women's empowerment.
  - Gender concerns need to be adequately mainstreamed in order to ensure that gender perspectives and the goal of gender equity are central to all REDD+-related activities.

### Capacity Building

- A high level of capacity building, including training and adequate financing and resourcing of gender support units at multiple levels is required.

7. Gender Action Plan

Data presented above provides context and an overall baseline assessment on the gender dynamics, inequalities and state-of-play within Indonesia, its forest sector and in its efforts on REDD+ action to date. This information highlights differences between men and women and has helped to identify gaps and provide a baseline for comparison.

Addressing the gender gaps noted above, this Gender Action Plan provides suggested entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under the applicable activities of the proposed project. It will ensure: first, conducting gender analysis to inform gender responsive project planning and implementation (e.g., through participatory gender assessments, gender budgeting, capacity building activities, revolving fund, among others); third, provision of equal access to decision making processes at all levels; fourth, increased women's access to productive assets; and finally, systematic collection of gender-disaggregated data. The Gender Action Plan also includes a number of mitigation measures to avoid potential project risks such as women’s losing access to forest resources and unequal benefit sharing mechanisms. Baseline indicators are also proposed to measure and track progress towards the actions at the activity level. This can be integrated into the baseline plan which will be developed at the start of implementation process and provides concrete recommendations on how to collect gender (including disaggregated data) continues to be collected and measured throughout implementation.

The overall budget for the gender action plan is tentatively budgeted for 500,000 USD. As the overall project budget is not yet known and is subject to change, pending feedback from the project review and approval process, this gender budget has not yet been broken down by activity level. It is planned to assign and allocate these costs once the final project budget is approved.

To note, as baseline data on women in the field may be under-informed or in the forest sector and around REDD+ issues within the country, detailed gender baseline data, as they pertain to the project activities, in many cases are not yet known. In these instances, baseline information for the activity level will need to be collected during implementation of the project. Baseline information is not yet available. Such information will be collected during the process of developing the gender-responsive ESIA, wherein the generation of gender-responsive baseline data for the proposed programmed will be undertaken. Thereafter, the Gender Action Plan below will be revised as necessary and gender baseline information will be provided.

## 2.1 Support the operationalization of Forest Management Units (FMUs)

### Gender-responsive capacity building and technical assistance to Provincial & District authorities, FMUs and local communities

#### Baseline: I
Indicator: Evidence that training and capacity building delivered to Provincial & District authorities, FMUs and local communities accounts for at least 25% women's groups and are gender-sensitive.
Target: At least 10 participants involved in workshops for this activity who felt training delivery allowed them to actively participate and found the content being discussed relevant and useful.
Mean of verification: Workshop reports

#### Baseline: II
Indicator: At least a third of stakeholders involved in the participatory multi-stakeholder engagement processes who represent women's groups.
Target: Women's groups represent at least 25% of participants and are actively engaged throughout the process.
Mean of verification: Reports from multi-stakeholder platform meetings with participation

### Support small-scale community investments in gender-responsive manner

#### Baseline: I
Indicator: At least a third of small-scale community investments that take into account preferences identified through local-level gender analysis and consultations.
Target: 50% of small-scale community investments accounts for preferences identified by women in community consultations, including prioritization of projects.
Mean of verification: Records of community consultations. Financial reports on small-scale community investments
